  // enact the browser preference if "automatic", 
  // without expecting DOM load to have completed.
  //
  // Also restore any saved readingmode and ToC display preferences.
  function initializeReadingPreferences() {
    let saved_theme = localStorage.getItem("ar5iv_theme") || "automatic";
    if (saved_theme === "automatic") {
      if (window.matchMedia("(prefers-color-scheme: dark)").matches) {
        saved_theme = "dark";
      }
    }
    if (saved_theme == "dark") {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "dark");
    } else {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "light");
    }

    const tocDisplay = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_toc_display');
    if (tocDisplay) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-toc-display", tocDisplay);
    }
    const readingMode = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_reading_mode');
    if (readingMode) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-reading-mode", readingMode);
    }
    // Pre-apply spinout-banner dismissal here, before the banner paints, so it
    // never flashes in only to be hidden later by the deferred arxiv-header.js.
    // Key matches arxiv-header.js: "arxiv-banner-dismissed:" + data-banner-name.
    if (localStorage.getItem('arxiv-banner-dismissed:spinout-nonprofit')) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-banner-dismissed", "");
    }
  }
  // Run as soon as JS starts, to minimize repainting
  initializeReadingPreferences();

 Verifiable Quantum Advantage via Optimized DQI Circuits 
 
 
 Tanuj Khattar

 Noah Shutty
 
 Corresponding author:  shutty@google.com 
 
 Affiliation:  Google Quantum AI, Venice, CA 90291

 October 09, 2025 

 Abstract 
    
 Decoded Quantum Interferometry (DQI) provides a framework for superpolynomial quantum speedups by reducing certain optimization problems to reversible decoding tasks. We apply DQI to the Optimal Polynomial Intersection (OPI) problem, whose dual code is Reed-Solomon (RS).
We establish that DQI for OPI is the first known candidate for verifiable quantum advantage with optimal asymptotic speedup: solving instances with classical hardness  O ⁡ ( 2 N ) O(2^{N})  requires only  O ~ ​ ( N ) \widetilde{O}(N)  quantum gates, matching the theoretical lower bound. Realizing this speedup requires highly efficient reversible RS decoders.
We introduce novel quantum circuits for the Extended Euclidean Algorithm, the decoder’s bottleneck. Our techniques, including a new representation for implicit Bézout coefficient access, and optimized in-place architectures, reduce the leading-order space complexity to the theoretical minimum of  2 ​ n ​ b 2nb  qubits while significantly lowering gate counts.
These improvements are broadly applicable, including to Shor’s algorithm for the discrete logarithm.
We analyze OPI over binary extension fields  G ​ F ​ ( 2 b ) GF(2^{b}) , assess hardness against new classical attacks, and identify resilient instances.
Our resource estimates show that classically intractable OPI instances (requiring  > 10 23 >10^{23}  classical trials) can be solved with approximately 5.72 million Toffoli gates.
This is substantially less than the count required for breaking RSA-2048, positioning DQI as a compelling candidate for practical, verifiable quantum advantage. 
  
 
 
 Data availability:  Code and assets created for this paper are
 available on Zenodo   [ 1 ] .

 I  Introduction 

 
 The pursuit of verifiable quantum advantage is a central goal in quantum computing.
 Quantum advantage  refers to solving problems efficiently on a quantum computer where no efficient classical algorithm is known.  Verifiability  implies we can efficiently check the solution on a classical computer.
Verifiable quantum advantage problems are a useful model of future applications of quantum computers to classical search and optimization problems abundant in industry. 
 
 
 A fundamental question in this pursuit concerns the efficiency of the quantum speedup itself. Given a problem instance with a target classical hardness of  O ⁡ ( 2 N ) O(2^{N})  (where  N N  is the security parameter), what is the required quantum runtime?
The theoretical lower bound is  Ω ⁡ ( N ) \Omega(N)  quantum gates  [ 2 ] . However, existing candidates for verifiable superpolynomial advantage exceed this bound. For example, achieving  O ⁡ ( 2 N ) O(2^{N})  hardness in integer factorization requires Shor’s algorithm  [ 3 ]  to use  O ~ ​ ( N 6 ) \widetilde{O}(N^{6})  gates (against the General Number Field Sieve), while Elliptic Curve Cryptography (ECC) requires  O ~ ​ ( N 2 ) \widetilde{O}(N^{2})  gates (against Pollard Rho). 
 
 
 One corollary of the resource estimation performed in this work is to demonstrate that Decoded Quantum Interferometry (DQI)  [ 4 ] , when applied to the Optimal Polynomial Intersection (OPI) problem, is the first known candidate for verifiable quantum advantage that achieves the optimal asymptotic speedup (up to polylogarithmic factors), requiring only  O ~ ​ ( N ) \widetilde{O}(N)  quantum gates to solve instances with  O ⁡ ( 2 N ) O(2^{N})  classical hardness: 
 
 
 Theorem I.1 . 
 
 There is an NP-search / optimization problem where the runtime of the best-known classical algorithm for the problem is  2 N 2^{N}  and which can be solved with a circuit of  O ~ ​ ( N ) \widetilde{O}(N)  quantum gates. 
 
 
 
 We prove this theorem in  Section   IV . We compare this against prior speedups based on Shor’s algorithm in  Table   1 .

 To understand how DQI achieves this speedup, we must formalize the class of problems it addresses. DQI provides a framework for approximating solutions to constraint satisfaction problems, specifically max-LINSAT, where its efficiency is tied to the efficiency of reversibly decoding a related error-correcting code. 
 
 
 Definition I.2   (max-LINSAT  [ 4 ] ) . 
 
 Let  𝔽 q \mathbb{F}_{q}  be a finite field. Given an  m × n m\times n  matrix  B B  over  𝔽 q \mathbb{F}_{q}  (with  m > n m>n ), and for each constraint  i = 1 , 2 , … , m i=1,2,\dots,m , a subset  F i ⊂ 𝔽 q F_{i}\subset\mathbb{F}_{q} . The max-LINSAT problem is to find an assignment  x ∈ 𝔽 q n x\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}  that maximizes the number of satisfied constraints, where the  i i -th constraint is satisfied if  ∑ j = 1 n B i ​ j ​ x j ∈ F i \sum_{j=1}^{n}B_{ij}x_{j}\in F_{i} . 
 
 
 
 The DQI algorithm addresses max-LINSAT by defining a related objective function,  f ⁡ ( x ) f(x) , as the number of satisfied constraints minus the number of unsatisfied constraints. This can be expressed as: 
 

 
 
 f ⁡ ( x ) = ∑ i = 1 m f i ​ ( ∑ j = 1 n B i ​ j ​ x j ) , where f i ​ ( y ) = { + 1 if  ​ y ∈ F i − 1 if  ​ y ∉ F i . f(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}f_{i}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}B_{ij}x_{j}\right),\quad\text{where}\quad f_{i}(y)=\begin{cases}+1&\text{if }y\in F_{i}\\
-1&\text{if }y\notin F_{i}\end{cases}. 
 
 (1) 
 
 
 
 Maximizing  f ⁡ ( x ) f(x)  is equivalent to maximizing the number of satisfied constraints. The DQI algorithm works by preparing a quantum state  | P ⁡ ( f ) ⟩ = ∑ x P ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( x ) ) ​ | x ⟩ |P(f)\rangle=\sum_{x}P(f(x))|x\rangle , where  P P  is a polynomial of degree  ℓ \ell  designed to enhance the amplitudes of states  | x ⟩ \ket{x}  where  f ⁡ ( x ) f(x)  is large. The core insight of DQI is that the preparation of this state can be reduced to a decoding problem on the dual code  C ⟂ = { d ∈ 𝔽 q m : B T ​ d = 0 } C^{\perp}=\{d\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{m}:B^{T}d=0\} . The algorithm involves creating a superposition of errors  e e  and their syndromes  B T ​ e B^{T}e . To achieve the necessary interference that amplifies good solutions, the error register  | e ⟩ |e\rangle  must be coherently uncomputed, which necessitates a reversible quantum implementation of a decoder for  C ⟂ C^{\perp} .
The performance of DQI is directly tied to the error-correction capability of this decoder. The degree  ℓ \ell  of the enhancing polynomial  P P  corresponds to the maximum number of errors the decoder must correct. A key result of the DQI framework  [ 4 ]  is the semicircle law, which provides a rigorous performance guarantee based on the decoding capability. 
 
 
 Theorem I.3   (DQI Semicircle Law (Informal)  [ 4 ] ) . 
 
 Given a max-LINSAT instance where the allowed sets  F i F_{i}  have size  r r  over a field of size  q q . If the dual code  C ⟂ C^{\perp}  can be efficiently decoded up to  ℓ \ell  errors, DQI can sample solutions that satisfy an expected fraction of constraints  ⟨ s ⟩ / m \langle s\rangle/m  approaching: 
 

 
 
 ( ℓ m ​ ( 1 − r q ) + ( 1 − ℓ m ) ​ r q ) 2 . \left(\sqrt{\frac{\ell}{m}\left(1-\frac{r}{q}\right)}+\sqrt{\left(1-\frac{\ell}{m}\right)\frac{r}{q}}\right)^{2}. 
 
 (2) 
 
 
 
 
 This theorem formalizes the intuition that a better decoder (a larger correctable error fraction  ℓ / m \ell/m ) leads to a better optimization result. Consequently, the efficiency of the reversible decoder dominates the resource requirements of the entire DQI algorithm. 
 
 
 In this work, we focus on constructing efficient quantum circuits for DQI applied to the Optimal Polynomial Intersection (OPI) problem, identified in  [ 4 ]  as a candidate for superpolynomial quantum speedup. 
 
 
 Definition I.4   (Optimal Polynomial Intersection (OPI)) . 
 
 Let  𝔽 q \mathbb{F}_{q}  be a finite field and  n < q − 1 n<q-1 . Given  m = q − 1 m=q-1  subsets  F y ⊂ 𝔽 q F_{y}\subset\mathbb{F}_{q}  for each  y ∈ 𝔽 q ∗ y\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{*} , find a polynomial  Q ∈ 𝔽 q ​ [ y ] Q\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[y]  of degree at most  n − 1 n-1  that maximizes the objective function:  f OPI ​ ( Q ) = | { y ∈ 𝔽 q ∗ : Q ⁡ ( y ) ∈ F y } | f_{\text{OPI}}(Q)=|\{y\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{*}:Q(y)\in F_{y}\}| . 
 
 
 
 When OPI is cast as max-LINSAT, the constraint matrix  B B  is a Vandermonde matrix, implying the dual code  C ⟂ C^{\perp}  is a Reed-Solomon (RS) code. For OPI, DQI can achieve approximation ratios that appear beyond the reach of known polynomial-time classical algorithms  [ 4 ] . RS codes possess efficient classical decoders, such as the Berlekamp-Massey decoder  [ 6 ]  or the Extended Euclidean Algorithm (EEA)  [ 7 ] . Our primary contribution is the development of highly optimized, reversible quantum circuits for syndrome decoding of Reed-Solomon codes using Extended Euclidean Algorithm-based decoders. We introduce several key strategies to minimize the quantum resources required: 
 
 
 
 1.  
 
 Analysis of OPI over Binary Extension Fields:  We shift the analysis from prime fields to binary extension fields,  GF ​ ( 2 b ) \text{GF}(2^{b}) .
This choice significantly reduces the cost of the underlying quantum arithmetic (leveraging techniques like Karatsuba multiplication  [ 8 ,  9 ]  and Itoh-Tsujii inversion  [ 10 ,  9 ] ).
Crucially, we analyze the classical hardness in this setting, confirming that the problem remains intractable against known classical attacks like Prange’s algorithm  [ 11 ]  and a novel variant, the Extended Prange’s algorithm (XP), tailored for extension fields. 
 
 2.  
 
 Optimized Quantum Circuits for the Extended Euclidean Algorithm:  We introduce two distinct compilation strategies for the EEA, each achieving minimal qubit overhead while being tailored for different algorithmic requirements.
These strategies are general and shall offer substantial improvements for other quantum algorithms utilizing the EEA, such as those in elliptic curve cryptography  [ 12 ,  13 ,  14 ]  and DQI with EEA-based decoders for other codes like algebraic geometry codes  [ 15 ]  and RS codes with prime fields  [ 4 ] . 
 
 
 
 •  
 
 For scenarios requiring  explicit  access to the Bézout coefficients, we present an improved synchronized circuit for the classic Euclidean algorithm  [ 16 ,  12 ] . By storing quotients in-place within shared registers and merging arithmetic cycles, we deterministically achieve a leading-order space complexity of  2 ​ n ​ b 2nb  qubits and substantially reduce gate counts. 
 
 •  
 
 For scenarios where  implicit  access is sufficient, we introduce the novel  Dialog  representation. This data structure records the execution trace of a constant-time EEA, allowing our circuits to capitalize on the low gate costs of modern, division-free EEA algorithms  [ 17 ]  without incurring their typically large qubit overhead  [ 14 ,  18 ] . 
 
 
 
 3.  
 
 Holistic Reversible Reed-Solomon Decoder Design:  We construct end-to-end reversible quantum circuits for the full RS decoder that integrate our optimized EEA modules. Our design is compatible with both the explicit and implicit EEA approaches and fully accounts for the resource costs of the subsequent decoding stages—Chien Search and Forney’s algorithm—when operating on the compact, shared-register data structures produced by our EEA implementations. 
 
 
 
 
 We synthesize these techniques to construct end-to-end quantum circuits and provide detailed resource estimates using Qualtran  [ 19 ] .
Our results demonstrate that classically intractable instances of OPI (requiring  > 10 23 >10^{23}  classical trials) can be solved with modest quantum resources.
For instance, an  ( m = 4095 , n = 70 , b = 12 ) (m=4095,n=70,b=12)  instance requires approximately  5.72 × 10 6 5.72\times 10^{6}  Toffoli gates and  1885 1885  logical qubits.
This is roughly  1000 ​ 𝐱 1000\mathbf{x}  fewer Toffolis than that required for factoring 2048-bit RSA integers  [ 20 ] , suggesting that DQI may offer a compelling near-term path to practical, verifiable quantum advantage in optimization.
We also provide a physical resource estimate showing that the  ( m = 4095 , n = 70 , b = 12 ) (m=4095,n=70,b=12)  OPI instance can be solved using eight hundred thousand physical qubits and  1 1  hour of runtime under standard assumptions for superconducting architectures: a square grid of qubits with nearest
neighbor connections, a uniform gate error rate of 0.1%, a surface code cycle time of 1
microsecond, and a control system reaction time of 10 microseconds. 
 
 
 
 II  Methods for Optimized Implementation 

 
 We begin by presenting an improved, space-efficient construction of the DQI quantum circuit.
We then present new techniques for compiling the Extended Euclidean Algorithm (EEA) for the two regimes—first, where one needs to explicitly compute the Bézout coefficients in memory, and second, where an implicit access to the Bézout coefficients is sufficient. For both scenarios, our goal is to find a construction that minimizes the qubit counts.
For the first case, we make several improvements to the classical reversible EEA construction by  [ 12 ,  16 ] , resulting in lower qubit and gate counts.
For the second case, we first formalize the idea of having implicit access to the Bézout coefficients and show how one can take advantage of the low gate counts for constant-time division-free variants of EEA  [ 21 ,  17 ]  while avoiding the high ancilla overhead.
In both cases, we achieve the theoretical minimum leading order space complexity of  2 ​ n ​ b + 𝒪 ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ ( n ) ) 2nb+\mathcal{O}(\log_{2}(n)) .
We believe these improved techniques for compiling the Extended Euclidean Algorithm will be useful beyond DQI, especially in the context of Elliptic Curve Cryptography  [ 14 ,  22 ] .
We then specialize the discussion to the Optimal Polynomial Intersection (OPI) problem over binary extension fields, motivated by the fact that arithmetic circuits over binary extension fields are significantly cheaper to compile.
In the end, we show how the decoding problem for Reed-Solomon codes can be solved using the Extended Euclidean Algorithm, along with other subroutines like Chien Search  [ 23 ]  and Forney’s algorithm  [ 24 ] . We present optimized quantum circuits to account for the cost of these subroutines.
 Table   2  provides a reference for the key parameters used throughout our analysis. 
 
 
 Table 2:  Key parameters and their dual roles in the DQI framework  [ 4 ] , connecting the optimization problem with the corresponding coding theory problem.
For Reed Solomon codes, the field size  q ≥ m q\geq m . For our resource estimates,  m = q − 1 m=q-1  and  ℓ ≈ n / 2 \ell\approx n/2 .

 Bit-length of the code symbols.

 Maximum number of errors DQI is configured to handle, which sets the degree of the enhancing polynomial  P P .

 The  n × m n\times m  matrix used to compute the syndrome.

 II.1  The DQI Quantum Circuit: An Improved Construction for Qubit Efficiency 

 
 The goal of the Decoded Quantum Interferometry (DQI) circuit is to efficiently prepare the state  | P ⁡ ( f ) ⟩ = ∑ x P ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( x ) ) ​ | x ⟩ |P(f)\rangle=\sum_{x}P(f(x))|x\rangle , where  P P  is an appropriately normalized degree- ℓ \ell  polynomial.
In the original construction [ 4 , Section-8] , the algorithm requires simultaneous instantiation of an  m ​ b mb -sized error register, to hold a superposition of error patterns  e ∈ 𝔽 q m e\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{m} , and an  n ​ b nb -sized syndrome register to hold the syndrome values  s = B T ​ e s=B^{T}e .
This leads to a total space complexity of  ( m + n ) ​ b (m+n)b  plus the ancilla overhead due to reversible decoding.
Note that the decoding problem is defined on an input of size  n ​ b nb  (the length of the syndrome), and hence for the regime where  m ≫ n m\gg n , the multiplicative  m ​ b mb  qubit overhead in the DQI circuit is prohibitive. 
 
 
 We present an optimized circuit construction that significantly reduces qubit overhead by reformulating the Dicke state preparation, syndrome computation, and reversible decoding steps (stages 1, 2 and 3).
The Dicke states in the DQI circuit are used to encode the locations of  ℓ < n 2 \ell<\frac{n}{2}  errors in the length  m m  codeword. Instead of using a dense representation consisting of  m m  qubits, we use a sparse representation consisting of  ℓ ⋅ ⌈ log 2 ⁡ m ⌉ ≤ ℓ ⋅ b \ell\cdot\lceil\log_{2}{m}\rceil\leq\ell\cdot b  qubits to encode this information. We show how to efficiently prepare Sparse Dicke States in  Appendix   B .
Next, instead of generating the entire  m ​ b mb -qubit error state  | e ⟩ |e\rangle  to encode the values of errors at each of the  m m  locations in the codeword, we employ a sequential approach utilizing Measurement-Based Uncomputation (MBU)  [ 25 ,  26 ] .
We iterate through the  m m  constraints, generate one error symbol  e i e_{i}  (of size  b b  qubits) at a time, update the syndrome register with its contribution, and immediately uncompute  e i e_{i}  via measurement in the X-basis.
This leads to phase errors that we later fix as part of the reversible decoding step, where we sequentially generate each decoded error term  e i e_{i}  and apply a phase fixup using the measurement result  c i c_{i} . This allows us to reuse a single  b b -qubit ancilla register for all error terms during state preparation.
Using Sparse Dicke state preparation and sequential computation of the error terms, the ancilla cost for preparing the  n ​ b nb -qubit syndrome register reduces to  n ​ b nb  qubits. We will later show how to perform the reversible decoding on the  n ​ b nb -qubit syndrome register using  n ​ b + 𝒪 ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ ( n ) ) nb+\mathcal{O}(\log_{2}(n))  ancilla qubits, thus achieving a total space cost of  2 ​ n ​ b + 𝒪 ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ ( n ) ) 2nb+\mathcal{O}(\log_{2}(n))  qubits. 
 
 
 The following description of the DQI quantum circuit is for the general max-LINSAT case over a Galois field  𝔽 q \mathbb{F}_{q} . The construction proceeds through four main stages.
The evolution of the quantum state across these stages is summarized below, utilizing an error locator register ( ℓ ⋅ b \ell\cdot b  qubits), an error value register ( b b  qubits), and a syndrome register ( n ⋅ b n\cdot b  qubits). We omit normalization factors for clarity.

 •  
 
 Stage 1: Sparse Dicke State Preparation: 
We prepare a superposition  ∑ k = 0 l w k ​ | k ⟩ \sum_{k=0}^{l}w_{k}|k\rangle , using classically pre-computed coefficients  w k w_{k}  which define the optimal enhancing polynomial  P P   [ 27 ,  28 ,  29 ] .
This is used to prepare the  ℓ ⋅ b \ell\cdot b -qubit error locator register into the corresponding superposition of Sparse Dicke states: 
 
 
 

 
 
 ∑ k = 0 l w k ( m k ) − 1 / 2 ( ∑ 1 ≤ j 1 < j 2 < ⋯ < j k ≤ m j k + 1 ​ … ​ j ℓ = 0 | j 1 ⟩ b | j 2 ⟩ b ⋯ | j ℓ ⟩ b ) \sum_{k=0}^{l}w_{k}\binom{m}{k}^{-1/2}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j_{1}<j_{2}<\dots<j_{k}\leq m\\
j_{k+1}\dots j_{\ell}=0\end{subarray}}\ket{j_{1}}_{b}\ket{j_{2}}_{b}\cdots\ket{j_{\ell}}_{b}\right) 
 
 (3) 
 
 
 
 In  Appendix   B , we describe a way to prepare Sparse Dicke States using  2 ⋅ k ⋅ log 2 ⁡ m 2\cdot k\cdot\log_{2}{m}  qubits and  𝒪 ( m . k + k 2 b 2 ) \mathcal{O}(m.k+k^{2}b^{2})  gates. The sparse construction is useful in reducing the qubit counts for instances where  n ≪ m n\ll m . 
 
 •  
 
 Stage 2: Sequential Syndrome Computation and MBU: 
This stage translates the error locator register into a superposition of syndromes, using our space-efficient sequential approach. 
 
 
 Defining the Constraint Encoding Gates  G i G_{i} : 
The operations in this stage depend on the specific constraints of the optimization problem.
Recall the objective function  f ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) = ∑ i = 1 m f i ​ ( 𝐛 i ⋅ 𝐱 ) f(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}f_{i}(\mathbf{b}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{x})  with  f i : 𝔽 q → { + 1 , − 1 } f_{i}:\mathbb{F}_{q}\to\{+1,-1\} .
As detailed in  [ 4 , Section 8.2.1] , we find it convenient to work in terms of  g i g_{i} , which we define as  f i f_{i}  shifted and rescaled such that: 
 

 
 
 g i ​ ( x ) := f i ​ ( x ) − f ¯ c , g_{i}(x):=\frac{f_{i}(x)-\bar{f}}{c}, 
 
 (4) 
 
 where  f ¯ \bar{f}  is the average value of  f i f_{i}  over  𝔽 q \mathbb{F}_{q}  and  c c  is a normalization constant.
This standardization ensures that the Fourier transform of  g i g_{i} , denoted  g ~ i ​ ( e ) \tilde{g}_{i}(e) , vanishes at  e = 0 e=0  and is normalized ( ∑ e | g ~ i ​ ( e ) | 2 = 1 \sum_{e}|\tilde{g}_{i}(e)|^{2}=1 ).
We then define an isometry  G i G_{i} , which acts on a singe qubit  y i y_{i}  storing whether an error occurs at index  i i  or not, and maps it to a superposition over  b b -qubit error terms that encode these Fourier coefficients: 
 

 
 
 G i ​ | 0 ⟩ = | 0 ⟩ b , G i ​ | 1 ⟩ = ∑ e ∈ 𝔽 q , e ≠ 0 g ~ i ​ ( e ) ​ | e ⟩ b . G_{i}|0\rangle=|0\rangle_{b},\quad G_{i}|1\rangle=\sum_{e\in\mathbb{F}_{q},e\neq 0}\tilde{g}_{i}(e)|e\rangle_{b}. 
 
 (5) 
 
 
 
 Sequential Syndrome Computation: 
We now iterate  i i  from  1 1  to  m m , utilizing a single  b b -qubit ancilla register: 
 
 (2a)  
 
 Check if marked in Sparse Dicke State:  Check whether index  i i  is contained in the Sparse Dicke state and record the outcome in a qubit  y i y_{i} .
Naively, this requires performing a  b b -bit comparison with each of the  ℓ \ell  registers per iteration, requiring a total of  m ⋅ ℓ ⋅ b m\cdot\ell\cdot b  gates across  m m  iterations.
However, since we are sequentially comparing each of the  ℓ \ell  registers in the Sparse Dicke State with consecutive classical constants  i = 1 , 2 , … , m i=1,2,\dots,m , we can use a unary iteration  [ 30 ,  31 ]  circuit to merge adjacent comparisons and get a total cost of  m ⋅ ℓ m\cdot\ell  gates across all  m m  iterations. 
 
 (2b)  
 
 Error Generation:  Apply  G i G_{i}  on the  i i -th qubit of the mask register  | y i ⟩ \ket{y_{i}}  to temporarily generate the  i i ’th error term  | e i ⟩ b \ket{e_{i}}_{b}  using the  b b -qubit ancilla register. 
 
 (2c)  
 
 Syndrome Update:  Compute the contribution of  e i e_{i}  to the syndrome register  | s ⟩ \ket{s}  by coherently adding  b i ⋅ e i b_{i}\cdot e_{i}  to  | s ⟩ \ket{s}  (where  b i b_{i}  is the  i i -th column of  B T B^{T} ). 
 
 (2d)  
 
 Measurement-Based Uncomputation:  Immediately uncompute the ancilla  | e i ⟩ \ket{e_{i}}  using MBU  [ 25 ,  26 ] . The  b b  qubits are measured in the X basis, recording the classical outcome  c i c_{i} . This resets the ancilla for the next iteration but introduces a known phase kickback  ( − 1 ) c i ⋅ e i (-1)^{c_{i}\cdot e_{i}} . 
 
 
 At the end of the iteration, we simply do a measurement-based uncomputation of the Sparse Dicke state and perform phase fixups corresponding to the error locations during the reversible decoding step.
By reusing a single ancilla register across all  m m  iterations, we avoid the  m ​ b mb -qubit overhead of the original DQI construction. The final state of the system is 
 

 
 
 ∑ k = 0 l w k ( m k ) ​ ∑ | e | = k ( ∏ i = 1 m g ~ i ​ ( e i ) ​ ( − 1 ) c i . e i ) ​ | B T ​ e ⟩ n ​ b . \sum_{k=0}^{l}\frac{w_{k}}{\sqrt{\binom{m}{k}}}\sum_{|e|=k}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m}\tilde{g}_{i}(e_{i})(-1)^{c_{i}.e_{i}}\right)|B^{T}e\rangle_{nb}. 
 
 (6) 
 
 
 •  
 
 Stage 3: Phase Fixups via Reversible Decoding: 
In the original DQI construction  [ 4 ] , a reversible decoder was used to uncompute the explicit error register  | e ⟩ |e\rangle .
In our construction, the error register was already uncomputed via MBU in Stage 2.
However, we must now correct the phase errors  ( − 1 ) c ⋅ e (-1)^{c\cdot e}  introduced by the measurements.
Here, we perform reversible decoding using the syndrome register  | s ⟩ = | B T ​ e ⟩ |s\rangle=|B^{T}e\rangle .
We sequentially compute each decoded error term  e i e_{i}  and use the measurement results  c i c_{i}  to fix the phase error  ( − 1 ) c i . e i (-1)^{c_{i}.e_{i}} .
For the Optimal Polynomial Intersection (OPI) problem, this requires a reversible implementation of a decoder for Reed-Solomon codes, like the Berlekamp-Massey algorithm  [ 6 ]  or the Extended Euclidean Algorithm-based decoder  [ 7 ,  32 ] .
The final state of the system after this step is: 
 

 
 
 ∑ k = 0 l w k ( m k ) ​ ∑ | e | = k ( ∏ i = 1 m g ~ i ​ ( e i ) ) ​ | B T ​ e ⟩ n ​ b ≡ | P ⁡ ( f ) ~ ⟩ n ​ b \sum_{k=0}^{l}\frac{w_{k}}{\sqrt{\binom{m}{k}}}\sum_{|e|=k}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m}\tilde{g}_{i}(e_{i})\right)|B^{T}e\rangle_{nb}\equiv|\widetilde{P(f)}\rangle_{nb} 
 
 (7) 
 
 
 •  
 
 Stage 4: Final Transformation and Measurement: 
After the successful phase fixups, the syndrome register is left in a state that is the Quantum Fourier Transform of the desired output state. To obtain the final state, an Inverse Quantum Fourier Transform (IQFT) is applied to the  n n  qudits of the syndrome register. This produces the final DQI state: 
 

 
 
 | P ⁡ ( f ) ⟩ = ∑ x P ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( x ) ) ​ | x ⟩ . |P(f)\rangle=\sum_{x}P(f(x))|x\rangle. 
 
 (8) 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 II.2  Efficient Quantum Circuits for Extended Euclidean Algorithm 

 
 The Extended Euclidean Algorithm (EEA) is a cornerstone of computational number theory and a critical subroutine in algorithms for both classical and quantum computing  [ 33 ] .
While often introduced for integers, its principles generalize to other Euclidean domains like univariate polynomials with coefficients in a finite field, which is relevant to Reed-Solomon decoding.
Given two polynomials,  A ⁡ ( z ) A(z)  and  B ⁡ ( z ) B(z) , Euclid’s algorithm iteratively generates a sequence of remainder polynomials  r i ​ ( z ) r_{i}(z)  using the recurrence  r i − 2 ​ ( z ) = r i − 1 ​ ( z ) ​ q i ​ ( z ) + r i ​ ( z ) r_{i-2}(z)=r_{i-1}(z)q_{i}(z)+r_{i}(z) , where  r − 1 ​ ( z ) = A ​ ( z ) r_{-1}(z)=A(z) ,  r 0 ​ ( z ) = B ​ ( z ) r_{0}(z)=B(z)  and the quotient  q i ​ ( z ) q_{i}(z)  is chosen such that  deg ⁡ ( r i ​ ( x ) ) < deg ⁡ ( r i − 1 ​ ( x ) ) \deg(r_{i}(x))<\deg(r_{i-1}(x)) . The EEA is an extension to Euclid’s algorithm, which, alongside this sequence, also maintains two corresponding cofactor sequences,  u i ​ ( z ) u_{i}(z)  and  v i ​ ( z ) v_{i}(z) , that are updated at each step to preserve a crucial linear relationship known as Bézout’s identity. At every iteration  i i , the triplet  ( r i ​ ( z ) , u i ​ ( z ) , v i ​ ( z ) ) (r_{i}(z),u_{i}(z),v_{i}(z))  satisfies the invariant: 
 

 
 
 A ⁡ ( z ) ​ u i ​ ( z ) + B ⁡ ( z ) ​ v i ​ ( z ) = r i ​ ( z ) . A(z)u_{i}(z)+B(z)v_{i}(z)=r_{i}(z). 
 
 (9) 
 
 This process is guaranteed to terminate because the degree of the remainder polynomial,  deg ⁡ ( r i ​ ( z ) ) \deg(r_{i}(z)) , is a non-negative integer that strictly decreases with each iteration. The algorithm stops when the remainder  r m ​ ( z ) r_{m}(z)  becomes the zero polynomial. At this point, the previous remainder,  r m − 1 ​ ( z ) r_{m-1}(z) , is the  gcd ⁡ ( A ⁡ ( z ) , B ⁡ ( z ) ) \gcd(A(z),B(z)) , and the corresponding cofactors  u m − 1 ​ ( z ) u_{m-1}(z)  and  v m − 1 ​ ( z ) v_{m-1}(z)  are the final Bézout coefficients. 
 
 
 Each step of the EEA can be expressed as an application of a  2 × 2 2\times 2  transition matrix  τ i \scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{i}  on the vector  [ r i − 2 , r i − 1 ] T [r_{i-2},r_{i-1}]^{T} . The product of the first  i i  transition matrices store the Bézout coefficients  u i u_{i} ,  u i − 1 u_{i-1}  and  v i v_{i} ,  v i − 1 v_{i-1} . This matrix representation of the EEA will be useful as we describe our new constructions below. 
 
 
 

 
 
 ( r i − 1 r i ) = ( 0 1 1 − q i ) ⏟ τ i ​ ( r i − 2 r i − 1 ) \displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}r_{i-1}\\
r_{i}\end{pmatrix}=\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&-q_{i}\end{pmatrix}}_{\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{i}}\begin{pmatrix}r_{i-2}\\
r_{i-1}\end{pmatrix} 
 
 ( v i − 1 v i ) = τ i ​ ( v i − 2 v i − 1 ) \displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}v_{i-1}\\
v_{i}\end{pmatrix}=\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{\text{i}}\begin{pmatrix}v_{i-2}\\
v_{i-1}\end{pmatrix} 
 
 ( u i − 1 u i ) = τ i ​ ( u i − 2 u i − 1 ) \displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}u_{i-1}\\
u_{i}\end{pmatrix}=\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{\text{i}}\begin{pmatrix}u_{i-2}\\
u_{i-1}\end{pmatrix} 
 
 (10) 
 
 
 
 

 
 
 τ total = τ m ⋅ τ m − 1 ​ … ​ τ 1 = ( u m − 1 v m − 1 u m v m ) \displaystyle\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{\text{total}}=\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{m}\cdot\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{m-1}\dots\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}u_{m-1}&v_{m-1}\\
u_{m}&v_{m}\end{pmatrix} 
 
 ( gcd ⁡ ( A , B ) 0 ) = τ total ​ ( A B ) \displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\gcd(A,B)\\
0\end{pmatrix}=\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{\text{total}}\begin{pmatrix}A\\
B\end{pmatrix} 
 
 (11) 
 
 
 
 One important application of the Extended Euclidean Algorithm is to compute multiplicative inverses in a finite field  𝔽 q ≅ 𝔽 p ​ [ z ] / P ⁡ ( z ) \mathbb{F}_{q}\cong\mathbb{F}_{p}[z]/P(z) , where  P ⁡ ( z ) P(z)  is an irreducible polynomial.
To find the inverse of a polynomial  A ⁡ ( z ) A(z) , one applies the EEA to  P ⁡ ( z ) P(z)  and  A ⁡ ( z ) A(z) . This yields cofactor polynomials  u ⁡ ( z ) u(z)  and  v ⁡ ( z ) v(z)  such that  P ⁡ ( z ) ​ u ​ ( z ) + A ⁡ ( z ) ​ v ​ ( z ) = gcd ⁡ ( P ⁡ ( z ) , A ⁡ ( z ) ) = 1 P(z)u(z)+A(z)v(z)=\gcd(P(z),A(z))=1 . Taking this equation modulo  P ⁡ ( z ) P(z)  gives  A ⁡ ( z ) ​ v ​ ( z ) ≡ 1 ( mod P ⁡ ( z ) ) A(z)v(z)\equiv 1\pmod{P(z)} , revealing that the Bézout coefficient  v ⁡ ( z ) v(z)  is the modular inverse.
This operation is the principal computational bottleneck in Shor’s algorithm for Elliptic Curve Cryptography (ECC), a cornerstone of modern public-key infrastructure.
As a result, a significant body of research, summarized in  Table   3  and  Table   4 , has been dedicated to designing resource-efficient quantum circuits for the EEA. 
 
 
 Early work by  Proos and Zalka [16] ,  Kaye and Zalka [12]  showed that the standard Euclidean Algorithm based on polynomial long division is stepwise reversible and can be compiled using  3 ​ n ​ b + 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) 3nb+\mathcal{O}(\log{n})  qubits using a register-sharing technique, where the registers holding the remainders and the Bézout coefficients are overlapped to use a total of  2 ​ n ​ b 2nb  qubits and another  n ​ b nb  ancilla qubits are used to store the quotient in each iteration.
By tolerating a small error, they show how to further reduce the qubit counts down to  2 ​ n ​ b + 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) 2nb+\mathcal{O}(\log{n}) , by using a  𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) \mathcal{O}(\log{n})  sized register to store the quotients since large quotients occur relatively rarely in the EEA.
Their circuit design relies on a complex synchronization scheme, and they do not analyze the constant factors for gate counts.
More recent works  [ 13 ,  14 ,  18 ,  34 ]  focus on using constant-time division-free variants of the Extended Euclidean Algorithm, like Binary GCD  [ 21 ]  and Bernstein-Yang GCD  [ 17 ] , to reduce the gate counts but suffer from significantly higher qubit counts because each iteration of these constant-time EEA algorithms is not stepwise reversible and generates additional garbage that leads to a higher ancilla overhead. 
 
 
 In this work, we provide two improved constructions for compiling the EEA, the first where the EEA explicitly computes the Bézout coefficients in memory, and the second where it suffices to have an implicit representation of Bézout coefficients.
For both the constructions, we reduce the qubit counts to  2 ​ n ​ b + 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) 2nb+\mathcal{O}(\log{n}) , which is the best one can hope for given the input size is  2 ​ n ​ b 2nb , and our gate counts are lower than previous state of the art.

 II.2.1  Explicit versus Implicit Access to Bézout Coefficients 

 
 A key theme of our work is the optimization of quantum circuits by carefully considering how the results of the EEA are used by the broader algorithm. We distinguish between two computational models: one requiring  explicit  access to the Bézout coefficients, and another where  implicit  access is sufficient. This distinction allows us to select the most resource-efficient EEA implementation for the task at hand. 
 
 
 As outlined at the beginning of this section, any execution of the EEA can be viewed as generating a sequence of  2 × 2 2\times 2  transition matrices,  τ 1 , τ 2 , … , τ m \scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{1},\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{2},\dots,\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{m} . The product of these matrices,  τ total = τ m ⋅ τ m − 1 ⋯ τ 1 \scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{\text{total}}=\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{m}\cdot\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{m-1}\cdots\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{1} , contains the final Bézout coefficients as its entries. 
 
 
 
 •  
 
 Explicit Access:  This is the conventional model, where the primary goal of the EEA circuit is to compute and store the full product matrix  τ total \scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{\text{total}} . In the context of polynomial inputs, this means computing the coefficients of the final Bézout polynomials (e.g.,  v m − 1 ​ ( z ) v_{m-1}(z) ) and storing them explicitly in a quantum register. This approach is necessary when the full polynomial is required for subsequent algebraic manipulations. 
 
 •  
 
 Implicit Access.  In this model, we avoid the costly step of multiplying the sequence of transition matrices. Instead, we only compute and store a compact representation of each individual matrix  τ i \scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{i} . This metadata is sufficient to reconstruct and apply each  τ i \scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{i}  on the fly. The full Bézout coefficients are never materialized in memory but exist  implicitly  as the result of applying the sequence of stored transformations. Any operation requiring the Bézout coefficients is simply reformulated as a sequential application of the  τ i \scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{i}  matrices. 
 
 
 
 
 We illustrate the power of this implicit approach with two concrete examples: 
 
 
 
 1.  
 
 Modular Polynomial Division:  Consider the task of computing  A ⁡ ( z ) ​ B ​ ( z ) − 1 ( mod P ⁡ ( z ) ) A(z)B(z)^{-1}\pmod{P(z)} , where  P ⁡ ( z ) P(z)  is an irreducible polynomial. 
 
 •  
 
 The  explicit  “invert-then-multiply” approach first runs  EEA ​ ( P , B ) \text{EEA}(P,B)  to explicitly compute and store the inverse polynomial  v m − 1 ​ ( z ) = B ​ ( z ) − 1 ( mod P ⁡ ( z ) ) v_{m-1}(z)=B(z)^{-1}\pmod{P(z)} .
It then performs a separate quantum-quantum multiplication of  A ⁡ ( z ) A(z)  by this inverse polynomial. 
 
 •  
 
 The  implicit  “direct division” approach instead runs  EEA ​ ( P , B ) \text{EEA}(P,B)  to generate the sequence of matrices  τ 1 , τ 2 , … , τ m \scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{1},\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{2},\dots,\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{m} . To compute the division, it applies a sequence of matrix-vector multiplications to a different input vector,  [ 0 , A ⁡ ( z ) ] T [0,A(z)]^{T} . The final state after applying  τ total \scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{\text{total}}  is precisely  [ A ⁡ ( z ) ​ B ​ ( z ) − 1 , 0 ] T ( mod P ⁡ ( z ) ) [A(z)B(z)^{-1},0]^{T}\pmod{P(z)} , avoiding the need to ever store the full inverse polynomial. 
 
 
 We compare the costs of these approaches for modular division in  Table   4 . 
 
 2.  
 
 Polynomial Evaluation:  Consider evaluating the Bézout coefficient polynomial  v m − 1 ​ ( z ) v_{m-1}(z)  at a specific point  γ ∈ 𝔽 q \gamma\in\mathbb{F}_{q} . 
 
 •  
 
 With  explicit  access, one would use the stored coefficients of  v m − 1 ​ ( z ) v_{m-1}(z)  and perform a standard polynomial evaluation (e.g., via Horner’s method), requiring a series of quantum-classical multiplications. 
 
 •  
 
 With  implicit  access, we first evaluate the simple polynomial entries within each transition matrix  τ i \scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{i}  at the point  γ \gamma .
This yields a sequence of constant  2 × 2 2\times 2  matrices with entries in  𝔽 q \mathbb{F}_{q} .
We then apply this sequence of constant matrices to an initial vector, such as  [ 0 , 1 ] T [0,1]^{T} . The result of these operations over  𝔽 q \mathbb{F}_{q}  is the desired value,  v m − 1 ​ ( γ ) v_{m-1}(\gamma) . Once again, we did not ever store the full Bézout coefficient polynomial  v m − 1 ​ ( z ) v_{m-1}(z)  in memory. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Our work provides optimized circuits for both models. For explicit access, we improve upon the synchronized algorithm by  Proos and Zalka [16] , while for implicit access, we use the constant-time division-free variants of EEA  [ 21 ,  17 ]  as basis for the  Dialog  representation detailed in the following subsections.

 II.2.2  Synchronized Reversible EEA for Explicit Bézout coefficients 

 
 We advance the synchronized register-sharing framework of Zalka et al.  [ 16 ,  12 ]  through two key innovations, resulting in an implementation that is both rigorously space-optimal and time-efficient. 
 
 
 First, we achieve the  2 ​ N ​ b 2Nb  space bound deterministically via  in-register quotient storage . Prior synchronized implementations utilized an auxiliary register for the quotient  q q . To maintain low space overhead, this register was heuristically bounded to  O ⁡ ( log ⁡ N ) O(\log N)  qubits, incurring a fidelity loss for large quotients.
We eliminate this auxiliary register entirely by utilizing the guaranteed available padding within the shared register (storing the pair  ( u i , r i ) (u_{i},r_{i}) ) to temporarily store  q i + 1 q_{i+1} . This makes the  2 ​ N ​ b + O ⁡ ( log ⁡ N ) 2Nb+O(\log N)  space complexity rigorous, without relying on heuristics.  Fig.   1  shows the evolution of the state of the system for each logical iteration of the synchronized Extended Euclidean Algorithm. 
 
 
 Second, we introduce a  unified cycle architecture  to reduce time complexity constants. The baseline synchronized approach cycles through four distinct circuit blocks (Division, Normalization, Bézout Update, Swap), leading to redundant arithmetic operations.
For example, both the Division and Bézout Update step would need  n n  quantum-quantum multiplications and controlled additions each, even though for any step of the EEA these two cycles act on different parts of the shared registers storing the remainders and Bézout polynomials.
In our unified architecture, a single, optimized circuit executes every cycle, with its behavior controlled by the synchronization counter.
For example, our architecture integrates the arithmetic for Division and Bézout Update steps, such that a total of  n n  quantum-quantum multiplications and controlled additions are performed, effectively halving the dominant gate costs.
Furthermore, we optimize the access to the in-register quotient by integrating localized CSWAPs within the sequential arithmetic loop, avoiding expensive generalized quantum addressing mechanisms. 
 
 
 In  Appendix   D  we show that our synchronized reversible EEA requires  6 ​ n 6n  iterations to terminate in the worst case.  Fig.   9  in  Appendix   F  gives a complete Python implementation that uses  2 ​ n ​ b + 𝒪 ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ n ) 2nb+\mathcal{O}(\log_{2}{n})  space and  n n  quantum-quantum multiplications per iteration, resulting in the leading order gate cost of  6 ​ n 2 6n^{2}  quantum-quantum multiplications. 
 
 
 Figure 1:  Evolution of the state of the system for our improved construction of Zalka’s reversible EEA  [ 12 ,  16 ]  with in-register quotient storage which deterministically reduces the qubit counts to  2 ​ n ​ b + 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) 2nb+\mathcal{O}(\log{n}) .
 Step-0  shows the initial configuration of the system. Empty space corresponds to qubits in the  | 0 ⟩ \ket{0}  state.
The direction of the arrow denotes the order in which the coefficients of the polynomials are stored, where the tip of the arrow stores the highest degree coefficients and the tail stores the lowest degree coefficient.
At each step, three key invariants are satisfied - (a)  deg ⁡ ( u i ) + deg ⁡ ( r i ) ≤ n \deg(u_{i})+\deg(r_{i})\leq n , (b)  deg ⁡ ( r i ) < deg ⁡ ( r i − 1 ) \deg(r_{i})<\deg(r_{i-1})  and  deg ⁡ ( u i ) > deg ⁡ ( u i − 1 ) \deg(u_{i})>\deg(u_{i-1})  and (c)  deg ⁡ ( u i ) + deg ⁡ ( r i − 1 ) = n \deg(u_{i})+\deg(r_{i-1})=n .
 Step-1  iteratively computes each term of the quotient  q i + 1 = ⌊ r i − 1 / r i ⌋ q_{i+1}=\lfloor r_{i-1}/r_{i}\rfloor  where  deg ⁡ ( q i + 1 ) = deg ⁡ ( r i − 1 ) − deg ⁡ ( r i ) = m − deg ⁡ ( u i ) − deg ⁡ ( r i ) \deg(q_{i+1})=\deg(r_{i-1})-\deg(r_{i})=m-\deg(u_{i})-\deg(r_{i}) , and thus the quotient is stored in-place within the shared register storing  u i u_{i}  and  r i r_{i} .
 Step-2  performs right shift until the leader order coefficient of  r i + 1 r_{i+1}  is non-zero, such that at the end of step 2, the polynomial long division is finished and we have successfully computed both  r i + 1 r_{i+1}  and  q i + 1 q_{i+1} 
 Step-3  iteratively right shifts  u i − 1 u_{i-1}  until  deg ⁡ ( u i − 1 ) = deg ⁡ ( u i ) \deg(u_{i-1})=\deg(u_{i}) .
 Step-4  iteratively computes  u i + 1 = u i − 1 − q i + 1 ​ u i u_{i+1}=u_{i-1}-q_{i+1}u_{i}  by
iteratively performing  u i − 1 = ( u i × q i + 1 , j − u i − 1 ) × x u_{i-1}=(u_{i}\times q_{i+1,j}-u_{i-1})\times x  for all  j ​  in  ​ [ 0 , … , deg ⁡ ( q i + 1 ) ] j\text{ in }[0,\dots,\deg(q_{i+1})] . The multiplication by  x x  corresponds to a right shift for  u i − 1 u_{i-1} .
 Step-5  Swaps the two registers and finishes one logical iteration of the EEA.
 
 
 
 
 II.2.3  Dialog Representation for Implicit Bézout coefficients 

 
 To understand the power of our implicit access model, it is useful to think not just about algorithms, but about different ways to represent mathematical objects.
The familiar binary representation of an integer, for instance, is a list of  0 0  and  1 1  symbols.
It is a  representation  (known since the square-and-multiply exponentiation algorithm) because the integer can be recreated from the symbols by initializing an accumulator to  0 0 , and then iterating through the list of symbols and applying operations depending on the symbol (with ‘0’ meaning  multiply accumulator by 2  and ‘1’ meaning  multiply accumulator by 2 and add 1 ).
The binary representation has many strengths.
For example, digital logic circuits can implement the addition of binary integers in logarithmic depth.
However, binary representation is not always the optimal choice.
For example, carry-save adders use a slightly larger representation that enables performing addition in constant depth  [ 35 ] . 
 
 
 In this paper, we employ a different representation of numbers and polynomials.
We call this representation the  Dialog representation .
Concretely, the Dialog is the sequence of transition matrices,  τ 1 , τ 2 , … , τ m \scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{1},\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{2},\dots,\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{m} , generated by executing the Extended Euclidean Algorithm on the input pair  A , B A,B .
For constant-time division-free GCD algorithms  [ 21 ,  17 ] , this corresponds to a recording of the conditional branch decisions taken in each iteration.
Abstractly, the Dialog is a decomposition of a pair of inputs (numbers or polynomials) into a series of small invertible matrix multiplications.
This sequence is a valid representation because it provides a complete recipe to recover the original inputs from the output. Specifically, the total transformation  τ total = τ m ⋅ τ m − 1 ⋯ τ 1 \scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{\text{total}}=\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{m}\cdot\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{m-1}\cdots\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{1}  maps the input vector  [ A , B ] T [A,B]^{T}  to the output vector  [ gcd ⁡ ( A , B ) , 0 ] T [\gcd(A,B),0]^{T} . Consequently, applying the inverse transformation,  τ total − 1 \scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{\text{total}}^{-1} , to the output recovers the original inputs. 
 
 
 

 
 
 ( gcd ⁡ ( A , B ) 0 ) = τ total ​ ( A B ) = τ m ⋅ τ m − 1 ​ … ​ τ 1 ⏟ Dialog Representation ​ ( A B ) \begin{pmatrix}\gcd(A,B)\\
0\end{pmatrix}=\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{\text{total}}\begin{pmatrix}A\\
B\end{pmatrix}=\underbrace{\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{m}\cdot\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{m-1}\dots\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{1}}_{\text{Dialog Representation}}\begin{pmatrix}A\\
B\end{pmatrix} 
 
 
 
 
 

 
 
 ( u m u m − 1 ) = τ total ​ ( 1 0 ) \displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}u_{m}\\
u_{m-1}\end{pmatrix}=\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{\text{total}}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix} 
 
 ( v m v m − 1 ) = τ total ​ ( 0 1 ) \displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}v_{m}\\
v_{m-1}\end{pmatrix}=\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{\text{total}}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix} 
 
 ( A B ) = τ total − 1 ​ ( gcd ⁡ ( A , B ) 0 ) \displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}A\\
B\end{pmatrix}=\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{\text{total}}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\gcd(A,B)\\
0\end{pmatrix} 
 
 
 
 
 

 
 
 ( u m ⋅ C u m − 1 ⋅ C ) = τ total ​ ( C 0 ) \displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}u_{m}\cdot C\\
u_{m-1}\cdot C\end{pmatrix}=\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{\text{total}}\begin{pmatrix}C\\
0\end{pmatrix} 
 
 ( v m ⋅ C v m − 1 ⋅ C ) = τ total ​ ( 0 C ) \displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}v_{m}\cdot C\\
v_{m-1}\cdot C\end{pmatrix}=\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{\text{total}}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
C\end{pmatrix} 
 
 ( A ⋅ C B ⋅ C ) = τ total − 1 ​ ( gcd ⁡ ( A , B ) ⋅ C 0 ) \displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}A\cdot C\\
B\cdot C\end{pmatrix}=\scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{\text{total}}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\gcd(A,B)\cdot C\\
0\end{pmatrix} 
 
 
 Every symbol in the dialog representation must correspond to a small invertible matrix.
When a representation has this property, we call it a  linear representation .
Note that the binary representation  is not  linear, because the ‘1’ symbol needs to increment the accumulator, and this action does not correspond to a matrix multiplication.
The linearity of the dialog representation is a powerful property that allows us to perform complex algebraic manipulations by simply operating on the dialog itself. For example: 
 
 
 
 •  
 
 Direct Modular Division and Multiplication: 
Since the transformation from inputs to outputs is linear, we can apply it to different vectors.
To compute  C ⁡ ( z ) ​ B ​ ( z ) − 1 ( mod P ⁡ ( z ) ) C(z)B(z)^{-1}\pmod{P(z)}  where  gcd ⁡ ( P ⁡ ( z ) , B ⁡ ( z ) ) = 1 \gcd(P(z),B(z))=1 , we first compute the Dialog representation of  ( P ⁡ ( z ) , B ⁡ ( z ) ) (P(z),B(z)) .
We can then apply the forward transformation  τ total \scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{\text{total}}  to the scaled vector  [ 0 , C ⁡ ( z ) ] T [0,C(z)]^{T} .
By linearity, this directly yields the result  [ C ⁡ ( z ) ​ B ​ ( z ) − 1 , 0 ] T ( mod P ⁡ ( z ) ) [C(z)B(z)^{-1},0]^{T}\pmod{P(z)}  without ever materializing the inverse. Similarly, we can also perform modular multiplication by applying  τ total − 1 \scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{\text{total}}^{-1}  to the vector  [ C ⁡ ( z ) , 0 ] T [C(z),0]^{T}  to obtain  [ 0 , B ⁡ ( z ) ] T ( mod P ⁡ ( z ) ) [0,B(z)]^{T}\pmod{P(z)} . 
 
 •  
 
 Evaluating the Bézout Polynomial: 
To evaluate the Bézout Polynomial  v m ​ ( z ) v_{m}(z)  at a point  γ ∈ 𝔽 q \gamma\in\mathbb{F}_{q} , we can distribute the evaluation across the composition.
We simply evaluate the polynomial entries of each transformation matrix  τ i ​ ( z ) \scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{i}(z)  at  γ \gamma  to get a sequence of constant matrices, and then multiply these constant matrices together. 
 
 
 
 
 This linear representation is most powerful when the transformation matrices  τ i \scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{i}  are themselves simple. For this reason, we construct the Dialog not from the classic EEA, but from constant-time, division-free algorithms like Bernstein-Yang  [ 17 ] , whose steps correspond to simple, constant-time matrix operations such as these: 
 
 
 
 •  
 
 Doubling:  The operation  b ⁡ ( z ) ← b ⁡ ( z ) / z b(z)\leftarrow b(z)/z  corresponds to: 
 
 
 

 
 
 M double = ( 1 0 0 1 / z ) M_{\text{double}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1/z\end{pmatrix} 
 
 
 
 •  
 
 Inversion:  The operation  ( a ⁡ ( z ) , b ⁡ ( z ) ) ← ( b ⁡ ( z ) , a ⁡ ( z ) ) (a(z),b(z))\leftarrow(b(z),a(z))  corresponds to the matrix: 
 
 
 

 
 
 M invert = ( 0 1 1 0 ) M_{\text{invert}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix} 
 
 
 
 •  
 
 Addition:  The operation  b ⁡ ( z ) ← b ⁡ ( z ) − c ⋅ a ⁡ ( z ) b(z)\leftarrow b(z)-c\cdot a(z) , where  c ∈ 𝔽 q c\in\mathbb{F}_{q} , corresponds to the matrix: 
 
 
 

 
 
 M add ​ ( c ) = ( 1 0 − c 1 ) M_{\text{add}}(c)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-c&1\end{pmatrix} 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 In this case, the  Dialog  is simply the list of coefficients  c i c_{i}  and the swap decisions from each step, such that it defines the ordered list of transformation matrices  τ i \scalebox{1.44}{$\tau$}_{i} . The name Dialog itself is a mnemonic for the core arithmetic operations involved:  D oubling (or shifting),  I nversion (or swap), and  A ddition/subtraction, and the representation is similar to a logarithm because it makes multiplication and division easy but addition and subtraction hard. For two input polynomials of degree up to  n − 1 n-1 , the Dialog consists of  2 ​ n 2n  field elements. 
 
 
 A naïve quantum circuit for constructing the dialog would require  2 ​ n ​ b 2nb  qubits to store and manipulate the remainder polynomials  r − 1 = A ( z ) , r 0 = B ( z ) , r 1 , … r m r_{-1}=A(z),r_{0}=B(z),r_{1},\dots r_{m}  and another  2 ​ n ​ b 2nb  qubits to store the dialog, resulting in overall qubit count of  4 ​ n ​ b + 𝒪 ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ n ) 4nb+\mathcal{O}(\log_{2}{n}) . In order to reduce the qubit counts to  2 ​ n ​ b + 𝒪 ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ n ) 2nb+\mathcal{O}(\log_{2}{n}) , we once again use a register sharing technique by observing a key invariant of the constant-time EEA  [ 17 ] :
 In each iteration, as one field element is computed and appended to the Dialog, the total number of coefficients required to represent the active polynomials,  r i − 1 ​ ( z ) r_{i-1}(z)  and  r i ​ ( z ) r_{i}(z) , decreases by one. 
This inverse relationship between the size of the Dialog and the size of the remaining polynomial data allows for an elegant quantum circuit that dynamically reclaims and repurposes qubits.
Our construction uses a shared quantum register,  poly , which stores the coefficients of the remainder polynomials  r i − 1 ​ ( z ) , r i ​ ( z ) r_{i-1}(z),r_{i}(z)  and another quantum register,  dialog , that stores the growing Dialog.
At any point in time, we have the invariant:  len ( poly )   +   len ( dialog )   =   2 n  such that the total space occupied is  2 ​ n ​ b + 𝒪 ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ n ) 2nb+\mathcal{O}(\log_{2}{n}) .
The architecture can be described as follows: 
 
 
 
 1.  
 
 Initialization:  The  poly  register is initialized to hold the coefficients of the two input polynomials,  a ⁡ ( z ) a(z)  and  b ⁡ ( z ) b(z) , stored from opposite ends of the register. This creates a layout with a central padded region: 
 [ a 0 , a 1 , … , a n a , 0 , 0 , 0 , b n b , … , b 1 , b 0 ] [a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{n_{a}},0,0,0,b_{n_{b}},\dots,b_{1},b_{0}] 
 
 2.  
 
 Constant-Time Iteration Loop:  The circuit proceeds for a fixed  2 ​ n 2n  iterations, where  n n  is the maximum degree of the input polynomials. This ensures the algorithm completes for any input. Inside each iteration  i = 1 , 2 , … , 2 ​ n 1,2,\dots,2n : 
 
 •  
 
 The control flow (e.g., the decision to swap the logical polynomials) is managed by the integer  delta  and the constant term of  b ⁡ ( z ) b(z)  (i.e.,  poly [-1] ). A swap is performed by efficiently reversing the entire  poly  register, an operation implementable with a sequence of  len ( poly )//   2  CSWAP gates. 
 
 •  
 
 The field element  coeff  for the update step is calculated from the constant terms of the active polynomials ( poly [0]  and  poly [-1] ). 
 
 •  
 
 The polynomial subtraction,  b ⁡ ( z ) ← b ⁡ ( z ) − coeff ⋅ a ⁡ ( z ) b(z)\leftarrow b(z)-\text{coeff}\cdot a(z) , is performed in-place.
As per  [ 17 , Theorem A.1] , after  i i  iterations, we have 
 

 
 
 
 2 ​ deg ⁡ ( a k ) ≤ 2 ​ d − 1 − i + δ n \displaystyle 2\deg(a_{k})\leq 2d-1-i+\delta_{n} 
 
 
 
 
 2 ​ deg ⁡ ( b k ) ≤ 2 ​ d − 1 − i − δ n \displaystyle 2\deg(b_{k})\leq 2d-1-i-\delta_{n} 
 
 
 When  coeff ≠ 0 \text{coeff}\neq 0 , after the conditional swap, we always have  δ i ≤ 0 \delta_{i}\leq 0  such that  deg ⁡ ( b ⁡ ( z ) ) > deg ⁡ ( a ⁡ ( z ) ) \deg(b(z))>\deg(a(z)) .
Thus, in the shared register representation, we perform
 poly [ n   -   j   -   1]   -=   coeff   *   poly [ j ]   ∀ j ∈ [ 0 , len(poly) + δ i 2 ) \forall j\in[0,\frac{\text{len(poly)}+\delta_{i}}{2}) . 
 
 •  
 
 As a result of the subtraction and a subsequent logical right-shift of  b ⁡ ( z ) b(z) , the rightmost  b b  qubits of the register (previously holding  b 0 b_{0} ) become free. 
 
 •  
 
 This newly freed block of  b b  qubits is immediately removed from the  poly  register and appended at the end of the  dialog  register, thus immediately re-purposing to store the computed  coeff , which is the next element of the Dialog. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Crucially, at any intermediate step  i  of the algorithm, the  poly  and  dialog  registers contain a complete, holistic representation of the EEA’s state. The growing  dialog  register holds the partially constructed Dialog (from step  1   to   i ), while the shrinking  poly  register holds the coefficients of the current remainder polynomials,  r i − 1 ​ ( z ) r_{i-1}(z)  and  r i ​ ( z ) r_{i}(z) . From these two registers of total size  2 ​ n ​ b 2nb , one can always reconstruct the full mathematical state: the remainders are available directly, and the corresponding Bézout coefficients,  u i ​ ( z ) u_{i}(z)  and  v i ​ ( z ) v_{i}(z) , can be computed by playing back the partial Dialog.
After the full  2 ​ n 2n  iterations, the original polynomials have been completely reduced, and the Dialog has expanded to occupy the entire  2 ​ n ​ b 2nb  qubits. The final state of the system is the complete Dialog, from which the gcd and the final Bézout coefficients can be derived implicitly. This in-place architecture, detailed in  Fig.   11  of  Appendix   F , successfully constructs the full Dialog representation using a leading-order qubit cost of only  2 ​ n ​ b 2nb . 
 
 
 
 
 II.3  Optimal Polynomial Intersection (OPI) over binary extension fields 

 
 While the original DQI paper focused on the Optimal Polynomial Intersection (OPI) problem over prime fields  𝔽 p \mathbb{F}_{p} , we now study the problem defined over binary extension fields,  GF ​ ( 2 b ) \text{GF}(2^{b}) . The primary motivation for this is the prospect of substantially more efficient quantum circuits. Arithmetic in fields of characteristic 2 is often less resource-intensive to implement on a quantum computer. For instance, addition is a simple bitwise XOR, implementable with a linear number of CNOT gates.
Quantum-Classical multiplication and Squaring are linear reversible circuits that can also be implemented using only the CNOT gates.
Quantum-quantum multiplication based on Karatsuba algorithm  [ 8 ]  uses  𝒪 ⁡ ( b log 2 ⁡ 3 ) \mathcal{O}(b^{\log_{2}{3}})  Toffoli gates,  𝒪 ⁡ ( b 2 ) \mathcal{O}(b^{2})  CNOT gates, and no ancilla.
Inversion can be reduced to only  𝒪 ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ b ) \mathcal{O}(\log_{2}{b})  quantum-quantum multiplications, resulting in low Toffoli counts  [ 36 ,  10 ] .
 Appendix   C  details our optimized implementations of arithmetic circuits for  G ​ F ​ ( 2 b ) GF(2^{b}) .
This reduction in the cost of the underlying field arithmetic translates directly to lower resource requirements for the overall DQI algorithm. 
 
 
 The OPI problem and the DQI algorithm generalize naturally to this setting, as discussed in  [ 4 , Section 14] . The problem can be stated analogously: 
 
 
 Definition II.1   (OPI over  GF ​ ( 2 b ) \text{GF}(2^{b}) ) . 
 
 Let  q = 2 b q=2^{b} . Given integers  n < q − 1 n<q-1 , an instance of the Optimal Polynomial Intersection problem over  GF ​ ( 2 b ) \text{GF}(2^{b})  is as follows. For each non-zero element  y ∈ 𝔽 q ∗ y\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{*} , let  F y F_{y}  be a subset of the finite field  𝔽 q \mathbb{F}_{q} . The problem is to find a polynomial  Q ∈ 𝔽 q ​ [ y ] Q\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[y]  of degree at most  n − 1 n-1  that maximizes the objective function: 
 

 
 
 f O ​ P ​ I ​ ( Q ) = | { y ∈ 𝔽 q ∗ : Q ⁡ ( y ) ∈ F y } | f_{OPI}(Q)=|\{y\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{*}:Q(y)\in F_{y}\}| 
 
 
 
 
 
 For a general OPI instance, the subsets  F y F_{y}  can be arbitrary. As described in  Section   II.1 , implementing the DQI algorithm requires constructing constraint-encoding gates  G i G_{i}  that prepare a superposition related to these sets.
For an arbitrary set  F y F_{y} , this step would require a generic quantum state preparation, costing roughly  𝒪 ⁡ ( q ) \mathcal{O}(q)  gates for each of the  m m  constraints. 
 
 
 To reduce the gate cost of the constraint encoding step while maintaining the classical hardness of the problem, we focus our resource analysis on a specific, structured variant of OPI where the sets  F y F_{y}  are chosen to allow for highly efficient implementations of the  G i G_{i}  gates.
Specifically, we introduce and analyze the  Twisted Bent Target OPI  ( Definition   III.5 ).
In this variant, the sets  F y F_{y}  are constructed from Maiorana-McFarland bent functions. This structure ensures that each gate  G i G_{i}  can be implemented with a gate cost that scales as  𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ q ) \mathcal{O}(\log q)  rather than  O ⁡ ( q ) O(q) . 
 
 
 
 II.4  Decoding Reed Solomon Codes using the Extended Euclidean Algorithm 

 
 Having developed efficient quantum circuits for the EEA under both explicit and implicit access models, we now apply them to the central challenge of our work: constructing a complete, reversible decoder for Reed-Solomon (RS) codes. This decoder is the computational core of the DQI algorithm when applied to the OPI problem.
Syndrome decoding of Reed-Solomon codes addresses the problem of recovering an error pattern  e = [ e 0 , e 1 , … , e m − 1 ] e=[e_{0},e_{1},\dots,e_{m-1}] , where each  e i ∈ 𝔽 q e_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}  and the number of non-zero errors  | e | |e|  is at most  ℓ \ell . The input to the decoder is a list of known syndromes, which are represented as the coefficients of a syndrome polynomial,  S ⁡ ( z ) = s 0 + s 1 ​ z + ⋯ + s n − 1 ​ z n − 1 S(z)=s_{0}+s_{1}z+\dots+s_{n-1}z^{n-1} .
The full decoding procedure involves three main stages: first, solving the  key equation  using the EEA; second, finding the error locations using a Chien search; and third, finding the error values using Forney’s algorithm. 
 
 
 Solving the Key Equation: 

 
 The core of the decoding process is to solve the fundamental key equation, a polynomial congruence that relates the known  S ⁡ ( z ) S(z)  to two unknown polynomials: the error-locator polynomial  σ ⁡ ( z ) \sigma(z)  and the error-evaluator polynomial  Ω ⁡ ( z ) \Omega(z) : 
 
 
 

 
 
 σ ⁡ ( z ) ​ S ​ ( z ) ≡ Ω ⁡ ( z ) ( mod z 2 ​ ℓ ) . \sigma(z)S(z)\equiv\Omega(z)\pmod{z^{2\ell}}. 
 
 (12) 
 
 Here,  ℓ = n 2 \ell=\frac{n}{2}  is the maximum number of errors that the code can correct,  σ ⁡ ( z ) \sigma(z)  is called the error-locator polynomial because its roots identify the error positions, and  Ω ⁡ ( z ) \Omega(z)  is called the error-evaluator polynomial and is used to find the error magnitudes  [ 24 ,  6 ] . A unique solution exists if  deg ⁡ ( σ ⁡ ( z ) ) = | e | ≤ ℓ \deg(\sigma(z))=|e|\leq\ell  and  deg ⁡ ( Ω ⁡ ( z ) ) < ℓ \deg(\Omega(z))<\ell . 
 
 
 This polynomial congruence can be solved using several algorithms, most notably the Berlekamp-Massey algorithm  [ 6 ]  or the Extended Euclidean Algorithm (EEA)  [ 7 ] .
To solve it using EEA, we apply the EEA to the two specific polynomials  A ⁡ ( z ) = z 2 ​ ℓ A(z)=z^{2\ell}  and  B ⁡ ( z ) = S ⁡ ( z ) B(z)=S(z) . As established in the previous section, the EEA produces a sequence of remainders  r i ​ ( z ) r_{i}(z)  and corresponding Bézout coefficients  u i ​ ( z ) u_{i}(z)  and  v i ​ ( z ) v_{i}(z)  such that: 
 

 
 
 A ⁡ ( z ) ​ u i ​ ( z ) + B ⁡ ( z ) ​ v i ​ ( z ) = r i ​ ( z ) . A(z)u_{i}(z)+B(z)v_{i}(z)=r_{i}(z). 
 
 (13) 
 
 Taking this identity modulo  A ⁡ ( z ) = z 2 ​ ℓ A(z)=z^{2\ell} , the first term vanishes, leaving: 
 

 
 
 S ⁡ ( z ) ​ v i ​ ( z ) ≡ r i ​ ( z ) ( mod z 2 ​ ℓ ) . S(z)v_{i}(z)\equiv r_{i}(z)\pmod{z^{2\ell}}. 
 
 (14) 
 
 
 
 This directly matches the form of the key equation  Eq.   12 , with  σ ​ ( z ) ∝ v i ​ ( z ) \sigma(z)\propto v_{i}(z)  and  Ω ​ ( z ) ∝ r i ​ ( z ) \Omega(z)\propto r_{i}(z) .
The algorithm is halted at the first iteration  i i  where  deg ⁡ ( r i ​ ( z ) ) < ℓ \deg(r_{i}(z))<\ell , which guarantees that the degree constraints for a valid solution are met. 
 
 
 Our quantum circuits for EEA perform this first step using either the synchronized algorithm of  Section   II.2.2  for explicit access to both  σ ⁡ ( z ) \sigma(z)  and  Ω ⁡ ( z ) \Omega(z) , or the Dialog-based method of  Section   II.2.3  for implicit access to  σ ⁡ ( z ) \sigma(z)  and explicit access to  Ω ⁡ ( z ) \Omega(z) .
The efficiency of the subsequent decoding stages depends on which model is used. 
 
 
 
 Chien Search and Forneys algorithm: 

 
 Once the key equation has been solved via the EEA, yielding the error-locator polynomial  σ ⁡ ( z ) \sigma(z)  and the error-evaluator polynomial  Ω ⁡ ( z ) \Omega(z) , the decoder proceeds in two final stages. First, the Chien search  [ 23 ]  finds the error locations by identifying the roots of  σ ⁡ ( z ) \sigma(z) . This is achieved by evaluating  σ ⁡ ( z ) \sigma(z)  at every non-zero element of the field,  γ j ∈ 𝔽 q ∗ \gamma_{j}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{*} . An error is located at position  j j  if  σ ⁡ ( γ j − 1 ) = 0 \sigma(\gamma_{j}^{-1})=0 . Second, For each error location  j j  found by the Chien search, Forney’s algorithm  [ 24 ]  computes the corresponding error value  e j e_{j}  using the formula: 
 

 
 
 e j = − Ω ⁡ ( γ j − 1 ) σ ′ ​ ( γ j − 1 ) , e_{j}=-\frac{\Omega(\gamma_{j}^{-1})}{\sigma^{\prime}(\gamma_{j}^{-1})}, 
 
 (15) 
 
 where  σ ′ ​ ( z ) \sigma^{\prime}(z)  is the formal derivative of  σ ⁡ ( z ) \sigma(z) . This requires evaluating both  Ω ⁡ ( z ) \Omega(z)  and  σ ′ ​ ( z ) \sigma^{\prime}(z)  at the point  γ j − 1 \gamma_{j}^{-1} , followed by a modular division. 
 
 
 The implementation of these two steps differs significantly between the access models.
In the  explicit model , the EEA provides the coefficients of  σ ⁡ ( z ) \sigma(z)  and  Ω ⁡ ( z ) \Omega(z)  directly. The Chien search and Forney’s algorithm then proceed by evaluating these polynomials (and the easily computed derivative  σ ′ ​ ( z ) \sigma^{\prime}(z) ) at each point  γ j − 1 \gamma_{j}^{-1}  using  n n  quantum-classical multiplications per evaluation.
In contrast, the  implicit model  provides the Dialog of length  n = 2 ​ ℓ n=2\ell  for  σ ⁡ ( z ) \sigma(z) , while still providing  Ω ⁡ ( z ) \Omega(z)  explicitly as the final remainder.
Here, evaluating  σ ⁡ ( γ j − 1 ) \sigma(\gamma_{j}^{-1})  and  σ ′ ​ ( γ j − 1 ) \sigma^{\prime}(\gamma_{j}^{-1})  is achieved by playing back the Dialog on an augmented state vector.
Since differentiation is a linear operator, we can track the derivative through the sequence of linear transformations by applying the chain and product rules.
This requires  2 ​ n 2n  quantum-quantum multiplications and  n / 2 n/2  quantum-classical multiplications per evaluation. 
 
 
 The quantum resource costs for these stages depend heavily on whether the explicit or implicit access model was used for the initial EEA step. The trade-off is summarized in  Table   5 . The dominant costs arise from sequences of Galois field multiplications, which are categorized as either quantum-quantum (QQ), where both operands are in quantum registers, or quantum-classical (QC), where one operand is a known classical value. 
 
 
 Table 5:  
Leading-order gate costs for the reversible EEA-based RS decoder, comparing the explicit ( Section   II.2.2 ) and implicit ( Section   II.2.3 ) access models.
Gate Costs are dominated by quantum-quantum (QQ) and quantum-classical (QC) multiplications.
GF Inverse refers to the number of field inversions.
The explicit access model also has a much higher constant factor coming from other operations like  CSWAPS  and  CADDS , to manage the state of the system as part of the complex synchronization scheme.
