 As the table shows, the choice of model creates a clear trade-off. In the typical regime for OPI where  n ≪ m n\ll m , the complexity of both models is dominated by the  O ⁡ ( m ​ n ) O(mn)  terms from the Chien search and Forney’s algorithm. For the implicit model, the dominant cost is  2 ​ m ​ n 2mn  quantum-quantum multiplications needed to evaluate  σ ⁡ ( z ) \sigma(z)  and its derivative via Dialog playback. For the explicit model, the dominant cost is  m ​ n mn  quantum-classical multiplications to evaluate the three required polynomials. 
 
 
 For circuits over binary extension fields, a quantum-classical multiplication in  GF ​ ( 2 b ) \text{GF}(2^{b})  can be implemented very efficiently using only Clifford gates (a sequence of CNOTs), whereas a quantum-quantum multiplication requires non-Clifford resources (Toffoli gates). Therefore, for the parameter regimes we study, the  explicit access approach is substantially cheaper , as its dominant cost has a much lower Toffoli complexity. However, this trade-off should be carefully re-evaluated for other applications, such as those over prime fields, where quantum-classical multiplication is also expensive. 
 
 
 
 
 
 III  Classical Attacks on OPI 

 
 Suppose we have a max-LINSAT problem over  𝔽 q \mathbb{F}_{q}  with allowed sets  F 1 , … , F m F_{1},\ldots,F_{m}  of size  | F i | = r |F_{i}|=r .
Prange’s algorithm  [ 11 ]  can be applied to this problem as follows.
First, we subsample any  n n  constraints to enforce via a linear system on the  n n  variables. This system can then be solved with Gaussian elimination. Since we have disregarded the remaining  m − n m-n  constraints, we expect that roughly  r / q ⋅ ( m − n ) r/q\cdot(m-n)  will be satisfied by chance since each constraint is satisfied by  r r  of the  q q . Thus, we get, in expectation, a solution that satisfies  n + r / q ⋅ ( m − n ) n+r/q\cdot(m-n)  clauses.
Prange’s algorithm can be repeated to achieve any desired fraction of satisfied constraints, albeit potentially with an exponential overhead in the number of repetitions. This is the most efficient classical attack on OPI that we are aware of.
In  Section   III.1 , we comment on how Prange’s algorithm could be optimized and show how to estimate the runtime required to match DQI’s performance on a large supercomputer.
Then in  Section   III.2 , we explain an improvement to Prange’s algorithm over extension fields and the impact on the runtime estimates in  Section   V . 
 
 
 III.1  Optimizing Prange’s Algorithm 

 
 We first comment on how Gaussian elimination in Prange’s algorithm  [ 11 ]  can be optimized to avoid an  n 3 n^{3}  cost per attempt.
First, we can bring the generator matrix  G ∈ 𝔽 q n × m G\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n\times m}  into row echelon form such that the leftmost  n n  columns form the identity matrix. We let  𝐞 ( 1 ) , 𝐞 ( 2 ) , … , 𝐞 ( n ) ∈ Span ⁡ ( G ) \mathbf{e}^{(1)},\mathbf{e}^{(2)},\ldots,\mathbf{e}^{(n)}\in\mathrm{Span}(G)  denote the rows of this row echelon form.
We can then represent any codeword  𝐜 \mathbf{c}  as 
 

 
 
 𝐜 = ∑ i = 1 n c i ​ 𝐞 ( i ) . \mathbf{c}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}\mathbf{e}^{(i)}. 
 
 (16) 
 
 To iterate over the codewords that satisfy the first  n n  clauses, we can view the problem as iterating over the strings where the  c i ∈ F i c_{i}\in F_{i}  above.
Assume each  | F i | |F_{i}|  has size at least 2. Then we can iterate over strings that satisfy the first  n n  clauses using a Gray code that only updates two of the first  n n  coordinates per iteration. Over  𝔽 2 b \mathbb{F}_{2^{b}} , this could be implemented as two vectorized SIMD operations per update. 
 
 
 The semicircle law of  [ 4 ]  states that DQI will satisfy  t t  clauses in expectation, where 
 

 
 
 t / m = ( ℓ m ​ ( 1 − r q ) + ( 1 − ℓ m ) ​ r q ) 2 . t/m=\left(\sqrt{\frac{\ell}{m}\left(1-\frac{r}{q}\right)}+\sqrt{\left(1-\frac{\ell}{m}\right)\frac{r}{q}}\right)^{2}. 
 
 (17) 
 
 
 
 Suppose we apply the classical attack to find a solution satisfying at least  t t  of the  m m  clauses, where  t > n t>n . The number of satisfied clauses is distributed binomially, and the probability of sampling such a solution is: 
 

 
 
 p ⁡ ( t ) := ∑ s = t m ( m − n s − n ) ​ ( r q ) s − n ​ ( 1 − r q ) m − s . p(t):=\sum_{s=t}^{m}\binom{m-n}{s-n}\left(\frac{r}{q}\right)^{s-n}\left(1-\frac{r}{q}\right)^{m-s}. 
 
 (18) 
 
 To match this with the classical attack will take, in expectation,  1 / p ⁡ ( t ) 1/p(t)  trials.
We estimate the classical resources required for one trial, using the Frontier supercomputer as an example  [ 37 ] .
Frontier contains 37,632 AMD Instinct MI250X accelerators (i.e., GPUs). Each MI250x has 220 compute units (CUs) and runs at 1.7 GHz. Each CU has 4 SIMD lanes. So, we can imagine that with an optimized implementation, Frontier could potentially run this many trials of the heuristic in one day: 
 

 
 
 24 × 3600 × 1 2 × 37,632 × 220 × 4 × 1.7 × 10 9 ≈ 2.43 × 10 21 24\times 3600\times\frac{1}{2}\times 37,632\times 220\times 4\times 1.7\times 10^{9}\approx 2.43\times 10^{21} 
 
 (19) 
 
 Note that no networking is required for our classical attacks (i.e., they are embarrassingly parallel), so we could easily distribute the workload over the Internet to many independent machines that run at different times, in odd hours, etc. 
 
 
 
 III.2  Extended Prange’s Algorithm over Extension Fields 

 
 The eXtended Prange’s algorithm (XP) is a folklore algorithm alluded to in  [ 38 ] , although we do not know of a reference that fully formalizes it as a cryptanalytic attack.
For simplicity, suppose  F 1 = F 2 = … = F m = F F_{1}=F_{2}=\ldots=F_{m}=F . Suppose  q = p b q=p^{b}  for prime  p p . Let 
 

 
 
 ϕ : \displaystyle\phi: 
 𝔽 p b ↔ 𝔽 p b \displaystyle\mathbb{F}_{p^{b}}\leftrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{p}^{b} 
 
 
 
 ϕ ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) \displaystyle\phi(\mathbf{x}) 
 = ( tr ( ζ x ) : ζ ∈ ℬ ) \displaystyle=\left(\mathrm{tr}\left(\zeta x\right):\zeta\in\mathcal{B}\right) 
 
 (20) 
 
 denote the natural bijection from elements in  𝔽 p b \mathbb{F}_{p^{b}}  to vectors in  𝔽 p b \mathbb{F}_{p}^{b} , where  ℬ \mathcal{B}  is any basis for  𝔽 p b \mathbb{F}_{p^{b}}  over  𝔽 p \mathbb{F}_{p}  and  tr ⁡ ( α ) = ∑ i = 0 b − 1 α q i \mathrm{tr}(\alpha)=\sum_{i=0}^{b-1}\alpha^{q^{i}}  is the field trace (See Ch. 2 of  [ 39 ] ). Let  Aff ⊆ 2 𝔽 p b \mathrm{Aff}\subseteq 2^{\mathbb{F}_{p}^{b}}  denote the set of nonempty affine subspaces of  𝔽 p b \mathbb{F}_{p}^{b} .
For each codimension  s ∈ { 0 , 1 , … , b } s\in\{0,1,\ldots,b\} , let  A s ∈ Aff A_{s}\in\mathrm{Aff}  denote the  ( b − s ) (b-s) -dimensional affine subspace which has the  largest intersection   | A ∩ ϕ ⁡ ( F ) | |A\cap\phi(F)|  with the set  ϕ ⁡ ( F ) \phi(F) , and let  P ⁡ [ s ] P[s]  denote its fractional overlap by  P ⁡ [ s ] := | A ∩ ϕ ⁡ ( F ) | | A | P[s]:=\frac{|A\cap\phi(F)|}{|A|} . The general idea is that if we “pay”  s s  affine constraints over the base field on one of the coordinates  i ∈ [ m ] i\in[m] , then we get to flip a  P ⁡ [ s ] P[s] -biased coin that determines whether the  i i th constraint is satisfied. We have a budget of  n ⋅ b n\cdot b  base field constraints to spend. We call any budget-respecting strategy which assigns a value of  s s  to each coordinate  i i  an “XP Allocation Strategy”. We consider two such strategies below. 
 
 
 III.2.1  Expectation-Optimal XP Allocation Strategies via Linear Programming 

 
 We can use a randomized procedure to decide  s s  for each clause. For each clause, this strategy samples  s s  independently according to the discrete probability distribution  p s p_{s} .
This distribution is constrained such that, on average, we use  b ​ n / m bn/m   𝔽 p \mathbb{F}_{p} -linear constraints, meaning we are exactly within-budget. It can be optimized to get the maximum expected number of satisfied constraints by solving the following linear program (LP): 
 

 
 
 maximize \displaystyle\mathrm{maximize}\, 
 ∑ s = 0 b p s ​ P ​ [ s ] \displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{b}p_{s}P[s] 
 
 
 
 subject ​ to \displaystyle\mathrm{subject\,to}\, 
 ∑ s = 0 b p s = 1 \displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{b}p_{s}=1 
 
 
 
 and \displaystyle\mathrm{and}\, 
 ∑ s = 0 b s ​ p s ≤ b ​ n / m \displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{b}sp_{s}\leq\nicefrac{{bn}}{{m}} 
 
 
 
 where \displaystyle\mathrm{where}\,\,\, 
 p s ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] ​ ∀ s ∈ { 0 , … , b } . \displaystyle p_{s}\in[0,1]\,\,\,\,\,\forall s\in\{0,\ldots,b\}. 
 
 (21) 
 
 The optimal value of the above LP gives the optimal expected fraction of satisfied constraints achieved by the XP.
We recover the truncation heuristic when we replace  Aff \mathrm{Aff}  with the set  𝒯 := { { e } : e ∈ 𝔽 q } ∪ { 𝔽 q } \mathcal{T}:=\{\{e\}:e\in\mathbb{F}_{q}\}\cup\{\mathbb{F}_{q}\} .
There are exponentially many affine subspaces in  b b , the degree of the field extension (e.g. when  p = 2 p=2 ,  | Aff | ∼ 7.37 ⋅ 2 ( b + 1 ) 2 / 4 |\mathrm{Aff}|\sim 7.37\cdot 2^{(b+1)^{2}/4} ). But for  p = 2 p=2  and  b ≤ 10 b\leq 10 , we have  | Aff | < 10 9 |\mathrm{Aff}|<10^{9}  and we can explicitly enumerate these to compute the  P ⁡ [ s ] P[s]  table and solve eq ( 21 ).
Moreover, it seems unlikely we would really need to enumerate all the affine subspaces, since most of them will have small overlap with the set  F F . Perhaps, using better heuristics or Fourier-analytic techniques, we could directly compute the optimal allocation in time  poly ⁡ ( q ) \mathrm{poly}(q) .
The XP can sometimes achieve a significantly higher expected value than Prange’s algorithm. For example, over  𝔽 4 \mathbb{F}_{4}  with  | F | = 2 |F|=2  and  n / m = 1 / 2 \nicefrac{{n}}{{m}}=1/2 , XP has an expected value of  1 1 , whereas Prange’s algorithm would give an expected value of  3 / 4 \nicefrac{{3}}{{4}} . 
 
 
 
 III.2.2  Probability-Optimal XP Allocation Strategies via a Knapsack Solver 

 
 If  t t  is larger than the optimal expected fraction of satisfied constraints from eq ( 21 ), we may need to run the XP many times to obtain one sample that satisfies  ≥ t \geq t  clauses. Rather than maximizing the expectation value, we should instead maximize the probability of obtaining a sample that satisfies  ≥ t \geq t  clauses. The optimal-probability allocation is the solution to the following problem: 
 

 
 
 maximize \displaystyle\mathrm{maximize}\,\,\, 
 ℙ ⁡ ( ∑ i = 1 m X i ≥ t ) \displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}X_{i}\geq t\right) 
 
 
 
 subject ​ to \displaystyle\mathrm{subject\,to}\,\,\, 
 X i ∼ Ber ⁡ ( P ⁡ [ s i ] ) \displaystyle X_{i}\sim\mathrm{Ber}(P[s_{i}]) 
 
 
 
 
 ∑ i = 1 m s i ≤ B \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}s_{i}\leq B 
 
 
 
 
 0 ≤ s i ≤ b \displaystyle 0\leq s_{i}\leq b 
 
 (22) 
 
 This problem is non-convex, and the solution may not be the same as the allocation strategy from eq ( 21 ).
To obtain good approximate solutions to equation ( 22 ), we use a dynamic programming algorithm explained in  Appendix   A . 
 
 
 
 III.2.3  Search for good target sets for OPI over binary extension fields 

 
 The choice of good target sets seems to be a nontrivial problem. For a fixed set size  | F | |F| , we want to make the overlaps  P ⁡ [ s ] P[s]  as small as possible for each  s ∈ { 0 , … , b } s\in\{0,\ldots,b\} . This way, the XP allocation strategies of eq ( 21 ) and eq ( 22 ) will have lower optimal values, giving a larger classical runtime. Identifying the optimal such set  F F  or even the best attainable list of overlaps  P ⁡ [ s ] P[s]  appears to be an open problem.
Some recent insight on a good way to choose  F F  came from  [ 40 ] , who performed algebraic cryptanalysis of the binary OPI problem in the case that  F F  is chosen as the solution set of a multivariate polynomial equation (i.e., an algebraic variety).
They concluded that for their application, cubic or higher-degree varieties were required. This is because quadratic varieties contain many dimension  b / 2 b/2  affine subspaces. They operated in a high-rate regime (specifically,  n / m > 7 / 8 n/m>7/8 ) so that they could obtain an exact solution with their quantum algorithm. The problem that then arises is that these affine subspaces are so large that the number of linear equations sufficient to enforce them as constraints is small enough that a rate  1 / 2 1/2  code would already be able to satisfy every clause using XP.
However, in our scenario, we propose to use DQI, which is an  approximate optimization  algorithm. This allows us to keep the rate  n / m n/m  significantly lower than in  [ 40 ]  (in fact, the space cost scaling is dominated by  n n , so the lower the rate of the code, the better).
Although we do not obtain exact solutions, we obtain better approximate optima than can be obtained in polynomial time by any algorithm known to us, which is sufficient to obtain the large speedups here. 
 
 
 In fact, we find that a particular quadratic variety is an excellent choice to get a quantum advantage.
Specifically, we chose bent functions from the Maiorana-McFarland family.
In more detail, let  n = 2 ​ k n=2k . Let 
 

 
 
 S k = { ( x 1 , … , x n ) ∈ 𝔽 2 n : ∑ i = 1 k x i ​ x i + k = 1 mod 2 } . S_{k}=\left\{(x_{1},...,x_{n})\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}:\sum_{i=1}^{k}x_{i}x_{i+k}=1\mod 2\right\}. 
 
 (23) 
 
 As we show in  Section   III.2.4 , for any affine subspace  A ⊂ 𝔽 2 n A\subset\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}  of dimension  d = dim A d=\dim A , we have 
 

 
 
 | A ∩ S k | ≤ { 2 d d < k 2 d − 1 + 2 k − 2 k ≤ d < 2 ​ k − 1 2 2 ​ k − 2 d = 2 ​ k − 1 2 2 ​ k − 1 − 2 k − 1 d = 2 ​ k . |A\cap S_{k}|\leq\begin{cases}2^{d}&d<k\\
2^{d-1}+2^{k-2}&k\leq d<2k-1\\
2^{2k-2}&d=2k-1\\
2^{2k-1}-2^{k-1}&d=2k.\end{cases} 
 
 (24) 
 
 This can be fed as input to the knapsack solver described in  Appendix   A . 
 
 
 
 III.2.4  Upper bounds on affine intersections of Maiorana-McFarland target sets 

 
 We have found a technical proof of the upper bounds in ( 24 ), although we believe a simpler proof may be possible. All the key concepts involved are present in the proof for affine spaces with  k ≤ dim A < 2 ​ k − 1 k\leq\dim A<2k-1 . Therefore, we defer the proofs of upper bounds for  d < k d<k ,  d = 2 ​ k − 1 d=2k-1 , and  d = 2 ​ k d=2k  to  Appendix   E  and focus here on  k ≤ d < 2 ​ k − 1 k\leq d<2k-1 . 
 
 
 Let  k k  be a positive integer. We use the standard dot product  ⟨ x , y ⟩ := ∑ i = 1 k x i ​ y i ∈ 𝔽 2 \langle x,y\rangle:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}x_{i}y_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}  where  x , y ∈ 𝔽 2 k x,y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}  to define 
 

 
 
 R k := { ( x , y ) ∈ 𝔽 2 k × 𝔽 2 k : ⟨ x , y ⟩ = 0 } \displaystyle R_{k}:=\left\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}:\langle x,y\rangle=0\right\} 
 
 (25) 
 
 
 S k := { ( x , y ) ∈ 𝔽 2 k × 𝔽 2 k : ⟨ x , y ⟩ = 1 } \displaystyle S_{k}:=\left\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}:\langle x,y\rangle=1\right\} 
 
 (26) 
 
 so that  R k ∪ S k = 𝔽 2 2 ​ k R_{k}\cup S_{k}=\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k}  and  R k ∩ S k = ∅ R_{k}\cap S_{k}=\emptyset . We will use  ⊔ \sqcup  to make simultaneous assertions about the union and intersection of two sets. Namely, we define  X ⊔ Y = Z ⇔ ( X ∪ Y = Z ) ∧ ( X ∩ Y = ∅ ) X\sqcup Y=Z\iff(X\cup Y=Z)\,\land\,(X\cap Y=\emptyset)  for any three sets  X X ,  Y Y , and  Z Z . Thus,  R k ⊔ S k = 𝔽 2 2 ​ k R_{k}\sqcup S_{k}=\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k} . In arguments below, we will make frequent use of the implication  X ⊔ Y = Z ⟹ | X | + | Y | = | Z | X\sqcup Y=Z\implies|X|+|Y|=|Z| . 
 
 
 We begin by deriving  recursive structure formulas  that express the sets  A ∩ R k A\cap R_{k}  and  A ∩ S k A\cap S_{k}  as the disjoint union  ⊔ \sqcup  of the sets of the form  B ∩ R k − 1 B\cap R_{k-1}  and  B ∩ S k − 1 B\cap S_{k-1}  for some affine subspaces  B ⊂ 𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 B\subset\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2} . To that end, we use two linear projections  π 0 : 𝔽 2 2 ​ k → 𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 \pi_{0}:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2}  and  π 1 : 𝔽 2 2 ​ k → 𝔽 2 2 \pi_{1}:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2}  defined by 
 

 
 
 π 0 ​ ( x ​ a ​ y ​ b ) = x ​ y , π 1 ​ ( x ​ a ​ y ​ b ) = a ​ b \displaystyle\pi_{0}(xayb)=xy,\quad\pi_{1}(xayb)=ab 
 
 (27) 
 
 where  x , y ∈ 𝔽 2 k − 1 x,y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k-1}  and  a , b ∈ 𝔽 2 a,b\in\mathbb{F}_{2}  and where juxtaposition denotes concatenation. For a  w ∈ 𝔽 2 2 ​ k w\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k} , we will refer to  π 0 ​ ( w ) \pi_{0}(w)  as the  root  of  w w  and to  π 1 ​ ( w ) \pi_{1}(w)  as the  suffix  of  w w . The map  ( π 0 , π 1 ) : 𝔽 2 2 ​ k → 𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 × 𝔽 2 2 (\pi_{0},\pi_{1}):\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2}  is invertible. Its inverse is  ⊗ : 𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 × 𝔽 2 2 → 𝔽 2 2 ​ k \otimes:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k}  defined as 
 

 
 
 x ​ y ⊗ a ​ b = x ​ a ​ y ​ b \displaystyle xy\otimes ab=xayb 
 
 (28) 
 
 where  x , y ∈ 𝔽 2 k − 1 x,y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k-1}  and  a , b ∈ 𝔽 2 a,b\in\mathbb{F}_{2}  as before. For  X ⊂ 𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 X\subset\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2} ,  Y ⊂ 𝔽 2 2 Y\subset\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2} , and  s ∈ 𝔽 2 2 s\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2} , we will write  X ⊗ s = { x ⊗ s | x ∈ X } X\otimes s=\{x\otimes s\,|\,x\in X\}  and  X ⊗ Y = { x ⊗ y | x ∈ X , y ∈ Y } X\otimes Y=\{x\otimes y\,|\,x\in X,y\in Y\} . Note that  | X ⊗ Y | = | X | ⋅ | Y | |X\otimes Y|=|X|\cdot|Y| . 
 
 
 The recursive structure of  A ∩ R k A\cap R_{k}  and  A ∩ S k A\cap S_{k}  arises from 
 

 
 
 ⟨ x , y ⟩ = ⟨ π 0 ​ ( x ) , π 0 ​ ( y ) ⟩ + ⟨ π 1 ​ ( x ) , π 1 ​ ( y ) ⟩ \displaystyle\langle x,y\rangle=\langle\pi_{0}(x),\pi_{0}(y)\rangle+\langle\pi_{1}(x),\pi_{1}(y)\rangle 
 
 (29) 
 
 where we slightly abused notation by using  ⟨ . , . ⟩ \langle.,.\rangle  to refer to the dot product in three different vector spaces,  𝔽 2 2 ​ k \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k} ,  𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2} , and  𝔽 2 2 \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2} . We now state and prove the recursive structure formulas. 
 
 
 Lemma III.1   (Recursive Structure Formulas) . 
 
 
 For integers  d ≥ k > 1 d\geq k>1 , let  A A  be a  d d -dimensional affine subspace of  𝔽 2 2 ​ k \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k} . Then 
 

 
 
 A ∩ R k = ⨆ σ ∈ F { ( A σ ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ σ if σ = 11 ( A σ ′ ∩ R k − 1 ) ⊗ σ if σ ≠ 11 \displaystyle A\cap R_{k}=\bigsqcup_{\sigma\in F}\begin{cases}(A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes\sigma&\text{if}\quad\sigma=11\\
(A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1})\otimes\sigma&\text{if}\quad\sigma\neq 11\end{cases} 
 
 (30) 
 
 
 A ∩ S k = ⨆ σ ∈ F { ( A σ ′ ∩ R k − 1 ) ⊗ σ if σ = 11 ( A σ ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ σ if σ ≠ 11 \displaystyle A\cap S_{k}=\bigsqcup_{\sigma\in F}\begin{cases}(A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1})\otimes\sigma&\text{if}\quad\sigma=11\\
(A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes\sigma&\text{if}\quad\sigma\neq 11\end{cases} 
 
 (31) 
 
 where  F = π 1 ​ ( A ) F=\pi_{1}(A)  is an affine subspace of  𝔽 2 2 \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2}  and the sets  A σ ′ ⊂ π 0 ​ ( A ) A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\subset\pi_{0}(A)  are affine subspaces of  𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2}  that arise as translations of the same linear subspace  W ′ ⊂ 𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 W^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2}  with  dim W ′ = dim A − dim F \dim W^{\prime}=\dim A-\dim F . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We can write  A = a + V A=a+V  where  a ∈ 𝔽 2 2 ​ k a\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k}  is a fixed bit string and  V V  is a  d d -dimensional linear subspace of  𝔽 2 2 ​ k \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k} . Let  ℬ = { b 1 , … , b d } \mathcal{B}=\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{d}\}  be an arbitrary basis of  V V . Suppose  b 1 , b 2 ∈ ℬ b_{1},b_{2}\in\mathcal{B}  are two distinct bit strings with suffix  01 01 . Then  b 1 + b 2 b_{1}+b_{2}  has suffix  00 00 , so  { b 1 + b 2 } ∪ ℬ ∖ { b 2 } \{b_{1}+b_{2}\}\cup\mathcal{B}\setminus\{b_{2}\}  is another basis of  V V  with the number of elements with suffix  01 01  reduced by one. By iterating this procedure, we can reduce the number of elements with suffix  01 01  to at most one. 
 
 
 Similarly, we can reduce the number of basis elements with suffixes  10 10  and  11 11  to at most one. Moreover, if  ℬ \mathcal{B}  contains three elements with distinct nonzero suffixes, then we can replace one of them with an element with suffix  00 00  by adding the other two elements to it. Thus,  V V  has a basis  ℬ \mathcal{B}  that takes one of the following three forms 
 

 
 
 ℬ = { ℬ 00 ℬ 00 ⊔ { b } ℬ 00 ⊔ { b , c } \displaystyle\mathcal{B}=\begin{cases}\mathcal{B}_{00}\\
\mathcal{B}_{00}\sqcup\{b\}\\
\mathcal{B}_{00}\sqcup\{b,c\}\end{cases} 
 
 (32) 
 
 where  ℬ 00 \mathcal{B}_{00}  consists of bit strings with suffix  00 00  and  b , c b,c  are two bit strings with distinct nonzero suffixes. 
 
 
 Define  F := π 1 ​ ( A ) F:=\pi_{1}(A)  as the set of suffixes of the elements of  A = a + V A=a+V . The projector  π 1 \pi_{1}  is a linear map, so  F F  is an affine subspace of  𝔽 2 2 \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2}  and therefore it has either one, two or four elements. Indeed, 
 

 
 
 ℬ = ℬ 00 \displaystyle\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{00} 
 ⇔ F = { σ } \displaystyle\iff F=\{\sigma\} 
 
 (33) 
 
 
 ℬ = ℬ 00 ⊔ { b } \displaystyle\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{00}\sqcup\{b\} 
 ⇔ F = { σ , τ } \displaystyle\iff F=\{\sigma,\tau\} 
 
 (34) 
 
 
 ℬ = ℬ 00 ⊔ { b , c } \displaystyle\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{00}\sqcup\{b,c\} 
 ⇔ F = { 00 , 01 , 10 , 11 } \displaystyle\iff F=\{00,01,10,11\} 
 
 (35) 
 
 where  σ = π 1 ​ ( a ) \sigma=\pi_{1}(a)  and  τ = π 1 ​ ( a + b ) \tau=\pi_{1}(a+b) . 
 
 
 For every suffix  σ ∈ F \sigma\in F , let  A σ := A ∩ π 1 − 1 ​ ( { σ } ) A_{\sigma}:=A\cap\pi_{1}^{-1}(\{\sigma\})  be the set of elements in  A A  with suffix  σ \sigma  and  A σ ′ := π 0 ​ ( A σ ) A_{\sigma}^{\prime}:=\pi_{0}(A_{\sigma})  the set of roots of those elements of  A A . A singleton set is an affine space, the intersection of affine subspaces is an affine subspace, and  π 0 \pi_{0} ,  π 1 \pi_{1}  are linear maps, so  A σ A_{\sigma}  and  A σ ′ A_{\sigma}^{\prime}  are affine subspaces of  𝔽 2 2 ​ k \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k}  and  𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2} , respectively. We can express  A A  in terms of  A σ ′ A_{\sigma}^{\prime}  for  σ ∈ F \sigma\in F  as 
 

 
 
 A = ⨆ σ ∈ F A σ = ⨆ σ ∈ F π 0 ​ ( A σ ) ⊗ σ = ⨆ σ ∈ F A σ ′ ⊗ σ . \displaystyle A=\bigsqcup_{\sigma\in F}A_{\sigma}=\bigsqcup_{\sigma\in F}\pi_{0}(A_{\sigma})\otimes\sigma=\bigsqcup_{\sigma\in F}A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\otimes\sigma. 
 
 (36) 
 
 Define  W := span ⁡ ( ℬ 00 ) W:=\mathrm{span}(\mathcal{B}_{00}) , so that  A σ = u σ + W A_{\sigma}=u_{\sigma}+W  where  u σ ∈ 𝔽 2 2 ​ k u_{\sigma}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k}  is any bit string in  A A  with suffix  π 1 ​ ( u σ ) = σ \pi_{1}(u_{\sigma})=\sigma . Then,  A σ ′ = π 0 ​ ( u σ ) + W ′ A_{\sigma}^{\prime}=\pi_{0}(u_{\sigma})+W^{\prime}  where  W ′ := π 0 ​ ( W ) W^{\prime}:=\pi_{0}(W) . But  π 0 \pi_{0}  is a bijection on  W W , so  dim A σ ′ = dim W ′ = dim W = | ℬ 00 | \dim A_{\sigma}^{\prime}=\dim W^{\prime}=\dim W=|\mathcal{B}_{00}| . 
 
 
 Finally, intersecting both sides of ( 36 ) with  R k R_{k}  (respectively,  S k S_{k} ), we obtain 
 

 
 
 A ∩ R k = ⨆ σ ∈ F { ( A σ ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ σ if σ = 11 ( A σ ′ ∩ R k − 1 ) ⊗ σ if σ ≠ 11 \displaystyle A\cap R_{k}=\bigsqcup_{\sigma\in F}\begin{cases}(A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes\sigma&\text{if}\quad\sigma=11\\
(A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1})\otimes\sigma&\text{if}\quad\sigma\neq 11\end{cases} 
 
 (37) 
 
 
 A ∩ S k = ⨆ σ ∈ F { ( A σ ′ ∩ R k − 1 ) ⊗ σ if σ = 11 ( A σ ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ σ if σ ≠ 11 \displaystyle A\cap S_{k}=\bigsqcup_{\sigma\in F}\begin{cases}(A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1})\otimes\sigma&\text{if}\quad\sigma=11\\
(A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes\sigma&\text{if}\quad\sigma\neq 11\end{cases} 
 
 (38) 
 
 where we switch between  R k R_{k}  and  S k S_{k}  when  σ = 11 \sigma=11  as needed in light of ( 29 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark III.1 . 
 
 The recursive structure formulas take five forms depending on  0 ≤ dim F ≤ 2 0\leq\dim F\leq 2  and on whether  11 ∈ F 11\in F . 
 
 1.  
 
 If  F = { σ } F=\{\sigma\}  with  σ ≠ 11 \sigma\neq 11 , then 
 

 
 
 A ∩ R k \displaystyle A\cap R_{k} 
 = ( A σ ′ ∩ R k − 1 ) ⊗ σ \displaystyle=(A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1})\otimes\sigma 
 
 (39) 
 
 
 A ∩ S k \displaystyle A\cap S_{k} 
 = ( A σ ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ σ . \displaystyle=(A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes\sigma. 
 
 (40) 
 
 
 2.  
 
 If  F = { 11 } F=\{11\} , then 
 

 
 
 A ∩ R k \displaystyle A\cap R_{k} 
 = ( A 11 ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ 11 \displaystyle=(A_{11}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes 11 
 
 (41) 
 
 
 A ∩ S k \displaystyle A\cap S_{k} 
 = ( A 11 ′ ∩ R k − 1 ) ⊗ 11 . \displaystyle=(A_{11}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1})\otimes 11. 
 
 (42) 
 
 
 3.  
 
 If  F = { σ , τ } F=\{\sigma,\tau\}  with  11 ∉ F 11\notin F  and  σ ≠ τ \sigma\neq\tau , then 
 

 
 
 A ∩ R k \displaystyle A\cap R_{k} 
 = ( ( A σ ′ ∩ R k − 1 ) ⊗ σ ) ⊔ ( ( A τ ′ ∩ R k − 1 ) ⊗ τ ) \displaystyle=\left((A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1})\otimes\sigma\right)\sqcup\left((A_{\tau}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1})\otimes\tau\right) 
 
 (43) 
 
 
 A ∩ S k \displaystyle A\cap S_{k} 
 = ( ( A σ ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ σ ) ⊔ ( ( A τ ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ τ ) . \displaystyle=\left((A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes\sigma\right)\sqcup\left((A_{\tau}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes\tau\right). 
 
 (44) 
 
 
 4.  
 
 If  F = { σ , 11 } F=\{\sigma,11\}  with  σ ≠ 11 \sigma\neq 11 , then 
 

 
 
 A ∩ R k \displaystyle A\cap R_{k} 
 = ( ( A σ ′ ∩ R k − 1 ) ⊗ σ ) ⊔ ( ( A 11 ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ 11 ) \displaystyle=\left((A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1})\otimes\sigma\right)\sqcup\left((A_{11}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes 11\right) 
 
 (45) 
 
 
 A ∩ S k \displaystyle A\cap S_{k} 
 = ( ( A σ ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ σ ) ⊔ ( ( A 11 ′ ∩ R k − 1 ) ⊗ 11 ) . \displaystyle=\left((A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes\sigma\right)\sqcup\left((A_{11}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1})\otimes 11\right). 
 
 (46) 
 
 
 5.  
 
 If  F = { 00 , 01 , 10 , 11 } F=\{00,01,10,11\} , then 
 

 
 
 A ∩ R k = ( ( A 11 ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ 11 ) ⊔ ⨆ σ ∈ { 00 , 01 , 10 } ( A σ ′ ∩ R k − 1 ) ⊗ σ \displaystyle A\cap R_{k}=((A_{11}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes 11)\sqcup\bigsqcup_{\sigma\in\{00,01,10\}}(A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1})\otimes\sigma 
 
 (47) 
 
 
 A ∩ S k = ( ( A 11 ′ ∩ R k − 1 ) ⊗ 11 ) ⊔ ⨆ σ ∈ { 00 , 01 , 10 } ( A σ ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ σ . \displaystyle A\cap S_{k}=((A_{11}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1})\otimes 11)\sqcup\bigsqcup_{\sigma\in\{00,01,10\}}(A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes\sigma. 
 
 (48) 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Lemma  III.1  enables inductive proof of the upper bound on  | A ∩ R k | |A\cap R_{k}|  and  | A ∩ S k | |A\cap S_{k}|  for affine spaces with dimension  dim A = 2 ​ k − 1 \dim A=2k-1 , which we defer to Lemma  E.3 . Below, we prove that  | A ∩ R k | ≤ 2 d − 1 + 2 k − 1 |A\cap R_{k}|\leq 2^{d-1}+2^{k-1}  and  | A ∩ S k | ≤ 2 d − 1 + 2 k − 2 |A\cap S_{k}|\leq 2^{d-1}+2^{k-2}  for affine  A A  with  k ≤ dim A < 2 ​ k − 1 k\leq\dim A<2k-1  which requires more sophisticated tools in addition to Lemma  III.1 . The right-hand sides  2 d − 1 + 2 k − 1 2^{d-1}+2^{k-1}  and  2 d − 1 + 2 k − 2 2^{d-1}+2^{k-2}  indicate that the cardinality of  A ∩ S k A\cap S_{k}  (respectively,  A ∩ R k A\cap R_{k} ) can exceed  1 2 ​ | A | \frac{1}{2}|A|  by at most  2 k − 2 2^{k-2}  (respectively,  2 k − 1 2^{k-1} ). Thus,  | A ∩ R k | |A\cap R_{k}|  can exceed  1 2 ​ | A | \frac{1}{2}|A|  by twice as much as  | A ∩ S k | |A\cap S_{k}| , so if a recursive structure formula for  A ∩ S k A\cap S_{k}  contains both an  A ∩ R k − 1 A\cap R_{k-1}  term and an  A ∩ S k − 1 A\cap S_{k-1}  term, then a naive inductive argument fails. We refer to such formulas as  mixed  recursive structure formulas. They have either two or four terms 
 

 
 
 A ∩ S k = \displaystyle A\cap S_{k}= 
 ( ( A σ ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ 00 ) ⊔ ( ( A 11 ′ ∩ R k − 1 ) ⊗ 11 ) \displaystyle\left((A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes 00\right)\sqcup\left((A_{11}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1})\otimes 11\right) 
 
 (49) 
 
 
 A ∩ S k = \displaystyle A\cap S_{k}= 
 ( ( A 00 ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ 00 ) ⊔ ( ( A 01 ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ 01 ) ⊔ ( ( A 10 ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ 10 ) ⊔ ( ( A 11 ′ ∩ R k − 1 ) ⊗ 11 ) . \displaystyle\left((A_{00}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes 00\right)\sqcup\left((A_{01}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes 01\right)\sqcup\left((A_{10}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes 10\right)\sqcup\left((A_{11}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1})\otimes 11\right). 
 
 (50) 
 
 
 
 These recursive structure formulas do not cause issues in the proof of Lemma  E.3  where we work around the challenge by exploiting the fact that when  dim A = 2 ​ k − 1 \dim A=2k-1  not all affine subspaces on the right hand side are distinct. To face a more general situation below, we will exploit hidden affine structures in certain combinations of sets of the form  A ∩ S k − 1 A\cap S_{k-1}  and  A ∩ R k − 1 A\cap R_{k-1}  that arise from the cancellation of quadratic terms in the indicator functions of these sets as they are combined together. 
 
 
 Let  W W  be a linear subspace of  𝔽 2 2 ​ k \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k} . If a function  f f  from  W W  to the underlying field of scalars  𝔽 2 \mathbb{F}_{2}  is linear, then we will refer to  f f  as a  linear functional . Let  a ∈ 𝔽 2 2 ​ k a\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k}  and let  A := a + W A:=a+W  be an affine subspace of  𝔽 2 2 ​ k \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k} . If a function  g g  from  A A  to the underlying field of scalars  𝔽 2 \mathbb{F}_{2}  can be written as  g ⁡ ( a + w ) = c + f ⁡ ( w ) g(a+w)=c+f(w)  for some  c ∈ 𝔽 2 c\in\mathbb{F}_{2}  and some linear functional  f f , then we will refer to  g g  as an  affine functional . The significance of affine functionals to our arguments derives from the way they partition their domain. If  g g  is a non-constant affine functional, then  f f  is a non-constant linear functional and  f − 1 ​ ( 1 ) f^{-1}(1)  is a coset of  ker ⁡ f = f − 1 ​ ( 0 ) \ker f=f^{-1}(0) . Therefore,  | f − 1 ​ ( 0 ) | = | f − 1 ​ ( 1 ) | |f^{-1}(0)|=|f^{-1}(1)| . But then also  | g − 1 ​ ( 0 ) | = | g − 1 ​ ( 1 ) | |g^{-1}(0)|=|g^{-1}(1)| . Thus, if  g : A → 𝔽 2 g:A\to\mathbb{F}_{2}  is an affine functional, then each  g − 1 ​ ( 0 ) g^{-1}(0)  and  g − 1 ​ ( 1 ) g^{-1}(1)  is either empty or affine. Moreover, if both  g − 1 ​ ( 0 ) g^{-1}(0)  and  g − 1 ​ ( 1 ) g^{-1}(1)  are non-empty, then  dim g − 1 ​ ( 0 ) = dim g − 1 ​ ( 1 ) = dim A − 1 \dim g^{-1}(0)=\dim g^{-1}(1)=\dim A-1 . 
 
 
 In order to express the value of the indicator function  𝟙 S k : 𝔽 2 2 ​ k → 𝔽 2 \mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}  of  S k S_{k} 
 

 
 
 𝟙 S k ​ ( x ) := ∑ i = 1 k x i ​ x k + i = ⟨ π L ​ ( x ) , π R ​ ( x ) ⟩ \displaystyle\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(x):=\sum_{i=1}^{k}x_{i}x_{k+i}=\langle\pi_{L}(x),\pi_{R}(x)\rangle 
 
 (51) 
 
 on bit strings in an affine space  A := a + W A:=a+W , we introduce the (skew-)symmetric bilinear form  ω : 𝔽 2 2 ​ k → 𝔽 2 \omega:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k}\to\mathbb{F}_{2} 
 

 
 
 ω ⁡ ( x , y ) := ∑ i = 1 k ( x i ​ y k + i + x k + i ​ y i ) = ⟨ π L ​ ( x ) , π R ​ ( y ) ⟩ + ⟨ π L ​ ( y ) , π R ​ ( x ) ⟩ \displaystyle\omega(x,y):=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(x_{i}y_{k+i}+x_{k+i}y_{i}\right)=\langle\pi_{L}(x),\pi_{R}(y)\rangle+\langle\pi_{L}(y),\pi_{R}(x)\rangle 
 
 (52) 
 
 where  π L , π R : 𝔽 2 2 ​ k → 𝔽 2 k \pi_{L},\pi_{R}:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}  are linear projectors defined via 
 

 
 
 π L ​ ( a ​ b ) = a , π R ​ ( a ​ b ) = b \displaystyle\pi_{L}(ab)=a,\quad\pi_{R}(ab)=b 
 
 (53) 
 
 for any  a , b ∈ 𝔽 2 k a,b\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k} . Indeed, we can express  𝟙 S k ​ ( a + x ) \mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+x)  for any  x ∈ W x\in W  in terms of  ω \omega  as 
 

 
 
 𝟙 S k ​ ( a + x ) \displaystyle\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+x) 
 = ⟨ a L + x L , a R + x R ⟩ = 𝟙 S k ​ ( a ) + ω ⁡ ( a , x ) + 𝟙 S k ​ ( x ) \displaystyle=\langle a_{L}+x_{L},a_{R}+x_{R}\rangle=\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a)+\omega(a,x)+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(x) 
 
 (54) 
 
 where  a L := π L ​ ( a ) a_{L}:=\pi_{L}(a) ,  a R := π R ​ ( a ) a_{R}:=\pi_{R}(a) ,  x L := π L ​ ( x ) x_{L}:=\pi_{L}(x) , and  x R := π R ​ ( x ) x_{R}:=\pi_{R}(x) . This way of writing  𝟙 S k \mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}  allows us to construct affine subspaces from sets of the form  A ∩ R k A\cap R_{k}  and  A ∩ S k A\cap S_{k}  by arranging for the cancellation of the quadratic terms  𝟙 S k ​ ( x ) \mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(x) . 
 
 
 We can use these objects and their properties to prove Lemma  III.2  and  E.4  that enable us to express the right hand side of ( 49 ) and ( 50 ) in terms of cardinalities of sets involving  S k − 1 S_{k-1} , but no  R k − 1 R_{k-1} . 
 
 
 Lemma III.2   (Proxy expression for mixed recursive structure formulas with two terms) . 
 
 
 Let  k k  be a positive integer and  a 1 , a 2 ∈ 𝔽 2 2 ​ k a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k} . For any two affine spaces  A 1 := a 1 + W A_{1}:=a_{1}+W  and  A 2 := a 2 + W A_{2}:=a_{2}+W  arising as translations of the same linear subspace  W ⊂ 𝔽 2 2 ​ k W\subset\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k} , there exist two sets  B 1 B_{1}  and  B 2 B_{2}  each of which is either empty or affine and such that 
 

 
 
 | A 1 ∩ R k | + | A 2 ∩ S k | = | B 1 | + 2 ⋅ | B 2 ∩ S k | \displaystyle|A_{1}\cap R_{k}|+|A_{2}\cap S_{k}|=|B_{1}|+2\cdot|B_{2}\cap S_{k}| 
 
 (55) 
 
 and  | B 1 | + | B 2 | = | W | |B_{1}|+|B_{2}|=|W| . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We begin by partitioning  W W  into four disjoint sets 
 

 
 
 W r ​ r \displaystyle W_{rr} 
 : = { x ∈ W | ( a 1 + x ∈ R k ) ∧ ( a 2 + x ∈ R k ) } \displaystyle:=\{x\in W\,|\,(a_{1}+x\in R_{k})\,\land\,(a_{2}+x\in R_{k})\} 
 
 (56) 
 
 
 W r ​ s \displaystyle W_{rs} 
 : = { x ∈ W | ( a 1 + x ∈ R k ) ∧ ( a 2 + x ∈ S k ) } \displaystyle:=\{x\in W\,|\,(a_{1}+x\in R_{k})\,\land\,(a_{2}+x\in S_{k})\} 
 
 (57) 
 
 
 W s ​ r \displaystyle W_{sr} 
 : = { x ∈ W | ( a 1 + x ∈ S k ) ∧ ( a 2 + x ∈ R k ) } \displaystyle:=\{x\in W\,|\,(a_{1}+x\in S_{k})\,\land\,(a_{2}+x\in R_{k})\} 
 
 (58) 
 
 
 W s ​ s \displaystyle W_{ss} 
 : = { x ∈ W | ( a 1 + x ∈ S k ) ∧ ( a 2 + x ∈ S k ) } \displaystyle:=\{x\in W\,|\,(a_{1}+x\in S_{k})\,\land\,(a_{2}+x\in S_{k})\} 
 
 (59) 
 
 so that  W = W r ​ r ⊔ W r ​ s ⊔ W s ​ r ⊔ W s ​ s W=W_{rr}\sqcup W_{rs}\sqcup W_{sr}\sqcup W_{ss} . Consider the sets 
 

 
 
 W 0 := W r ​ r ⊔ W s ​ s W 1 := W r ​ s ⊔ W s ​ r . \displaystyle W_{0}:=W_{rr}\sqcup W_{ss}\quad W_{1}:=W_{rs}\sqcup W_{sr}. 
 
 (60) 
 
 The indicator function of  W 1 W_{1}  restricted to  W W  can be written as 
 

 
 
 𝟙 W 1 ​ ( x ) \displaystyle\mathbb{1}_{W_{1}}(x) 
 = 𝟙 R k ​ ( a 1 + x ) ⋅ 𝟙 S k ​ ( a 2 + x ) + 𝟙 S k ​ ( a 1 + x ) ⋅ 𝟙 R k ​ ( a 2 + x ) \displaystyle=\mathbb{1}_{R_{k}}(a_{1}+x)\cdot\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a_{2}+x)+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a_{1}+x)\cdot\mathbb{1}_{R_{k}}(a_{2}+x) 
 
 (61) 
 
 
 
 = ( 1 + 𝟙 S k ​ ( a 1 + x ) ) ⋅ 𝟙 S k ​ ( a 2 + x ) + 𝟙 S k ​ ( a 1 + x ) ⋅ ( 1 + 𝟙 S k ​ ( a 2 + x ) ) \displaystyle=\left(1+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a_{1}+x)\right)\cdot\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a_{2}+x)+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a_{1}+x)\cdot\left(1+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a_{2}+x)\right) 
 
 (62) 
 
 
 
 = 𝟙 S k ​ ( a 1 + x ) + 𝟙 S k ​ ( a 2 + x ) \displaystyle=\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a_{1}+x)+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a_{2}+x) 
 
 (63) 
 
 
 
 = ω ⁡ ( a 1 + a 2 , x ) + 𝟙 S k ​ ( a 1 ) + 𝟙 S k ​ ( a 2 ) \displaystyle=\omega(a_{1}+a_{2},x)+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a_{1})+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a_{2}) 
 
 (64) 
 
 where we dropped the terms  𝟙 S k ​ ( a 1 + x ) ⋅ 𝟙 S k ​ ( a 2 + x ) \mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a_{1}+x)\cdot\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a_{2}+x)  and  𝟙 S k ​ ( x ) \mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(x) , because  𝔽 2 \mathbb{F}_{2}  has characteristic  2 2 . But  ω \omega  is bilinear, so  𝟙 W 1 \mathbb{1}_{W_{1}}  restricted to  W W  is an affine functional on  W W . Therefore,  W 1 W_{1}  is either empty or an affine subspace of  W W . Similarly, the indicator function of  W 0 W_{0}  restricted to  W W  is  𝟙 W 0 ​ ( x ) = 𝟙 W 1 ​ ( x ) + 1 \mathbb{1}_{W_{0}}(x)=\mathbb{1}_{W_{1}}(x)+1  and hence also an affine functional on  W W . Therefore,  W 0 W_{0}  is either empty or an affine subspace of  W W . Moreover,  W = W 0 ⊔ W 1 W=W_{0}\sqcup W_{1} , so if both  W 0 W_{0}  and  W 1 W_{1}  are non-empty, then  dim W 0 = dim W 1 = dim W − 1 \dim W_{0}=\dim W_{1}=\dim W-1 .
Translation is bijective, so definitions ( 56 )-( 59 ) imply that 
 

 
 
 | A 1 ∩ R k | + | A 2 ∩ S k | = | W r ​ r | + 2 ⋅ | W r ​ s | + | W s ​ s | . \displaystyle|A_{1}\cap R_{k}|+|A_{2}\cap S_{k}|=|W_{rr}|+2\cdot|W_{rs}|+|W_{ss}|. 
 
 (65) 
 
 Finally, define  B 1 := a 1 + W 0 B_{1}:=a_{1}+W_{0}  and  B 2 := a 2 + W 1 B_{2}:=a_{2}+W_{1} , so that  | B 1 | + | B 2 | = | W 0 | + | W 1 | = | W | |B_{1}|+|B_{2}|=|W_{0}|+|W_{1}|=|W|  and rewrite ( 65 ) as 
 

 
 
 | A 1 ∩ R k | + | A 2 ∩ S k | = | B 1 | + 2 ⋅ | B 2 ∩ S k | \displaystyle|A_{1}\cap R_{k}|+|A_{2}\cap S_{k}|=|B_{1}|+2\cdot|B_{2}\cap S_{k}| 
 
 (66) 
 
 where we used  | B 1 | = | W r ​ r | + | W s ​ s | |B_{1}|=|W_{rr}|+|W_{ss}|  and  | W r ​ s | = | B 2 ∩ S k | |W_{rs}|=|B_{2}\cap S_{k}| .
∎ 
 
 
 
 In order to facilitate the use of Lemma  III.2  in an inductive proof, we note the following conditional upper bound it implies. 
 
 
 Corollary III.1   (Bound for mixed recursive structure formulas with two terms) . 
 
 
 Let  e e  and  k > 1 k>1  be positive integers. If  | B ∩ S k − 1 | ≤ 2 dim B − 1 + 2 k − 3 |B\cap S_{k-1}|\leq 2^{\dim B-1}+2^{k-3}  for every affine subspace  B B  of  𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2}  with  dim B ∈ { e − 1 , e } \dim B\in\{e-1,e\} , then 
 

 
 
 | A 1 ∩ R k − 1 | + | A 2 ∩ S k − 1 | ≤ 2 e + 2 k − 2 \displaystyle|A_{1}\cap R_{k-1}|+|A_{2}\cap S_{k-1}|\leq 2^{e}+2^{k-2} 
 
 (67) 
 
 for every pair of  e e -dimensional affine subspaces  A 1 := a 1 + W A_{1}:=a_{1}+W  and  A 2 := a 2 + W A_{2}:=a_{2}+W  of  𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2}  that arise as translations of the same linear subspace  W W  of  𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 By Lemma  III.2 , there exist two sets  B 1 B_{1}  and  B 2 B_{2} , each of which is either empty or affine and such that 
 

 
 
 | A 1 ∩ R k − 1 | + | A 2 ∩ S k − 1 | = | B 1 | + 2 ⋅ | B 2 ∩ S k − 1 | \displaystyle|A_{1}\cap R_{k-1}|+|A_{2}\cap S_{k-1}|=|B_{1}|+2\cdot|B_{2}\cap S_{k-1}| 
 
 (68) 
 
 and  | B 1 | + | B 2 | = | W | |B_{1}|+|B_{2}|=|W| . If  B 1 = ∅ B_{1}=\emptyset , then  B 2 B_{2}  is an affine subspace of  𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2}  of dimension  e e . Therefore, 
 

 
 
 | A 1 ∩ R k − 1 | + | A 2 ∩ S k − 1 | ≤ 0 + 2 ⋅ ( 2 e − 1 + 2 k − 3 ) = 2 e + 2 k − 2 . \displaystyle|A_{1}\cap R_{k-1}|+|A_{2}\cap S_{k-1}|\leq 0+2\cdot(2^{e-1}+2^{k-3})=2^{e}+2^{k-2}. 
 
 (69) 
 
 If  B 2 = ∅ B_{2}=\emptyset , then  B 1 B_{1}  is an affine subspace of  𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2}  of dimension  e e . Therefore, 
 

 
 
 | A 1 ∩ R k − 1 | + | A 2 ∩ S k − 1 | ≤ 2 e + 2 ⋅ 0 < 2 e + 2 k − 2 . \displaystyle|A_{1}\cap R_{k-1}|+|A_{2}\cap S_{k-1}|\leq 2^{e}+2\cdot 0<2^{e}+2^{k-2}. 
 
 (70) 
 
 Finally, if neither  B 1 B_{1}  nor  B 2 B_{2}  is empty, then both are affine and  dim B 1 = dim B 2 = e − 1 \dim B_{1}=\dim B_{2}=e-1 . Therefore, 
 

 
 
 | A 1 ∩ R k − 1 | + | A 2 ∩ S k − 1 | ≤ 2 e − 1 + 2 ⋅ ( 2 e − 2 + 2 k − 3 ) = 2 e + 2 k − 2 \displaystyle|A_{1}\cap R_{k-1}|+|A_{2}\cap S_{k-1}|\leq 2^{e-1}+2\cdot(2^{e-2}+2^{k-3})=2^{e}+2^{k-2} 
 
 (71) 
 
 completing the proof of the Corollary.
∎ 
 
 
 
 A similar, although technically more complicated, proof establishes an analogous result for mixed recursive formulas with four terms, see Lemma  E.4  and Corollary  E.2 . Equipped with Corollary  III.1  above for dealing with two-term mixed recursive structure formulas and Corollary  E.2  for dealing with four-term mixed recursive structure formulas, we are now ready to prove the upper bounds on  | A ∩ R k | |A\cap R_{k}|  and  | A ∩ S k | |A\cap S_{k}|  for affine spaces with  k ≤ dim A < 2 ​ k − 1 k\leq\dim A<2k-1 . 
 
 
 Lemma III.3   (Upper bound on  | A ∩ R k | |A\cap R_{k}|  and  | A ∩ S k | |A\cap S_{k}|  when  k ≤ dim A < 2 ​ k − 1 k\leq\dim A<2k-1 ) . 
 
 
 Let  k , d k,d  be two positive integers and let  A ⊂ 𝔽 2 2 ​ k A\subset\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k}  be a  d d -dimensional affine subspace of  𝔽 2 2 ​ k \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k} . If  k ≤ d < 2 ​ k − 1 k\leq d<2k-1 , then 
 

 
 
 | A ∩ R k | \displaystyle|A\cap R_{k}| 
 ≤ 2 d − 1 + 2 k − 1 \displaystyle\leq 2^{d-1}+2^{k-1} 
 
 (72) 
 
 
 | A ∩ S k | \displaystyle|A\cap S_{k}| 
 ≤ 2 d − 1 + 2 k − 2 . \displaystyle\leq 2^{d-1}+2^{k-2}. 
 
 (73) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We prove the bounds by induction on  k k . If  k = 1 k=1 , then there are no affine subspaces of dimension  1 ≤ dim A < 1 1\leq\dim A<1 , so the upper bounds are vacuously satisfied. Fix  k > 1 k>1  and suppose that for any affine subspace  B ⊂ 𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 B\subset\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2}  whose dimension  e := dim B e:=\dim B  satisfies  k − 1 ≤ e < 2 ​ k − 3 k-1\leq e<2k-3 , it is the case that 
 

 
 
 | B ∩ R k − 1 | \displaystyle|B\cap R_{k-1}| 
 ≤ 2 e − 1 + 2 k − 2 \displaystyle\leq 2^{e-1}+2^{k-2} 
 
 (74) 
 
 
 | B ∩ S k − 1 | \displaystyle|B\cap S_{k-1}| 
 ≤ 2 e − 1 + 2 k − 3 . \displaystyle\leq 2^{e-1}+2^{k-3}. 
 
 (75) 
 
 We will also use ( 74 ) and ( 75 ) when  e = 2 ​ k − 3 e=2k-3  and  e = 2 ​ k − 2 e=2k-2  which follow from Lemma  E.3  and Corollary  E.1 , respectively. We will show that for every affine subspace  A ⊂ 𝔽 2 2 ​ k A\subset\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k}  of dimension  d = dim A d=\dim A  with  k ≤ d < 2 ​ k − 1 k\leq d<2k-1 , we have 
 

 
 
 | A ∩ R k | \displaystyle|A\cap R_{k}| 
 ≤ 2 d − 1 + 2 k − 1 \displaystyle\leq 2^{d-1}+2^{k-1} 
 
 (76) 
 
 
 | A ∩ S k | \displaystyle|A\cap S_{k}| 
 ≤ 2 d − 1 + 2 k − 2 . \displaystyle\leq 2^{d-1}+2^{k-2}. 
 
 (77) 
 
 By Remark  III.1 , we have five cases to consider 
 

 
 
 dim F = 0 \displaystyle\dim F=0 
 ∧ 11 ∉ F \displaystyle\quad\land\quad 11\notin F 
 
 (78) 
 
 
 dim F = 0 \displaystyle\dim F=0 
 ∧ 11 ∈ F \displaystyle\quad\land\quad 11\in F 
 
 (79) 
 
 
 dim F = 1 \displaystyle\dim F=1 
 ∧ 11 ∉ F \displaystyle\quad\land\quad 11\notin F 
 
 (80) 
 
 
 dim F = 1 \displaystyle\dim F=1 
 ∧ 11 ∈ F \displaystyle\quad\land\quad 11\in F 
 
 (81) 
 
 
 dim F = 2 \displaystyle\dim F=2 
 . 
 
 (82) 
 
 In the first case, where  F = { σ } F=\{\sigma\}  with  σ ≠ 11 \sigma\neq 11 , the recursive structure formulas ( 30 ) and ( 31 ) become 
 

 
 
 A ∩ R k \displaystyle A\cap R_{k} 
 = ( A σ ′ ∩ R k − 1 ) ⊗ σ \displaystyle=(A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1})\otimes\sigma 
 
 (83) 
 
 
 A ∩ S k \displaystyle A\cap S_{k} 
 = ( A σ ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ σ \displaystyle=(A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes\sigma 
 
 (84) 
 
 where the affine subspace  A σ ′ A_{\sigma}^{\prime}  has dimension  dim A σ ′ = d − dim F = d \dim A_{\sigma}^{\prime}=d-\dim F=d . Using the inductive hypothesis, we obtain 
 

 
 
 | A ∩ R k | \displaystyle|A\cap R_{k}| 
 = | A σ ′ ∩ R k − 1 | ≤ 2 d − 1 + 2 k − 2 < 2 d − 1 + 2 k − 1 \displaystyle=|A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1}|\leq 2^{d-1}+2^{k-2}<2^{d-1}+2^{k-1} 
 
 (85) 
 
 
 | A ∩ S k | \displaystyle|A\cap S_{k}| 
 = | A σ ′ ∩ S k − 1 | ≤ 2 d − 1 + 2 k − 3 < 2 d − 1 + 2 k − 2 . \displaystyle=|A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1}|\leq 2^{d-1}+2^{k-3}<2^{d-1}+2^{k-2}. 
 
 (86) 
 
 In the second case, where  F = { 11 } F=\{11\} , the recursive structure formulas ( 30 ) and ( 31 ) become 
 

 
 
 A ∩ R k \displaystyle A\cap R_{k} 
 = ( A 11 ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ σ \displaystyle=(A_{11}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes\sigma 
 
 (87) 
 
 
 A ∩ S k \displaystyle A\cap S_{k} 
 = ( A 11 ′ ∩ R k − 1 ) ⊗ σ \displaystyle=(A_{11}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1})\otimes\sigma 
 
 (88) 
 
 where the affine subspace  A 11 ′ A_{11}^{\prime}  again has dimension  dim A 11 ′ = d − dim F = d \dim A_{11}^{\prime}=d-\dim F=d . Using the inductive hypothesis, we find 
 

 
 
 | A ∩ R k | \displaystyle|A\cap R_{k}| 
 = | A 11 ′ ∩ S k − 1 | ≤ 2 d − 1 + 2 k − 3 < 2 d − 1 + 2 k − 1 \displaystyle=|A_{11}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1}|\leq 2^{d-1}+2^{k-3}<2^{d-1}+2^{k-1} 
 
 (89) 
 
 
 | A ∩ S k | \displaystyle|A\cap S_{k}| 
 = | A 11 ′ ∩ R k − 1 | ≤ 2 d − 1 + 2 k − 2 = 2 d − 1 + 2 k − 2 . \displaystyle=|A_{11}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1}|\leq 2^{d-1}+2^{k-2}=2^{d-1}+2^{k-2}. 
 
 (90) 
 
 In the third case, where  F = { σ , τ } F=\{\sigma,\tau\}  with  σ ≠ 11 \sigma\neq 11 ,  τ ≠ 11 \tau\neq 11  and  σ ≠ τ \sigma\neq\tau , the recursive structure formulas read 
 

 
 
 A ∩ R k \displaystyle A\cap R_{k} 
 = ( ( A σ ′ ∩ R k − 1 ) ⊗ σ ) ⊔ ( ( A τ ′ ∩ R k − 1 ) ⊗ τ ) \displaystyle=((A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1})\otimes\sigma)\sqcup((A_{\tau}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1})\otimes\tau) 
 
 (91) 
 
 
 A ∩ S k \displaystyle A\cap S_{k} 
 = ( ( A σ ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ σ ) ⊔ ( ( A τ ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ τ ) . \displaystyle=((A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes\sigma)\sqcup((A_{\tau}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes\tau). 
 
 (92) 
 
 Using the inductive hypothesis and  dim A σ ′ = dim A τ ′ = d − dim F = d − 1 \dim A_{\sigma}^{\prime}=\dim A_{\tau}^{\prime}=d-\dim F=d-1 , we find 
 

 
 
 | A ∩ R k | \displaystyle|A\cap R_{k}| 
 ≤ 2 ⋅ ( 2 d − 2 + 2 k − 2 ) = 2 d − 1 + 2 k − 1 \displaystyle\leq 2\cdot(2^{d-2}+2^{k-2})=2^{d-1}+2^{k-1} 
 
 (93) 
 
 
 | A ∩ S k | \displaystyle|A\cap S_{k}| 
 ≤ 2 ⋅ ( 2 d − 2 + 2 k − 3 ) = 2 d − 1 + 2 k − 2 . \displaystyle\leq 2\cdot(2^{d-2}+2^{k-3})=2^{d-1}+2^{k-2}. 
 
 (94) 
 
 In the fourth case, where  F = { σ , 11 } F=\{\sigma,11\}  with  σ ≠ 11 \sigma\neq 11 , the recursive structure formulas ( 30 ) and ( 31 ) become 
 

 
 
 A ∩ R k \displaystyle A\cap R_{k} 
 = ( ( A 11 ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ 11 ) ⊔ ( ( A σ ′ ∩ R k − 1 ) ⊗ σ ) \displaystyle=((A_{11}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes 11)\sqcup((A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1})\otimes\sigma) 
 
 (95) 
 
 
 A ∩ S k \displaystyle A\cap S_{k} 
 = ( ( A 11 ′ ∩ R k − 1 ) ⊗ 11 ) ⊔ ( ( A σ ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ σ ) \displaystyle=((A_{11}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1})\otimes 11)\sqcup((A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes\sigma) 
 
 (96) 
 
 where  dim A σ ′ = dim A 11 ′ = d − dim F = d − 1 \dim A_{\sigma}^{\prime}=\dim A_{11}^{\prime}=d-\dim F=d-1 . Consequently, 
 

 
 
 | A ∩ R k | \displaystyle|A\cap R_{k}| 
 = | A 11 ′ ∩ S k − 1 | + | A σ ′ ∩ R k − 1 | \displaystyle=|A_{11}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1}|+|A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1}| 
 
 (97) 
 
 
 | A ∩ S k | \displaystyle|A\cap S_{k}| 
 = | A 11 ′ ∩ R k − 1 | + | A σ ′ ∩ S k − 1 | . \displaystyle=|A_{11}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1}|+|A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1}|. 
 
 (98) 
 
 We obtain the upper bound on  | A ∩ R k | |A\cap R_{k}|  from the inductive hypothesis 
 

 
 
 | A ∩ R k | \displaystyle|A\cap R_{k}| 
 ≤ 2 d − 2 + 2 k − 3 + 2 d − 2 + 2 k − 2 < 2 d − 1 + 2 k − 1 \displaystyle\leq 2^{d-2}+2^{k-3}+2^{d-2}+2^{k-2}<2^{d-1}+2^{k-1} 
 
 (99) 
 
 and the upper bound on  | A ∩ S k | |A\cap S_{k}|  using Corollary  III.1  with  e = d − 1 e=d-1 
 

 
 
 | A ∩ S k | \displaystyle|A\cap S_{k}| 
 = | A 11 ′ ∩ R k − 1 | + | A σ ′ ∩ S k − 1 | ≤ 2 d − 1 + 2 k − 2 \displaystyle=|A_{11}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1}|+|A_{\sigma}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1}|\leq 2^{d-1}+2^{k-2} 
 
 (100) 
 
 where the Corollary’s assumption follows from the inductive hypothesis and Lemma  E.1 . 
 
 
 Finally, in the fifth case, where  F = { 00 , 01 , 10 , 11 } F=\{00,01,10,11\} , the recursive structure formulas ( 30 ) and ( 31 ) become 
 

 
 
 A ∩ R k = \displaystyle A\cap R_{k}= 
 ( ( A 00 ′ ∩ R k − 1 ) ⊗ 00 ) ⊔ ( ( A 01 ′ ∩ R k − 1 ) ⊗ 01 ) ⊔ ( ( A 10 ′ ∩ R k − 1 ) ⊗ 10 ) ⊔ ( ( A 11 ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ 11 ) \displaystyle\left((A_{00}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1})\otimes 00\right)\sqcup\left((A_{01}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1})\otimes 01\right)\sqcup\left((A_{10}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1})\otimes 10\right)\sqcup\left((A_{11}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes 11\right) 
 
 (101) 
 
 
 A ∩ S k = \displaystyle A\cap S_{k}= 
 ( ( A 00 ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ 00 ) ⊔ ( ( A 01 ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ 01 ) ⊔ ( ( A 10 ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ 10 ) ⊔ ( ( A 11 ′ ∩ R k − 1 ) ⊗ 11 ) . \displaystyle\left((A_{00}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes 00\right)\sqcup\left((A_{01}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes 01\right)\sqcup\left((A_{10}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes 10\right)\sqcup\left((A_{11}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1})\otimes 11\right). 
 
 (102) 
 
 There are two possibilities. Either all four affine subspaces  A σ ′ A_{\sigma}^{\prime}  of  𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2}  are distinct or they are not. 
 
 
 Suppose first that the affine subspaces  A σ ′ A_{\sigma}^{\prime}  are not all distinct, so that there are  A 1 ′ A_{1}^{\prime}  and  A 2 ′ A_{2}^{\prime}  such that 
 

 
 
 A 1 ′ := A 11 ′ = A ρ ′ , A 2 ′ := A σ ′ = A τ ′ . \displaystyle A_{1}^{\prime}:=A_{11}^{\prime}=A_{\rho}^{\prime},\quad A_{2}^{\prime}:=A_{\sigma}^{\prime}=A_{\tau}^{\prime}. 
 
 (103) 
 
 In this case, we use the fact that  R k − 1 ⊔ S k − 1 = 𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 R_{k-1}\sqcup S_{k-1}=\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2}  to write 
 

 
 
 | A ∩ R k | \displaystyle|A\cap R_{k}| 
 = | A 1 ′ | + 2 ⋅ | A 2 ′ ∩ R k − 1 | \displaystyle=|A_{1}^{\prime}|+2\cdot|A_{2}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1}| 
 
 (104) 
 
 
 | A ∩ S k | \displaystyle|A\cap S_{k}| 
 = | A 1 ′ | + 2 ⋅ | A 2 ′ ∩ S k − 1 | \displaystyle=|A_{1}^{\prime}|+2\cdot|A_{2}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1}| 
 
 (105) 
 
 which, using our inductive hypothesis and  dim A σ ′ = d − dim F = d − 2 \dim A_{\sigma}^{\prime}=d-\dim F=d-2  for all  σ ∈ 𝔽 2 2 \sigma\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2} , implies 
 

 
 
 | A ∩ R k | \displaystyle|A\cap R_{k}| 
 ≤ 2 d − 2 + 2 ⋅ ( 2 d − 3 + 2 k − 2 ) = 2 d − 1 + 2 k − 1 \displaystyle\leq 2^{d-2}+2\cdot(2^{d-3}+2^{k-2})=2^{d-1}+2^{k-1} 
 
 (106) 
 
 
 | A ∩ S k | \displaystyle|A\cap S_{k}| 
 ≤ 2 d − 2 + 2 ⋅ ( 2 d − 3 + 2 k − 3 ) = 2 d − 1 + 2 k − 2 . \displaystyle\leq 2^{d-2}+2\cdot(2^{d-3}+2^{k-3})=2^{d-1}+2^{k-2}. 
 
 (107) 
 
 
 
 Suppose now that the four affine subspaces  A σ ′ A_{\sigma}^{\prime}  of  𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2}  are all distinct. By Lemma  III.1 , the subspaces arise as translations of the same linear subspace  W ′ ⊂ 𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 W^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2}  of dimension  dim W ′ = d − dim F = d − 2 \dim W^{\prime}=d-\dim F=d-2 . 
 
 
 By combining  A 00 ′ A_{00}^{\prime}  and  A 01 ′ A_{01}^{\prime}  into  ( d − 1 ) (d-1) -dimensional affine space  A 00 , 01 ′ A_{00,01}^{\prime} , we can rewrite ( 101 ) as 
 

 
 
 A ∩ R k = \displaystyle A\cap R_{k}= 
 ( ( A 00 , 01 ′ ∩ R k − 1 ) ⊗ 00 ) ⊔ ( ( A 10 ′ ∩ R k − 1 ) ⊗ 10 ) ⊔ ( ( A 11 ′ ∩ S k − 1 ) ⊗ 11 ) \displaystyle\left((A_{00,01}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1})\otimes 00\right)\sqcup\left((A_{10}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1})\otimes 10\right)\sqcup\left((A_{11}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1})\otimes 11\right) 
 
 (108) 
 
 which leads to 
 

 
 
 | A ∩ R k | = | A 00 , 01 ′ ∩ R k − 1 | + | A 10 ′ ∩ R k − 1 | + | A 11 ′ ∩ S k − 1 | . \displaystyle|A\cap R_{k}|=|A_{00,01}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1}|+|A_{10}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1}|+|A_{11}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1}|. 
 
 (109) 
 
 If  d = k d=k , then  | A ∩ R k | ≤ 2 d − 1 + 2 k − 1 = 2 d |A\cap R_{k}|\leq 2^{d-1}+2^{k-1}=2^{d} . For affine spaces with  d > k d>k , we prove the upper bound on  | A ∩ R k | |A\cap R_{k}|  using Corollary  III.1  with  e = d − 2 e=d-2 
 

 
 
 | A ∩ R k | \displaystyle|A\cap R_{k}| 
 ≤ 2 d − 2 + 2 k − 2 + 2 d − 2 + 2 k − 2 = 2 d − 1 + 2 k − 1 \displaystyle\leq 2^{d-2}+2^{k-2}+2^{d-2}+2^{k-2}=2^{d-1}+2^{k-1} 
 
 (110) 
 
 where the Corollary’s assumption follows from the inductive hypothesis and Lemma  E.1 . The upper bound on  | A ∩ S k | |A\cap S_{k}|  follows from Corollary  E.2  with  e = d − 2 e=d-2 
 

 
 
 | A ∩ S k | \displaystyle|A\cap S_{k}| 
 = | A 00 ′ ∩ S k − 1 | + | A 01 ′ ∩ S k − 1 | + | A 10 ′ ∩ S k − 1 | + | A 11 ′ ∩ R k − 1 | ≤ 2 d − 1 + 2 k − 2 \displaystyle=|A_{00}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1}|+|A_{01}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1}|+|A_{10}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1}|+|A_{11}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1}|\leq 2^{d-1}+2^{k-2} 
 
 (111) 
 
 where the Corollary’s assumption follows from the inductive hypothesis and Lemma  E.1 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Theorem III.4 . 
 
 For any positive integer  k k  and any affine subspace  A ⊂ 𝔽 2 2 ​ k A\subset\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k}  of dimension  d = dim A d=\dim A , we have 
 

 
 
 | A ∩ S k | ≤ { 2 d d < k 2 d − 1 + 2 k − 2 k ≤ d < 2 ​ k − 1 2 2 ​ k − 2 d = 2 ​ k − 1 2 2 ​ k − 1 − 2 k − 1 d = 2 ​ k |A\cap S_{k}|\leq\begin{cases}2^{d}&d<k\\
2^{d-1}+2^{k-2}&k\leq d<2k-1\\
2^{2k-2}&d=2k-1\\
2^{2k-1}-2^{k-1}&d=2k\end{cases} 
 
 (112) 
 
 where 
 

 
 
 S k := { ( x 1 , … , x 2 ​ k ) ∈ 𝔽 2 2 ​ k : ∑ i = 1 k x i ​ x i + k = 1 mod 2 } . S_{k}:=\left\{(x_{1},...,x_{2k})\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k}:\sum_{i=1}^{k}x_{i}x_{i+k}=1\mod 2\right\}. 
 
 (113) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The four upper bounds in ( 112 ) follow from Lemma  E.1 , Lemma  III.3 , Lemma  E.3 , and Corollary  E.1 , respectively.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 III.3  Asymptotic Classical Hardness of Twisted Bent Target OPI 

 
 Definition III.5 . 
 
 Let  R , μ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) R,\mu\in(0,1)  be constants. For each positive integer  k k , we define the Twisted Bent Target OPI (TBT-OPI) problem as follows. let  S k S_{k}  be the set of eq ( 113 ).
For each of the  m = 2 2 ​ k − 1 m=2^{2k}-1  evaluation points  α ∈ 𝔽 2 2 ​ k ∗ \alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{2^{2k}}^{*} , let  A ∼ GL 2 ​ k ​ ( 𝔽 2 ) A\sim\mathrm{GL}_{2k}(\mathbb{F}_{2})  be a random independent invertible matrix and set 
 

 
 
 F α = ϕ − 1 ​ ( A ​ ϕ ​ ( x ) ) , F_{\alpha}=\phi^{-1}(A\phi(x)), 
 
 (114) 
 
 where  ϕ \phi  is the bijection of eq ( 20 ).
The  ( R , μ ) (R,\mu) -TBT-OPI problem is to find a polynomial  f ⁡ ( X ) f(X)  of degree at most  ⌈ R ⋅ m ⌉ \lceil R\cdot m\rceil  such that  f ⁡ ( α ) ∈ F α f(\alpha)\in F_{\alpha}  for at least  ⌊ μ ⋅ m ⌋ \lfloor\mu\cdot m\rfloor  distinct  α \alpha . 
 
 
 
 Lemma III.6 . 
 
 The TBT-OPI sets  F α F_{\alpha}  are efficiently constructible per requirement (2) of  IV.2 . 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Note that if  A = I A=I , then applying a phase to  S k S_{k}  can be achieved with a single layer of transversal CZ gates. We can then change the basis to account for  A A  by conjugating this with a circuit of  O ​ ( k 2 ) = O ~ ​ ( 1 ) O(k^{2})=\widetilde{O}(1)  CNOT gates.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 Theorem III.7 . 
 
 There exists a choice of constant  R ≈ 1 / 10 R\approx 1/10  so that, letting  μ = μ D ​ Q ​ I := 1 2 + R 2 ​ ( 1 − R 2 ) \mu=\mu_{DQI}:=\frac{1}{2}+\sqrt{\frac{R}{2}\left(1-\frac{R}{2}\right)} , the number of trials of XP required to solve  ( R , μ ) (R,\mu) -TBT-OPI (definition  III.5 ) is at least  exp ⁡ ( 0.02 ​ m ) \exp(0.02m) . 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The number of trials that XP needs to beat DQI is  1 γ \frac{1}{\gamma}  where  γ \gamma  is the objective function of ( ). This objective function is upper bounded through a direct application the Hoeffding inequality  [ 41 ]  by ( 115 ):

 IV  Asymptotically Optimal Quantum Speedup 

 
 To achieve the speedup of Theorem  I.1 , we have to use methods for the circuit implementation that are not necessarily optimal at the smaller problem sizes we envision for near-term applications. We describe these alternative implementations in  Section   IV.1 . Then in  Section   IV.2  we observe  Theorem   I.1  as a corollary of this implementation (on the quantum side) along with the lower bounds on runtime of XP that was shown in  Theorem   III.7 . 
 
 
 IV.1  Asymptotically Optimal Implementation 

 
 While  Section   II  focused on optimizing constant factors for finite-size implementations, we now analyze the asymptotic complexity of DQI applied to the OPI problem. We demonstrate that under certain conditions, the DQI algorithm can be implemented using a quantum circuit with a number of gates that is nearly linear in the problem size  m m . We use the notation  O ~ ​ ( m ) \widetilde{O}(m)  to hide factors polylogarithmic in  m m . 
 
 
 We first establish the complexity of the key algebraic subroutines required. These complexities are well-established in classical computer algebra and apply over arbitrary finite fields  𝔽 q \mathbb{F}_{q} . 
 
 
 Lemma IV.1   (Fast Algebraic Subroutines) . 
 
 Let  m m  be the problem size parameter. The following tasks can be computed reversibly using  O ~ ​ ( m ) \widetilde{O}(m)  field operations in  𝔽 q \mathbb{F}_{q} : 
 
 1.  
 
 Multipoint Polynomial Evaluation (MPE): Evaluating a polynomial of degree  < m <m  at  m m  points. 
 
 2.  
 
 Reed-Solomon (RS) Decoding: Decoding an RS code of length  m m  up to half its minimum distance. 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 
 
 
 1.  
 
 Fast algorithms for MPE utilize a divide-and-conquer strategy. The complexity is  O ⁡ ( M ⁡ ( m ) ​ log ⁡ m ) O(M(m)\log m)  field operations, where  M ⁡ ( m ) M(m)  is the cost to multiply two degree  m m  polynomials  [ 42 , Chapter 10] . Since  M ​ ( m ) = O ~ ​ ( m ) M(m)=\widetilde{O}(m)  using fast multiplication algorithms  [ 43 ,  44 ,  45 ] , the total cost is  O ~ ​ ( m ) \widetilde{O}(m)  field operations. 
 
 2.  
 
 The key equation for RS decoding can be solved using the Fast Extended Euclidean Algorithm (Fast EEA), which runs in  O ​ ( M ​ ( m ) ​ log ⁡ m ) = O ~ ​ ( m ) O(M(m)\log m)=\widetilde{O}(m)  field operations  [ 42 , Chapter 11] . Subsequent steps (root finding and error evaluation) also take  O ~ ​ ( m ) \widetilde{O}(m)  field operations using fast MPE  [ 46 ] . 
 
 
 
 
 These algorithms are algebraic and can be implemented reversibly on a quantum computer.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 We now analyze the overall complexity of DQI. The result depends critically on the efficiency of the objective function implementation and the size of the field relative to  m m . 
 
 
 Theorem IV.2 . 
 
 Consider the Optimal Polynomial Intersection (OPI) problem over  𝔽 q \mathbb{F}_{q}  with  m m  constraints and  n = R ​ m n=Rm  variables (where  R ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) R\in(0,1)  is constant). The DQI algorithm can be implemented by a quantum circuit using  O ~ ​ ( m ) \widetilde{O}(m)  elementary quantum gates, provided that: 
 
 1.  
 
 The field size  q q  satisfies  log ⁡ q = O ⁡ ( polylog ⁡ ( m ) ) \log q=O(\mathrm{polylog}(m))  (i.e., the bit length  b = O ~ ​ ( 1 ) b=\widetilde{O}(1) ). 
 
 2.  
 
 The subsets  F i F_{i}  are structured such that an oracle  U i U_{i}  for objective function  f i f_{i} , defined as  U i ​ | x ⟩ = f i ​ ( x ) ​ | x ⟩ U_{i}\ket{x}=f_{i}(x)\ket{x} , can be implemented by a quantum circuit using  O ~ ​ ( 1 ) \widetilde{O}(1)  gates. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We analyze the complexity of the DQI algorithm stages (as described in  Section   II.1 ). The conditions ensure that basic arithmetic operations (addition, multiplication) in  𝔽 q \mathbb{F}_{q}  require  O ~ ​ ( b ) = O ~ ​ ( 1 ) \widetilde{O}(b)=\widetilde{O}(1)  gates. The degree of the enhancing polynomial is  l = O ⁡ ( n ) = O ⁡ ( m ) l=O(n)=O(m) . 
 
 
 
 •  
 
 Stage 1: Dicke State Preparation. 
Standard dense Dicke state preparation requires  O ⁡ ( l ​ m ) = O ⁡ ( m 2 ) O(lm)=O(m^{2})  gates  [ 47 ] .
To achieve near linear time, we describe a new Dicke State Preparation method in  Section   B.2 , which employs a reversible implementation of a Divide and Conquer unranking strategy, accelerated by Binary Splitting. As analyzed in the appendix, the total gate complexity for this step is  O ~ ​ ( m ) \widetilde{O}(m) . 
 
 •  
 
 Stage 2: Syndrome Computation. 
We analyze the complexity of the sequential approach described in  Section   II.1 , but using dense Dicke States instead of sparse Dicke States.
This stage iterates  m m  times. Below, we show that the cost of each iteration is  O ~ ​ ( 1 ) \widetilde{O}(1)  resulting in a total cost of  O ~ ​ ( m ) \widetilde{O}(m)  : 
 
 
 
 –  
 
 Constraint Encoding and Error Generation (Apply  G i G_{i} ):  The gate  G i G_{i}  can be implemented using QFTs over  𝔽 q \mathbb{F}_{q}  and one call to the oracle  U i U_{i}   [ 4 , Section 14.4] . The cost of the QFT is  O ~ ​ ( b ) = O ~ ​ ( 1 ) \widetilde{O}(b)=\widetilde{O}(1) . By assumption, the cost of  U i U_{i}  is  O ~ ​ ( 1 ) \widetilde{O}(1) . Thus, the cost of  G i G_{i}  is  O ~ ​ ( 1 ) \widetilde{O}(1) . 
 
 –  
 
 Syndrome Update:  We update the syndrome register with  B i T ⋅ e i B^{T}_{i}\cdot e_{i} . This involves one field multiplication and addition, costing  O ~ ​ ( 1 ) \widetilde{O}(1)  gates. 
 
 
 
 •  
 
 Stage 3: Reversible Decoding. 
We reversibly decode the Reed-Solomon code  C ⟂ C^{\perp} . By Lemma  IV.1 , this requires  O ~ ​ ( m ) \widetilde{O}(m)  field operations, resulting in  O ~ ​ ( m ⋅ b ) = O ~ ​ ( m ) \widetilde{O}(m\cdot b)=\widetilde{O}(m)  gates. 
 
 •  
 
 Stage 4: Final Transformation (IQFT). 
Applying the inverse QFT over  𝔽 q n \mathbb{F}_{q}^{n} . This takes  O ~ ​ ( n ⋅ b ) = O ~ ​ ( m ) \widetilde{O}(n\cdot b)=\widetilde{O}(m)  gates. 
 
 
 
 
 Total Complexity: 
Since each stage requires  O ~ ​ ( m ) \widetilde{O}(m)  gates, the total gate complexity of the DQI algorithm under the stated conditions is  O ~ ​ ( m ) \widetilde{O}(m) .
∎ 
 
 
 
 The conditions required for  Theorem   IV.2  are met, for instance, when  q q  is small (e.g., the binary extension fields analyzed in this paper, provided  b b  is polylogarithmic in  m m ) and when the OPI instance has some special structure that allows for efficient constraint encoding. For example, in the Polynomial Approximation problem, the subsets  F i F_{i}  are contiguous intervals  [ 48 ] , allowing  U i U_{i}  to be implemented efficiently via comparators in  O ~ ​ ( b ) \widetilde{O}(b)  gates. 
 
 
 It is important to contrast this with the general OPI case studied in Section 5 of  [ 4 ] , where  m ≈ q m\approx q  and the subsets  F i F_{i}  are arbitrary. 
 
 
 Corollary IV.1 . 
 
 Consider the general OPI problem over  𝔽 q \mathbb{F}_{q}  where  m = q − 1 m=q-1  and the subsets  F i F_{i}  are arbitrary (e.g., the balanced case where  | F i | ≈ q / 2 |F_{i}|\approx q/2 ). The DQI algorithm requires  O ~ ​ ( m 2 ) \widetilde{O}(m^{2})  elementary quantum gates. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 In this case,  b = O ​ ( log ⁡ m ) = O ~ ​ ( 1 ) b=O(\log m)=\widetilde{O}(1) . However, implementing the oracle  U i U_{i}  or the gate  G i G_{i}  for an arbitrary function defined by a subset of size  O ⁡ ( q ) O(q)  generally requires  O ~ ​ ( q ) = O ~ ​ ( m ) \widetilde{O}(q)=\widetilde{O}(m)  gates (e.g., using QROM or generic state preparation  [ 27 ] ). The total cost of Stage 2 (Constraint Encoding) becomes  m ⋅ O ~ ​ ( m ) = O ~ ​ ( m 2 ) m\cdot\widetilde{O}(m)=\widetilde{O}(m^{2}) , dominating the overall complexity.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 IV.2  Asymptotic Optimality 

 
 Combining  Theorem   IV.2  and  Theorem   III.7  we have  Theorem   I.1 :
See  I.1 
Since any quantum circuit of  n n  gates can be simulated in time  O ⁡ ( 2 n ) O(2^{n}) , our speedup provides the largest separation possible between classical and quantum runtimes.
This is somewhat extraordinary since OPI is essentially a contrived problem and seems (to us) no more “quantum" or “natural" than e.g. factoring or period-finding.
We speculate informally on the outlook for this situation below. 
 
 •  
 
 Faster Classical Algorithms for OPI:  Although the best attacks we are currently aware of for OPI take exponential time in the number of evaluation points, it is certainly possible that better algorithms exist. It is interesting to note that none of the algorithms we know about leverage the low-degree algebraic structure and instead treat it as an arbitrary max-LINSAT problem. 
 
 •  
 
 Better Shor circuits:  Assuming the speedup for OPI stands, we wonder if further ideas along the line of thinking of  [ 5 ,  49 ]  could culminate in an asymptotically-optimal speedup based on Shor’s algorithm. 
 
 •  
 
 Peeling off log factors:  Although we omit the polylogarithmic factors here, there is room to optimize them and we wonder whether such effort would reveal a more practically-usable speedup or merely complicate matters without much gain at useful problem scales. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 V  Resource Estimates 

 
 V.1  Logical Costs 

 
 To provide a concrete assessment of the resources required to solve an instance of OPI using DQI, we performed a detailed logical cost analysis of the complete, end-to-end algorithm.
We implemented the key subroutines—including the RS Decoders using the Synchronized and Dialog based EEA methods (as detailed in  Section   II.2.2  and  Section   II.2.3 ), and the Sparse Dicke State Preparation from  Appendix   B ; as a  Bloq  in the Qualtran quantum compilation framework  [ 19 ]  and used it to analyze the precise qubit and gate costs. 
 
 
 The results of this analysis are summarized in  Table   6  for several representative instances of the Optimal Polynomial Intersection (OPI) problem. The table presents the total Toffoli and Clifford gate counts, the number of logical qubits required for the reversible decoder, and the estimated classical intractability of each instance.
For  G ​ F ​ ( 2 b ) GF(2^{b}) , since multiplication by a constant field element requires only Clifford gates, for small  n / m n/m  it turns out to be better to spend a higher gate cost to run Zalka’s EEA and have explicit access to the Bézout coefficients such that the Chien search subroutine that evaluates the Bézout coefficient polynomial  σ ⁡ ( x ) \sigma(x)  at  m m  different constants, uses only Clifford gates.
Using Dialogs, the EEA part is significantly cheaper than Zalka’s EEA, but because we only have implicit access to the polynomial  σ ⁡ ( x ) \sigma(x) , each evaluation of  σ ⁡ ( x ) \sigma(x)  and  σ ′ ​ ( x ) \sigma^{\prime}(x)  now requires applying the Dialog and uses quantum-quantum multiplication  [ 8 ]  of field elements instead of quantum-classical multiplication.
For both the approaches, the qubit counts scale as  2 ​ n ​ b + 𝒪 ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ ( n ) ) 2nb+\mathcal{O}(\log_{2}(n)) 
 
 
 These resource estimates become particularly noteworthy when contextualized against other well-studied, classically intractable and verifiable optimization problems. Integer factorization using Shor’s algorithm serves as the canonical benchmark for large-scale quantum computation. As shown in the state-of-the-art estimates in  [ 20 ] , factoring a 2048-bit RSA integer is estimated to require approximately  6.5 × 10 9 6.5\times 10^{9}  Toffoli gates and  1399 1399  logical qubits, while factoring a 1024-bit RSA integer requiers  1.1 × 10 9 1.1\times 10^{9}  Toffoli gates and  742 742  logical qubits. In comparison, several of our OPI instances that are well into the classically intractable regime (requiring  > 10 23 >10^{23}  trials) and exhibit Toffoli counts that are about two to three orders of magnitude smaller, with logical qubit counts that are about two to three times higher.

We do not know the ultimate limits nor do we understand how to choose the set in a principled way.
 
 
 
 
 V.2  Physical Costs 

 
 For estimating physical costs, we assume one large rectangular grid of physical qubits with nearest neighbor connections, a uniform gate error rate of 0.1%, a surface code cycle time of 1 microsecond, and a control system reaction time of 10 microseconds.
With these assumptions, the imagined physical layout of the algorithm will consist of a compute region using hot storage and a memory region using cold storage.
The compute region will store logical qubits “normally", as distance  d d  surface code patches using  2 ​ ( d + 1 ) 2 2(d+1)^{2}  physical qubits.
The cold storage memory region will store qubits more densely, by using 2D yoked surface codes  [ 50 ] .
Since our Toffoli gate counts are low enough, magic state cultivation  [ 51 ]  will suffice to prepare  | T ⟩ \ket{T}  states with low enough error rates, and thus we will not any dedicated space for magic state factories. 
 
 
 As an example, let us look at compiling the instance  ( m = 4095 , n = 70 , b = 12 ) (m=4095,n=70,b=12) .
The dominant subroutines in the decomposition of the circuit are primitives to do arithmetic over Galois Field, like quantum-quantum multiplication ( GF2Mul ) and synthesizing a linear reversible circuit ( SynthesizeLR ) for quantum-classical multiplication and squaring.
For these two primitives, we provide hand optimized lattice surgery layouts in  Fig.   4  and  Fig.   2  with magic state cultivation in-place. 
 
 
 Referring to  [ 20 , Figure 6] , note that a distance 21 patch is sufficient for normal surface code patches to reach a per-patch per-round logical error rate of  10 − 13 10^{-13} .
So our hot patches will use  2 ⋅ ( 21 + 1 ) 2 = 968 2\cdot(21+1)^{2}=968  physical qubits per logical qubit.
For the cold storage, again referring to  [ 20 , Figure 6] , yoking with a 2D parity check code reaches a logical error rate of  10 − 13 10^{-13}  when using  350 350  physical qubits per logical qubit.
So cold logical qubits will be roughly triple the density of hot logical qubits. For  ( m = 4095 , n = 70 , b = 12 ) (m=4095,n=70,b=12) , the algorithm uses  1885 1885  logical qubits.
We propose to allocate a  40 × 48 40\times 48  cold storage region with  1920 1920  logical qubits using 2D Yoked Surface codes.
We also propose a thin  3 × 40 = 120 3\times 40=120  qubit hot storage region.
Our mockup for GF multiplication for  b = 12 b=12  fits in a  3 × ( 2.5 ​ b + 9 ) = 3 × 39 3\times(2.5b+9)=3\times 39  region, including ancilla patches and space needed for cultivation.
Thus, our layout uses  120 × 968 + 1920 × 350 ≈ 800 ​ k 120\times 968+1920\times 350\approx 800k  physical qubits. A mockup of the high-level spacial layout is shown in  Fig.   5 . 
 
 
 Now let’s come to time. In the decomposition of logical circuit for  ( m = 4095 , n = 70 , b = 12 ) (m=4095,n=70,b=12) , there are  58215 58215  calls to  GF2Mul  and  687564 687564  calls to  SynthesizeLR .
We define the Parity Control Toffoli gate as a generalization of the Toffoli gate that computes the Boolean AND of the XOR of a subset of controls and updates a subset of targets  [ 52 , Figure 1] ; we employ such gates since they can be implemented efficiently using lattice surgery  Fig.   3 .
Our  GF2Mul  compilation from  Fig.   4  requires  1.5 ​ d 1.5d  rounds per Parity Control Toffoli gate (PCTOF).
For  b = 12 b=12 , using optimized GF2 arithmetic circuits from  Appendix   C , there are  51 51  PCTOF gates.
Therefore, it takes  1.5 ⋅ 51 ≈ 77 ​ d 1.5\cdot 51\approx 77d  rounds for one quantum-quantum multiplication of field elements.
For  SynthesizeLR , we present a hand-optimized layout in  Fig.   2  that takes  10 ​ d 10d  rounds and  3 × 23 3\times 23  surface code patches for  b = 12 b=12 .
Therefore, with  d = 21 d=21 , these two combined give us  58215 ⋅ 77 ⋅ 21 + 687564 ⋅ 10 ⋅ 21 = 2.4 × 10 8 58215\cdot 77\cdot 21+687564\cdot 10\cdot 21=2.4\times 10^{8} .
To provide a conservative estimate of the total runtime, we multiply this number by a factor of four:
doubling it once to account for overhead from other smaller operations and routing, and doubling it a second time to account for the time needed to move qubits between cold and hot storage. Thus, we estimate a total of  1 × 10 9 1\times 10^{9}  rounds.
We need to protect  1920 + 120 = 2040 1920+120=2040  logical qubits for a total of  1 × 10 9 1\times 10^{9}  rounds.
With an LER of  10 − 13 10^{-13} , this gives us a no-logical-error shot rate of  p lattice surgery = ( 1 − 10 − 13 ) 2040 ⋅ 1 ⋅ 10 9 = ( 1 − 10 − 13 ) 2 × 10 12 ≈ 81.5 % p_{\text{lattice surgery}}=(1-10^{-13})^{2040\cdot 1\cdot 10^{9}}=(1-10^{-13})^{2\times 10^{12}}\approx 81.5\%  and a per shot runtime of   16 16  minutes.
To account for error due to cultivation, we cultivate  | T ⟩ \ket{T}  states with a fidelity of  2 × 10 − 9 2\times 10^{-9} .
For a total Toffoli count of  5.72 × 10 6 5.72\times 10^{6} , this gives us a  p cultivation = ( 1 − 2 × 10 − 9 ) 4 × 5.72 × 10 6 ≈ 95.5 % p_{\text{cultivation}}=(1-2\times 10^{-9})^{4\times 5.72\times 10^{6}}\approx 95.5\% .
Combining the two success probabilities above, we have a per-shot success rate of  p cultivation × p lattice surgery ≈ 77.9 % p_{\text{cultivation}}\times p_{\text{lattice surgery}}\approx 77.9\% , which means about 4 retries are sufficient for boosting the probability of a successful shot close to 1. Therefore, the total runtime would be  ≈ 1 \approx 1  hour. 
 
 
 To summarize, we estimate that solving a classically intractable instance of the OPI problem using DQI requires  ≈ 800 ​ k \approx 800k  physical qubits and  ≈ 1 \approx 1  hour of runtime. This estimate assumes a quantum computer with a surface code cycle time of 1 microsecond, a control system reaction time of 10 microseconds, a square grid of qubits with nearest neighbor connectivity, and a uniform depolarizing noise model with a noise strength of 1 error per 1000 gates.

 VI  Conclusion 

 
 This work establishes Decoded Quantum Interferometry (DQI) applied to the Optimal Polynomial Intersection (OPI) problem as a landmark candidate for practical quantum advantage. From a complexity-theoretic perspective, we have shown that DQI+OPI is the first known proposal for verifiable superpolynomial speedup that achieves optimal asymptotic efficiency, requiring only  O ~ ​ ( N ) \widetilde{O}(N)  quantum gates to solve instances with classical hardness  O ⁡ ( 2 N ) O(2^{N}) . This matches the theoretical lower bound and significantly outperforms the asymptotic scaling of Shor’s algorithm for cryptography. 
 
 
 The realization of this speedup relies on the concrete efficiency of the underlying reversible Reed-Solomon (RS) decoder. We introduced a suite of novel algorithmic and compilation techniques targeting the Extended Euclidean Algorithm (EEA), the critical bottleneck in RS decoding. Our innovations, including the  Dialog  representation for implicit Bézout coefficient access and optimized  in-place register sharing  architectures, rigorously reduce the space complexity to the theoretical minimum of  2 ​ n ​ b 2nb  qubits while substantially lowering gate counts. These techniques are broadly applicable and promise significant improvements for other quantum algorithms reliant on the EEA, particularly those for Elliptic Curve Cryptography. 
 
 
 We provided a comprehensive end-to-end compilation and resource analysis for DQI, focusing on OPI over binary extension fields  G ​ F ​ ( 2 b ) GF(2^{b}) . We analyzed the classical hardness against tailored attacks, such as the Extended Prange algorithm, and identified resilient instances based on bent functions. Our concrete resource estimates demonstrate that DQI can solve instances requiring  > 10 23 >10^{23}  classical trials using approximately 5.72 million Toffoli gates and 1885 logical qubits. This represents a reduction of three orders of magnitude in the gate count compared to breaking RSA-2048.
Furthermore, our physical resource analysis, supported by hand-optimized lattice surgery layouts for key primitives, estimates that such instances could be solved on a fault-tolerant architecture with  800,000 800,000  physical qubits in  ≈ 1 \approx 1  hour of runtime. 
 
 
 While our results position DQI as a compelling pathway for demonstrating quantum advantage in optimization, several avenues for future research remain. 
 
 
 Continued refinement of the classical hardness analysis is crucial. While we have demonstrated resilience against the best known classical attacks in the low-rate approximation regime, further investigation into the impact of sophisticated algebraic attacks ( [ 40 ] ) and establishing formal average-case hardness guarantees remain important open problems. 
 
 
 On the quantum side, there are a few routes to improved performance as well. In  [ 54 ] , a few interesting ideas were proposed. Among these was the idea to use Guruswami-Sudan and Koetter-Vardy list decoding algorithms for RS codes  [ 55 ,  56 ] . This would allow us to find solutions that would take exponential time even with DQI and the Berlekamp-Massey decoder, with the cost of a higher (still polynomial) quantum circuit complexity.
In a concurrent work,  [ 15 ]  considers applying DQI to algebraic geometry codes. These codes have some potentially favorable properties for verifiable quantum advantage demonstrations. Notably, they can maintain a constant rate and relative distance with considerably smaller (and in some cases constant) alphabet sizes than Reed-Solomon codes, which could improve the space footprint of the syndrome registers. 
 
 
 The Dialog representation introduced here opens new theoretical and practical questions regarding linear representations of numbers and polynomials. Key open questions include: 
 
 1.  
 
 Can the size of the Dialog be reduced from  ≈ 2 ​ n \approx 2n  field elements to  ≈ n \approx n  when one of the inputs to the GCD is known classically? 
 
 2.  
 
 Is there an efficient, in-place algorithm for transforming the Dialog for  x x  into the Dialog for  x − 1 x^{-1} , or for computing the Dialog of  x + y x+y  from the Dialogs of  x x  and  y y , without passing through the standard polynomial representation? 
 
 3.  
 
 Are there more efficient linear representations beyond those based on the GCD algorithm, potentially enabling parallelization? 
 
 
 
 
 By bridging complexity theory with concrete algorithmic engineering, this work identifies and enables an asymptotically optimal and practically efficient route toward verifiable quantum advantage. 
 
 
 
 Acknowledgments:  We thank Mary Wootters for helpful conversations about decoding algorithms. We thank Alexandru Gheorghiu for teaching us about bent functions such as in eq ( 23 ) at a Simons workshop organized by Umesh Vazirani.
