 Appendix A  Optimizing the number of trials of Extended Prange 

 
 Recall the optimization problem from  Section   III.2 
 

 
 
 maximize \displaystyle\mathrm{maximize}\,\,\, 
 ℙ ⁡ ( ∑ i = 1 m X i ≥ t ) \displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}X_{i}\geq t\right) 
 
 
 
 subject ​ to \displaystyle\mathrm{subject\,to}\,\,\, 
 X i ∼ Ber ⁡ ( P ⁡ [ s i ] ) \displaystyle X_{i}\sim\mathrm{Ber}(P[s_{i}]) 
 
 
 
 
 ∑ i = 1 m s i ≤ B \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}s_{i}\leq B 
 
 
 
 
 0 ≤ s i ≤ b \displaystyle 0\leq s_{i}\leq b 
 
 
 
 
 ℙ ⁡ ( 1 | s ) ≤ ℙ ⁡ ( 1 | s + 1 ) \displaystyle\mathbb{P}(1|s)\leq\mathbb{P}(1|s+1) 
 
 (118) 
 
 
 
 Eq ( ) is hard to solve exactly for large  m , b , m,b,  and  B B , but using dynamic programming we can compute a tight approximation. To compute this approximation we note that eq ( ) is invariant under permutations of  s i s_{i}  which allows us to build the solution in any order we desire. 
 
 
 Before we describe our approach we note that any constructive that chooses the  s i s_{i}  values iteratively faces the problem of given  s 1 ⋯ s k s_{1}\cdots s_{k}  choose the best  s k + 1 s_{k+1}  which is a hard question since the sequence of  s i s_{i}  induce a probability distribution and the set of probability distributions is intrinsically an unordered set. However, given our objective we can impose a few ordering relations with various degrees of effectiveness. 
 
 
 To solve the problem we employ a knapsack like dynamic programming approach. Dynamic programming is a constructive paradigm and since we are building an approximation algorithm the order we choose  s i s_{i}  affects the quality of the result. Eq ( 120 ) shows that as we construct our solution the joint probability distribution induced by our choices of  s i s_{i}  flows in the direction of increasing  ∑ X i \sum X_{i} . We also note that the change in the joint distribution from  ℙ ( ∙ | s 1 ⋯ s k ) \mathbb{P}(\bullet|s_{1}\cdots s_{k})  to  ℙ ( ∙ | s 1 ⋯ s k s k + 1 ) \mathbb{P}(\bullet|s_{1}\cdots s_{k}s_{k+1})  is controlled by  ℙ ⁡ ( 1 | s k + 1 ) \mathbb{P}(1|s_{k+1})  which from eq ( 118 ) is monotonic, from these two observation we find that it is advantageous to choose the  s i s_{i}  values in descending order  s 1 ≥ s 2 ≥ ⋯ ≥ s m s_{1}\geq s_{2}\geq\cdots\geq s_{m} . The advantage of this ordering is two folds, the first is that after the first few choices of  s i s_{i}  the joint probability distribution stabilizes from one choice to another, the second is computational since it reduces the runtime of the algorithm. 
 
 
 

 
 
 ℙ ( ∑ i = 1 k + 1 X i ≥ t | s 1 ⋯ s k s k + 1 ) = ℙ ( ∑ i = 1 k X i ≥ t | s 1 ⋯ s k ) + ℙ ( 1 | s k + 1 ) ℙ ( ∑ i = 1 k X i = t − 1 | s 1 ⋯ s k ) \mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}X_{i}\geq t|s_{1}\cdots s_{k}s_{k+1}\right)=\\
\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}X_{i}\geq t|s_{1}\cdots s_{k}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(1|s_{k+1}\right)\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}X_{i}=t-1|s_{1}\cdots s_{k}\right)\\
 
 
 (120) 
 
 
 
 Our solution has a dynamic programming state of  ( i , budget , low ) (i,\mathrm{budget},\mathrm{low})  which means for the first  i i  variables and budget  budget ≤ B \mathrm{budget}\leq B  and  ∀ j ≤ i s j ≥ low \forall_{j\leq i}s_{j}\geq\mathrm{low}  what is the best joint probability distribution. This state has only two transitions  ( i , budget , low ) → ( i , budget , low + 1 ) (i,\mathrm{budget},\mathrm{low})\to(i,\mathrm{budget},\mathrm{low}+1)  and  ( i , budget , low ) → ( i − 1 , budget − low , low ) (i,\mathrm{budget},\mathrm{low})\to(i-1,\mathrm{budget}-\mathrm{low},\mathrm{low}) . To compare between the up to two probability distributions we get from these transitions we used two comparison functions. 
 
 
 The first comparison function is eq ( 122 ), which is quick and for most test cases we generated was at most  5 % 5\%  lower than the optimal result with some outliers with a bigger error. The second is eq ( 123 ) which is slower since it involves computing the convolution between the probability distribution induced by our tuple  s 1 ⋯ s k s_{1}\cdots s_{k}  and the one induced by the sequence computed by eq ( 124 ). The intuition behind  F F  which is computed using the classical dynamic programming knapsack algorithm is that we are doing a look ahead trying to estimate the best probability distribution given the remaining budget, but for computational reasons instead of solving the original problem we solve the related easier problem of maximizing the sum of probabilities, this gives us a better lower bound on the best final joint probability distribution starting from the given  S S . Note that, eq ( 122 ) is a special case of eq ( 123 ) since it can be interpreted as the convolution of the current probability distribution with the probability distribution  ℙ ⁡ ( 0 ) = 1 \mathbb{P}(0)=1 . 
 
 
 

 
 
 T ⁡ ( S ) = ( ℙ ⁡ ( ∑ X i ≥ t | S ) , ℙ ⁡ ( ∑ X i = t − 1 | S ) , ℙ ⁡ ( ∑ X i = t − 2 | S ) , ⋯ , ℙ ⁡ ( ∑ X i = 0 | S ) ) T(S)=(\mathbb{P}(\sum X_{i}\geq t|S),\mathbb{P}(\sum X_{i}=t-1|S),\mathbb{P}(\sum X_{i}=t-2|S),\cdots,\mathbb{P}(\sum X_{i}=0|S)) 
 
 (121) 
 
 
 
 

 
 
 S 1 ≥ S 2 ⇔ T ⁡ ( S 1 ) ≥ T ⁡ ( S 2 ) S_{1}\geq S_{2}\iff T(S_{1})\geq T(S_{2}) 
 
 (122) 
 
 
 
 

 
 
 S 1 ≥ S 2 \displaystyle S_{1}\geq S_{2} 
 ⇔ T ⁡ ( S 1 + F ⁡ ( m − k , budget − ∑ s ∈ S 1 s i , s k ( 1 ) ) ≥ T ⁡ ( S 2 + F ⁡ ( m − k , budget − ∑ s ∈ S 2 s i ) , s k ( 2 ) ) CLOSE \displaystyle\iff T(S_{1}+F(m-k,\mathrm{budget}-\sum_{s\in S_{1}}s_{i},\ s_{k}^{(1)})\geq T(S_{2}+F(m-k,\mathrm{budget}-\sum_{s\in S_{2}}s_{i}),\ s_{k}^{(2)}) 
 
 (123) 
 
 
 S 1 + S 2 \displaystyle S_{1}+S_{2} 
 = ( s 1 ( 1 ) , ⋯ , s a ( 1 ) ) + ( s 1 ( 2 ) , ⋯ , s b ( 2 ) ) \displaystyle=(s_{1}^{(1)},\cdots,s_{a}^{(1)})+(s_{1}^{(2)},\cdots,s_{b}^{(2)}) 
 
 
 
 
 = ( s 1 ( 1 ) , ⋯ , s a ( 1 ) , s 1 ( 2 ) , ⋯ , s b ( 2 ) ) \displaystyle=(s_{1}^{(1)},\cdots,s_{a}^{(1)},s_{1}^{(2)},\cdots,s_{b}^{(2)}) 
 
 
 
 
 

 
 
 F ⁡ ( m , budget , low ) \displaystyle F(m,\mathrm{budget},\mathrm{low}) 
 = argmax s 1 ⋯ s m ∑ i = 1 m P [ s i ] \displaystyle=\mathrm{argmax}_{s_{1}\cdots s_{m}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}P[s_{i}] 
 
 
 
 subject ​ to \displaystyle\mathrm{subject\,to} 
 ∑ s i ≤ budget \displaystyle\sum s_{i}\leq\mathrm{budget} 
 
 
 
 and \displaystyle\mathrm{and\ } 
 s i ≥ low \displaystyle s_{i}\geq\mathrm{low} 
 
 (124) 
 
 
 
 We were unable to find a case where the dynamic programming approach with the slow sorting relation eq ( 123 ) disagrees with brute force solution. Although such cases theoretically exist we believe the error will be small. The time complexity of this solution is  𝒪 ⁡ ( m ​ b ​ B ​ t ) \mathcal{O}(mbBt)  with the fast sorting relation and  𝒪 ⁡ ( m ​ b ​ B ​ t 2 ) \mathcal{O}(mbBt^{2})  with the slow sorting relation. 
 
 
 A.1  Upper Bound on the objective function 

 
 The objective function of ( ) is upper bounded through a direct application of the Hoeffding Inequality by 
 
 
 

 
 
 ℙ ⁡ ( ∑ i = 1 m X i ≥ t ) ≤ exp ⁡ ( − 2 ​ ( t − 𝔼 ⁡ [ ∑ i = 1 m X i ] ) 2 m ) \mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}X_{i}\geq t\right)\leq\exp\left(-2\frac{(t-\mathbb{E}[\sum_{i=1}^{m}X_{i}])^{2}}{m}\right) 
 
 (125) 
 
 
 
 This upper bound is minimized by maximizing  𝔼 ⁡ [ ∑ i = 1 m X i ] = ∑ i = 1 m P ⁡ [ s i ] \mathbb{E}[\sum_{i=1}^{m}X_{i}]=\sum_{i=1}^{m}P[s_{i}] . The function  P ⁡ [ s ] P[s]  is derived from ( 112 ) as 
 
 
 

 
 
 P ⁡ [ s ] = { 1 2 − 1 2 k + 1 s = 0 1 2 s = 1 1 2 + 1 2 k − s + 2 1 < s ≤ k 1 s > k P[s]=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}&s=0\\
\frac{1}{2}&s=1\\
\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2^{k-s+2}}&1<s\leq k\\

\end{cases}

 If we let  c j c_{j}  be the number of times  s i = j s_{i}=j  then maximizing the expectation can be written as

 Since the objective function is the expectation of the sum of  m m  Bernoulli variables, its value is a fraction of  m = h ​ m , 0 ≤ h ≤ 1 m=hm,0\leq h\leq 1  which can be substituted into ( 115 ) with  h h  controlling the exponent of ( 116 ). We can compute this expectation using a integer program or for large instances compute an upper bound by allowing  c s c_{s}  to be real rather than integer and solving the linear program. For the specific  P ⁡ [ s ] P[s]  function in ( 126 ) a simple strategy that uses all of the budget to get  s = k + 1 s=k+1  also maximizes the objective. This can be seen as follows.
After spending  s s  points the expectation value is increased by  P ⁡ [ s ] − P ⁡ [ 0 ] P[s]-P[0] . To maximize our total expectation value we should spend in a way that maximizes our gain per unit cost, i.e.  ( P ⁡ [ s ] − P ⁡ [ 0 ] ) / s (P[s]-P[0])/s . A brief analysis shows that this occurs for  s = k + 1 s=k+1 , so the optimal solution to the LP should greedily allocate as much mass as possible onto  p k + 1 p_{k+1} . 
 
 
 Using this greedy approach we get an upper bound on the expectation

 Appendix B  Sparse Dicke State Preparation 

 
 The Dicke state  | D k m ⟩ \ket{D^{m}_{k}}  is an equal weight superposition of all  m m -qubit states with Hamming weight  k k  (i.e. all strings of length  m m  with exactly  k k  ones over a binary alphabet). As shown in  Section   II.1 , to prepare the syndrome register for DQI, we need to prepare the state 
 

 
 
 ∑ k = 0 l w k ​ | D k m ⟩ \sum_{k=0}^{l}w_{k}\ket{D^{m}_{k}} 
 
 
 where  w 0 , w 1 , … , w l − 1 w_{0},w_{1},\dots,w_{l-1}  where  l = n / 2 l=n/2 , are classically known coefficients and  | D k m ⟩ \ket{D^{m}_{k}}  is the Dicke state 
 

 
 
 | D k m ⟩ = 1 ( m k ) ​ ∑ | y | = k | y ⟩ \ket{D^{m}_{k}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{{m}\choose{k}}}\sum_{|y|=k}\ket{y} 
 
 
 
 
 In the context of DQI, the Dicke state encodes a superposition over all possible  ( m k ) {m\choose k}  error locations for  0 ≤ k ≤ l 0\leq k\leq l  errors in a codeword of length  m m . This encoding uses  m m  qubits. However, when  m ≫ l = n / 2 m\gg l=n/2 , we can define a sparse encoding that captures the same information using  l × log 2 ⁡ m l\times\log_{2}{m}  qubits, and thus be more space efficient. 
 
 
 A Sparse Dicke state  | S ​ D k m ⟩ \ket{SD^{m}_{k}}  can be defined as a uniform superposition over all  k − combinations k-\text{combinations}  of indices  [ 1 , 2 , … , m ] [1,2,\dots,m] , stored using  k k  registers each of size  b = ⌈ log 2 ⁡ ( m + 1 ) ⌉ b=\lceil\log_{2}{(m+1)}\rceil , using a total of  k ⋅ b k\cdot b  qubits. 
 
 
 

 
 
 | S ​ D k m ⟩ = 1 ( m k ) ∑ 1 ≤ c 1 < c 2 < ⋯ < c k ≤ n | c 1 ⟩ b | c 2 ⟩ b ⋯ | c k ⟩ b \ket{SD^{m}_{k}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\binom{m}{k}}}\sum_{1\leq c_{1}<c_{2}<\dots<c_{k}\leq n}\ket{c_{1}}_{b}\ket{c_{2}}_{b}\cdots\ket{c_{k}}_{b} 
 
 
 
 
 First, we describe a way to prepare the Sparse Dicke states  | S ​ D k m ⟩ \ket{SD^{m}_{k}}  using the Combinatorial Number System  [ 57 ] . Then, we give two different constructions for quantum circuits to unrank combinations, one that minimizes the asymptotic complexity and other that minimizes the constant factors. 
 
 
 B.1  The unranking strategy 

 
 The Combinatorial Number System  [ 57 ]  defines an ordering over all  ( m k ) \binom{m}{k}   k − k- combinations such that a combination  [ c k , c k − 1 , … , c 1 ] [c_{k},c_{k-1},\dots,c_{1}]  where  n − 1 ≥ c k > c k − 1 > ⋯ > c 1 ≥ 0 n-1\geq c_{k}>c_{k-1}>\dots>c_{1}\geq 0  corresponds to a unique rank  0 ≤ r < ( m k ) 0\leq r<\binom{m}{k}  given by the bijection 
 

 
 
 r = ∑ j = 1 k ( c j j ) r=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\binom{c_{j}}{j} 
 
 
 
 
 Using this bijection, the state preparation strategy involves two steps: 
 
 1.  
 
 Prepare a uniform superposition of all ranks  r ∈ { 0 , … , ( m k ) − 1 } r\in\{0,\dots,\binom{m}{k}-1\}  using  𝒪 ⁡ ( k ⋅ b ) \mathcal{O}(k\cdot b)  gates  [ 30 ] . Here we use the fact that  ( m k ) ≤ m k \binom{m}{k}\leq m^{k} , so  log 2 ⁡ ( m k ) ≤ k ​ log 2 ​ m = k ⋅ b \log_{2}{\binom{m}{k}}\leq k\log_{2}{m}=k\cdot b . The state of the system is given as follows. 
 

 
 
 1 ( m k ) ​ ∑ r = 0 ( m k ) − 1 | r ⟩ k ⋅ b \frac{1}{\sqrt{\binom{m}{k}}}\sum_{r=0}^{\binom{m}{k}-1}\ket{r}_{k\cdot b} 
 
 
 
 2.  
 
 Apply a unitary transformation  U Unrank U_{\text{Unrank}}  that maps the rank  r r  to its corresponding combination  C ⁡ ( r ) C(r) : 
 

 
 
 1 ( m k ) ​ ∑ r | r ⟩ k ⋅ b → U Unrank 1 ( m k ) ​ ∑ r | C ⁡ ( r ) ⟩ k ⋅ b = | S ​ D k m ⟩ \frac{1}{\sqrt{\binom{m}{k}}}\sum_{r}\ket{r}_{k\cdot b}\xrightarrow{U_{\text{Unrank}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\binom{m}{k}}}\sum_{r}\ket{C(r)}_{k\cdot b}=\ket{SD^{m}_{k}} 
 
 
 
 
 The efficiency of the state preparation is determined by the complexity of  U Unrank U_{\text{Unrank}} . 
 
 
 
 B.2  Divide and Conquer Unranking for  O ~ ​ ( m ) \widetilde{O}(m)  Dicke State Preparation 

 
 We first describe a Divide and Conquer strategy for fast unranking.
Given inputs  ( m , k , r ) (m,k,r) , we split the  m m  elements into two halves,  M 1 M_{1}  and  M 2 M_{2} , of sizes  m 1 = ⌊ m / 2 ⌋ m_{1}=\lfloor m/2\rfloor  and  m 2 = ⌈ m / 2 ⌉ m_{2}=\lceil m/2\rceil , and then calculate: 
 
 1.  
 
 The number of elements  k 1 k_{1}  and  k 2 k_{2}  that the combination  C ⁡ ( r ) C(r)  selects from the first ( M 1 M_{1} ) and second halves ( M 2 M_{2} ) respectively, such that  k 1 + k 2 = k k_{1}+k_{2}=k . 
 
 2.  
 
 The residual ranks  r 1 r_{1}  and  r 2 r_{2} , such that  C m , k ​ ( r ) = C m 1 , k 1 ​ ( r 1 ) | C m 2 , k 2 ​ ( r 2 ) C_{m,k}(r)=C_{m_{1},k_{1}}(r_{1})\|C_{m_{2},k_{2}}(r_{2}) . 
 
 
 
 
 The unranking problem can then be solved by recursively solving the left and right subproblems for  ( m 1 , k 1 , r 1 ) (m_{1},k_{1},r_{1})  and  ( m 2 , k 2 , r 2 ) (m_{2},k_{2},r_{2}) . If the work done at one level of recursion is  W ⁡ ( m , k ) W(m,k) , then the overall complexity of  U Unrank U_{\text{Unrank}}  follows the following recurrence relation: 
 

 
 
 T ⁡ ( m , k ) = T ⁡ ( m / 2 , k 1 ) + T ⁡ ( m / 2 , k − k 1 ) + W ⁡ ( m , k ) T(m,k)=T(m/2,k_{1})+T(m/2,k-k_{1})+W(m,k) 
 
 
 If we can show that  W ​ ( m , k ) = O ~ ​ ( m ) W(m,k)=\widetilde{O}(m) , then by Master Theorem (Case 2), the total complexity is  T ​ ( m , k ) = O ~ ​ ( m ​ log ⁡ m ) T(m,k)=\widetilde{O}(m\log m) , which is  O ~ ​ ( m ) \widetilde{O}(m) . 
 
 
 The number of ways to choose  k k  items such that exactly  i i  items come from  M 1 M_{1}  is given by the Hypergeometric distribution: 
 

 
 
 H ⁡ ( i ) = ( m 1 i ) ​ ( m 2 k − i ) H(i)=\binom{m_{1}}{i}\binom{m_{2}}{k-i} 
 
 
 
 
 To find the correct  k 1 k_{1}  for rank  r r , we seek the value such that the prefix sum of  H ⁡ ( i ) H(i)  crosses  r r : 
 

 
 
 P ​ S ​ ( k 1 ) = ∑ i = 0 k 1 − 1 H ⁡ ( i ) ≤ r < P ​ S ​ ( k 1 + 1 ) PS(k_{1})=\sum_{i=0}^{k_{1}-1}H(i)\leq r<PS(k_{1}+1) 
 
 
 We can find  k 1 k_{1}  using a binary search in  O ⁡ ( log ⁡ k ) O(\log k)  steps, provided we can efficiently calculate the prefix sum  P ​ S ​ ( x ) PS(x) .
Once  k 1 k_{1}  is found, we calculate the residual ranks  r 1 r_{1}  and  r 2 r_{2}  corresponding to the left and right subproblems (via division and modulo operations on  r − P ​ S ​ ( k 1 ) r-PS(k_{1}) ), and recursively solve them. 
 
 
 The efficiency of this approach hinges on the fast calculation of the Hypergeometric prefix sum  P ​ S ​ ( x ) PS(x) . The numbers involved (ranks and binomial coefficients) have bit length  L = O ⁡ ( k ​ log ⁡ m ) L=O(k\log m) . A naive summation of  O ⁡ ( k ) O(k)  terms is too slow.
We use a technique called  Binary Splitting . This method is effective for evaluating series where the ratio of consecutive terms  R ⁡ ( i ) = H ⁡ ( i + 1 ) / H ⁡ ( i ) R(i)=H(i+1)/H(i)  is a simple rational function of  i i : 
 

 
 
 R ⁡ ( i ) = ( m 1 − i ) ​ ( k − i ) ( i + 1 ) ​ ( m 2 − k + i + 1 ) R(i)=\frac{(m_{1}-i)(k-i)}{(i+1)(m_{2}-k+i+1)} 
 
 
 The prefix sum can be written as  P ​ S ​ ( x ) = H ⁡ ( 0 ) ⋅ ( 1 + R ⁡ ( 0 ) + R ⁡ ( 0 ) ​ R ​ ( 1 ) + … ) PS(x)=H(0)\cdot(1+R(0)+R(0)R(1)+\dots) . Binary Splitting recursively computes this expression in a balanced tree structure. Crucially, it leverages fast integer multiplication algorithms (e.g., Schönhage–Strassen), which multiply  L L -bit numbers in time  O ~ ​ ( L ) \widetilde{O}(L) . The total time complexity for calculating  P ​ S ​ ( x ) PS(x)  using Binary Splitting is thus  O ~ ​ ( L ​ log ⁡ k ) = O ~ ​ ( k ​ log ⁡ m ) \widetilde{O}(L\log k)=\widetilde{O}(k\log m) . 
 
 
 The divide and conquer algorithm can be implemented as a reversible quantum circuit  U Unrank U_{\text{Unrank}} . 
 
 
 
 1.  
 
 Reversible Fast Arithmetic:  We require efficient reversible quantum circuits for fast integer multiplication and division that maintain the  O ~ ​ ( L ) \widetilde{O}(L)  complexity for arithmetic over  L L  bit integers. 
 
 2.  
 
 Reversible Binary Splitting ( U B ​ S U_{BS} ):  The Binary Splitting procedure is implemented reversibly using the fast arithmetic circuits. The gate complexity of  U B ​ S U_{BS}  is  O ~ ​ ( k ​ log ⁡ m ) \widetilde{O}(k\log m) . 
 
 3.  
 
 Reversible Search:  The binary search for  k 1 k_{1}  is implemented using the reversible Bit-wise Quantum Search strategy. This requires  O ⁡ ( log ⁡ k ) O(\log k)  coherent calls to  U B ​ S U_{BS} . 
 
 
 
 
 The work done at one level of the recursion,  W ⁡ ( m , k ) W(m,k) , is dominated by the reversible search: 
 

 
 
 W ⁡ ( m , k ) = O ⁡ ( log ⁡ k ) ⋅ Cost ​ ( U B ​ S ) = O ~ ​ ( k ​ log ⁡ m ) = O ~ ​ ( m ) W(m,k)=O(\log k)\cdot\text{Cost}(U_{BS})=\widetilde{O}(k\log m)=\widetilde{O}(m) 
 
 
 
 
 
 B.3  Iterative Unranking for low constant factor Dicke State Preparation 

 
 The divide and conquer strategy described has optimal asymptotic complexity but would not be ideal for a constant factor analysis. Here, we describe a simple greedy algorithm to find the  k − k- combination corresponding to rank  r r  with small constant factors: take  c k c_{k}  maximal with  ( c k k ) ≤ r \binom{c_{k}}{k}\leq r , then take  c k − 1 c_{k-1}  maximal with  ( c k − 1 k − 1 ) ≤ r − ( c k k ) \binom{c_{k-1}}{k-1}\leq r-\binom{c_{k}}{k} , and so on. We can translate this greedy algorithm into a reversible quantum circuit to prepare Sparse Dicke states using  2 ⋅ k ⋅ b + b 2\cdot k\cdot b+b  qubits and  𝒪 ( m . k + k 2 b 2 ) \mathcal{O}(m.k+k^{2}b^{2})  Toffoli gates as follows: 
 
 
 
 1.  
 
 The unitary  U Unrank ​ ( n , k ) U_{\text{Unrank}}(n,k)  can be defined as a product of  k k  unitaries, each of which iteratively finds the  j j th coefficient  c j c_{j}  in the sequence  C ⁡ ( r ) C(r)  as follows: 
 

 
 
 U Unrank ​ ( m , k ) = U search ​ ( m , 1 ) ⋅ U search ​ ( m , 2 ) ​ … ​ U search ​ ( m , k − 1 ) ⋅ U search ​ ( m , k ) U_{\text{Unrank}}(m,k)=U_{\text{search}}(m,1)\cdot U_{\text{search}}(m,2)\dots U_{\text{search}}(m,k-1)\cdot U_{\text{search}}(m,k) 
 
 
 Here  U search ​ ( m , j ) U_{\text{search}}(m,j)  can be defined as: 
 

 
 
 U search ​ ( m , j ) ​ | r ⟩ j ​ log 2 ​ m ​ | 0 ⟩ log 2 ⁡ m → | r − ( c j j ) ⟩ ( j − 1 ) ​ log 2 ​ m ​ | c j ⟩ log 2 ⁡ m U_{\text{search}}(m,j)\ket{r}_{j\log_{2}{m}}\ket{0}_{\log_{2}{m}}\rightarrow\ket{r-\binom{c_{j}}{j}}_{(j-1)\log_{2}{m}}\ket{c_{j}}_{\log_{2}{m}} 
 
 
 
 2.  
 
 The unitary  U search ​ ( m , j ) U_{\text{search}}(m,j)  can be implemented using a bitwise binary search strategy, where we determine the largest  c j c_{j}  such that  ( c j j ) ≤ r \binom{c_{j}}{j}\leq r , iterating over  log 2 ⁡ m \log_{2}{m}  bits of  c j c_{j}  from most significant bit to least significant bit, and for each  log 2 ⁡ m ≥ i ≥ 0 \log_{2}{m}\geq i\geq 0 , set  c j = c j + 2 i c_{j}=c_{j}+2^{i}  if  ( c j + 2 i j ) ≤ r \binom{c_{j}+2^{i}}{j}\leq r .
A reversible quantum circuit would therefore require  log 2 ⁡ ( m ) \log_{2}(m)  calls to a sparse QROM, the  i i th of which loads  2 i 2^{i}  binomial coefficients of the form  ( c j + 2 i j ) \binom{c_{j}+2^{i}}{j} . The total Toffoli cost of the sparse QROMs across all iterations is therefore  k . m k.m . Once the Binomial coefficients are loaded using the QROM, we also need a  k . log 2 ⁡ m k.\log_{2}{m}  comparator for each of the  log 2 ⁡ m \log_{2}{m}  bits of  c j c_{j} . The total Toffoli cost of the comparators across all iterations is therefore  𝒪 ⁡ ( ( k ​ log 2 ​ m ) 2 ) \mathcal{O}((k\log_{2}{m})^{2}) .

 Table 7:  Resource counts for GF ( 2 m ) (2^{m})  multiplication for  m = 10 , 11 , 12 m=10,11,12 , expressed as gate counts over two libraries, {Toffoli, CNOT} and {Parity Control Toffoli, CNOT}. 
 
 
 C.1  Parity Control Toffoli  P ​ C ​ T ​ O ​ F PCTOF  and Parity CNOT  P ​ C ​ N ​ O ​ T PCNOT 

 
 The parity control Toffoli PCTOF is equivalent to a multi-target Toffoli gate with controls being the parity of a subset of qubits. The PCNOT gate is a CNOT gate that computes the XOR of its controls and is equivalent to a series of CNOTs with the same target. The main advantages of the PCNOT gate is that in the presence of enough ancillae, it can be performed in a single cycle using lattice surgery independent of the number of controls. 
 
 
 When the two control sets of a PCTOF are disjoint, as is the case of GF ( 2 m ) (2^{m})  multiplication, the two control parities can be computed in a single cycle.  Fig.   6  shows the first PCTOF of  Fig.   7  expanded in terms of PCNOT and Toffoli and in terms of CNOT and Toffoli gates. 
 
 
 Figure 6:  PCTOF in terms of a Toffoli and PCNOTs and then expanded in terms of a Toffoli and CNOTs. Note that we omitted the ancilla cleanup part. 
 
 
 
 C.2  GF( 2 m 2^{m} ) arithmetic circuits 

 
 Our implementation of the DQI algorithm relies on arithmetic circuits over GF field sizes of  2 10 2^{10}  through  2 12 2^{12} . The bottleneck is GF multiplication. 
 
 
 To implement the respective multiplication (and division) circuits, we rely on  [ 9 ] .
This synthesis algorithm develops a modification of Karatsuba multiplication over a carefully selected irreducible polynomial, along with local optimizations using templates, allowing for a reduction in both Toffoli and CNOT gate counts compared to the state-of-the-art straight Karatsuba multiplication  [ 8 ] .
We tune the implementation  [ 9 ]  to focus on optimizing an  ∞ \infty -to-1 weighted sum of Toffoli and CNOT gate counts to utilize the PCTOF gates better.
To get constructions made of the PCTOF and CNOT gates, we start from the Toffoli and CNOT circuits from  [ 9 ]  and commute the CNOT gates to the left while updating the sets of the PCTOF gates, resulting in circuits that start with three disjoint CNOT stages acting on the two input registers and the resulting register followed by a circuit made of the PCTOF gates.
The first two CNOT circuits are equivalent to the identity since we do not modify the input registers and the CNOT circuit acting on the target can be ignored since the target is always initialized in the zero state. The resulting optimized circuits are summarized in  Table   7 .
 Fig.   7  shows the  G ​ F ​ ( 2 3 ) GF(2^{3})  multiplication circuit with irreducible polynomial  x 3 + x + 1 x^{3}+x+1  in terms of PCTOF gates and explicit circuit files are available as a part of the Zenodo upload of this paper. 
 
 
 In addition, we developed a circuit optimization method applicable to the kinds of circuits with PCTOF gates considered. The optimization algorithm relies on two observations: first, any two PCTOF gates commute, and second, each PCTOF gate computes a Boolean polynomial of degree 2 and then EXORs it onto a separate output register. This means that a single PCTOF gate can be thought of as a vector in the  m 2 m^{2} -dimensional Boolean space, where each coordinate corresponds to a Boolean product of some two variables, and circuits with  k k  PCTOF gates (with targets on the bottom register such as in our case of GF multiplication circuits) can be thought of as  k × m 2 k\,{\times}\,m^{2}  matrices. This means that the matrix rank determines the minimal number of PCTOF gates necessary, in a given set that implements the desired functionality. We implemented this optimization algorithm and found a small optimization compared to what is directly offered by  [ 9 ]  (compare Toffoli count to the PCTOF count in  Table   7 ). We suspect that the improvement is small due to the large number of optimizations that already went into  [ 9 ] . 
 
 
 Figure 7:  G ​ F ​ ( 2 3 ) GF(2^{3})  multiplication with irreducible polynomial  x 3 + x + 1 x^{3}+x+1  in terms of PCTOF gates. 
 
 
 
 C.3  Measurement-based Uncomputation for GF2 Multiplication. 

 
 The GF ( 2 m ) (2^{m})  multiplication operation is an out-of-place operation that applies the transformation  | x ⟩ ​ | y ⟩ ​ | 0 ⟩ → | x ⟩ ​ | y ⟩ ​ | x ​ y ⟩ \ket{x}\ket{y}\ket{0}\rightarrow\ket{x}\ket{y}\ket{xy} . The uncomputation of this operation can be done using only measurements and  C ​ Z CZ  gates. 
 
 
 The uncomputation starts by measuring the target register in the  X X  basis. This is equivalent to applying the  H ⊗ m H^{\otimes m}  to the target register and then measuring in the computational basis. The  H ⊗ m H^{\otimes m}  operations transform the state to  | x ⟩ ​ | y ⟩ ​ | x ​ y ⟩ = ∑ a i , j ​ | i ⟩ ​ | j ⟩ ​ | i ​ j ⟩ → H ⊗ m 1 2 m ​ ∑ c = 0 2 m − 1 { ∑ ( − 1 ) h ⁡ ( c , i ​ j ) ​ | i ⟩ ​ | j ⟩ } ​ | c ⟩ \ket{x}\ket{y}\ket{xy}=\sum a_{i,j}\ket{i}\ket{j}\ket{ij}\xrightarrow{H^{\otimes m}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{m}}}\sum_{c=0}^{2^{m}-1}\big\{\sum(-1)^{h(c,ij)}\ket{i}\ket{j}\big\}\ket{c}  where  h ⁡ ( a , b ) h(a,b)  is the Hamming weight of  a ⊕ b a\oplus b . After the measurement, we end up with a classical bitstring  c c  and phase flips on some of the coefficients of the input superposition  ∑ ( − 1 ) h ⁡ ( c , i ​ j ) ​ a i , j ​ | i ⟩ ​ | j ⟩ \sum(-1)^{h(c,ij)}a_{i,j}\ket{i}\ket{j} . 
 
 
 While it seems that we need to know the product to correct the phase, the linearity of  h h  and symmetry of  C ​ Z CZ  allow us to correct the phase with only  C ​ Z CZ s. For example if  c t = 1 c_{t}{=}1  then we need to apply  C ​ Z ​ ( x i , y j ) CZ(x_{i},y_{j})  for all  ( i , j ) (i,j)  pairs such that  x i + j mod p ⁡ ( x ) x^{i+j}\mod p(x)  has  x t x^{t}  where  p ⁡ ( x ) p(x)  is the irreducible polynomial. This leads to the requirement to implement a certain  C ​ Z CZ  gate circuit. Note that any such circuit can be implemented in depth  ⌊ m / 2 + 0.4993 ⋅ log 2 ⁡ ( m ) + 3.0191 ⋅ log ⁡ ( m ) − 10.9139 ⌋ \lfloor m/2+0.4993{\cdot}\log^{2}(m)+3.0191{\cdot}\log(m)-10.9139\rfloor , using no more than  m 2 / 4 + O ⁡ ( m ​ log 2 ⁡ ( m ) ) m^{2}/4+O(m\log^{2}(m))  Clifford gates  [ 58 ]  or  O ⁡ ( m 2 / log ⁡ ( m ) ) O(m^{2}/\log(m))  gates asymptotically. 
 
 
 Our implementation of measurement-based uncomputation of GF2 multiplication is available in Qualtran  [ 19 ]  as  GF2MulMBUC  and  Fig.   8  shows the uncomputation of GF2 multiplication for  m = 3 m{=}3  and irreducible polynomial  x 3 + x + 1 x^{3}+x+1 . 
 
 
 Figure 8:  Construction of measurement based uncomputation of GF2 multiplication for irreducible polynomial  x 3 + x + 1 x^{3}+x+1 . Note that the measurement is in the X basis. 
 
 
 
 
 Appendix D  Bounding the number of iterations for Synchronized Reversible Polynomial EEA 

 
 We analyze the worst-case number of iterations required for the synchronized, reversible implementation of the Polynomial Extended Euclidean Algorithm (PEEA) presented in  Section   II.2.2 . This implementation utilizes shared registers and a 4-stage synchronized architecture designed to allow computation branches in a superposition to proceed efficiently at their own pace. 
 
 
 Let the input polynomials be  A A  and  B B  over a finite field  𝔽 q \mathbb{F}_{q} , with  n = deg ⁡ ( A ) n=\deg(A)  and  deg ⁡ ( B ) < n \deg(B)<n . 
 
 
 Definition D.1   (Iteration Dynamics) . 
 
 For iteration  i i  (which computes quotient  Q i Q_{i} , remainder  R i R_{i} , and cofactor  U i U_{i} ): 
 
 •  
 
 d i = deg ⁡ ( Q i ) d_{i}=\deg(Q_{i}) . We define  d 0 = 0 d_{0}=0 . Since  deg ⁡ ( B ) < deg ⁡ ( A ) \deg(B)<\deg(A) ,  d i ≥ 1 d_{i}\geq 1  for  i ≥ 1 i\geq 1 . 
 
 •  
 
 s i = deg ⁡ ( R i − 1 ) − deg ⁡ ( R i ) s_{i}=\deg(R_{i-1})-\deg(R_{i}) : The degree reduction of the remainder.  s i ≥ 1 s_{i}\geq 1 . 
 
 
 Let  k k  be the total number of iterations. Let  D k = ∑ i = 1 k d i D_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}d_{i}  and  S k = ∑ i = 1 k s i S_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}s_{i} . 
 
 
 
 Lemma D.2   (PEEA Constraints) . 
 
 The variables are constrained by  k ≤ D k k\leq D_{k}  and  k ≤ S k k\leq S_{k} . For Full PEEA (coprime inputs, unequal degrees),  D k = n D_{k}=n  and  S k = n S_{k}=n . 
 
 
 
 We analyze the time complexity  T i T_{i}  of iteration  i i  based on the 5 steps described in  Fig.   9 . 
 
 
 Lemma D.3   (Cost Per Iteration) . 
 
 The number of cycles  T i T_{i}  required for iteration  i i  in the optimized implementation is  T i = 2 ​ d i + s i + d i − 1 + 2 T_{i}=2d_{i}+s_{i}+d_{i-1}+2 . 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We analyze the time required for each stage in the 4-stage cycle, corresponding to Steps 1-4 in Figure 1: 
 
 1.  
 
 Stage 1  (Step-1, Poly Long Division): Time taken is the length of  Q i Q_{i}  (number of terms), which is  d i + 1 d_{i}+1 . 
 
 2.  
 
 Stage 2  (Step-2, Remainder Normalization): Time taken is the total degree reduction  s i s_{i} . 
 
 3.  
 
 Stage 3  (Step-3, Bézout Cofactor Alignment): Time taken is the degree growth from the prior iteration,  d i − 1 d_{i-1} . 
 
 4.  
 
 Stage 4  (Step-4, Bézout Cofactor Update): Time taken is the length of  Q i Q_{i} , which is  d i + 1 d_{i}+1 . The register swap (Step-5) occurs concurrently during the final cycle of this stage, incurring no extra time. 
 
 
 
 
 Summing the costs for iteration  i i : 
 

 
 
 T i \displaystyle T_{i} 
 = ( d i + 1 ) + s i + d i − 1 + ( d i + 1 ) \displaystyle=(d_{i}+1)+s_{i}+d_{i-1}+(d_{i}+1) 
 
 
 
 
 = 2 ​ d i + s i + d i − 1 + 2 . \displaystyle=2d_{i}+s_{i}+d_{i-1}+2. 
 
 
 ∎ 
 
 
 
 
 Lemma D.4   (Total Cost Function) . 
 
 The total number of cycles  T T  required to complete  k k  iterations is  T = 3 ​ D k + S k − d k + 2 ​ k T=3D_{k}+S_{k}-d_{k}+2k . 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The total time is the sum of  T i T_{i}  from  i = 1 i=1  to  k k . 
 

 
 
 T \displaystyle T 
 = ∑ i = 1 k T i = ∑ i = 1 k ( 2 ​ d i + s i + d i − 1 + 2 ) \displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}T_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(2d_{i}+s_{i}+d_{i-1}+2) 
 
 
 
 
 = 2 ​ ∑ i = 1 k d i + ∑ i = 1 k s i + ∑ i = 1 k d i − 1 + 2 ​ k . \displaystyle=2\sum_{i=1}^{k}d_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}s_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}d_{i-1}+2k. 
 
 
 We evaluate the third term:  ∑ i = 1 k d i − 1 = d 0 + d 1 + ⋯ + d k − 1 \sum_{i=1}^{k}d_{i-1}=d_{0}+d_{1}+\dots+d_{k-1} . Since  d 0 = 0 d_{0}=0 , this equals  D k − d k D_{k}-d_{k} . 
 

 
 
 T = 2 ​ D k + S k + ( D k − d k ) + 2 ​ k = 3 ​ D k + S k − d k + 2 ​ k . T=2D_{k}+S_{k}+(D_{k}-d_{k})+2k=3D_{k}+S_{k}-d_{k}+2k. 
 
 
 ∎ 
 
 
 
 
 D.1  Worst-Case Analysis: Full PEEA (GCD) 

 
 We analyze the maximum cycles required for the Full PEEA, assuming worst-case coprime inputs with degrees  ( n , n − 1 ) (n,n-1) . 
 
 
 Theorem D.5   (Cycle Bound for Full PEEA) . 
 
 The maximum number of cycles required for the optimized synchronized Full PEEA on inputs of unequal degrees (max degree  n n ) is  T m ​ a ​ x = 6 ​ n − 1 T_{max}=6n-1 . 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We maximize the cost function  T = 3 ​ D k + S k − d k + 2 ​ k T=3D_{k}+S_{k}-d_{k}+2k .
We use the constraints for Full PEEA (Lemma  D.2 ):  D k = n D_{k}=n  and  S k = n S_{k}=n . 
 
 
 Substituting these values: 
 

 
 
 T = 3 ​ n + n − d k + 2 ​ k = 4 ​ n − d k + 2 ​ k . T=3n+n-d_{k}+2k=4n-d_{k}+2k. 
 
 
 To maximize  T T , we maximize the number of iterations  k k  and minimize the final quotient degree  d k d_{k} .
The constraints are  k ≤ n k\leq n  and  d k ≥ 1 d_{k}\geq 1 .
We set  k m ​ a ​ x = n k_{max}=n  and  d k , m ​ i ​ n = 1 d_{k,min}=1 . 
 

 
 
 T m ​ a ​ x = 4 ​ n − 1 + 2 ​ n = 6 ​ n − 1 . T_{max}=4n-1+2n=6n-1. 
 
 
 This worst case is achieved by the generalized Fibonacci sequence where  d i = 1 d_{i}=1  and  s i = 1 s_{i}=1  for all  i = 1 ​ … ​ n i=1\dots n .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 D.2  Worst-Case Analysis: Half PEEA (Reed-Solomon Decoding) 

 
 The Half-PEEA stops when  D k > n / 2 D_{k}>n/2 . 
 
 
 Theorem D.6   (Cycle Bound for Half PEEA) . 
 
 The maximum number of cycles required for the optimized synchronized Half-PEEA is  T m ​ a ​ x = 6 ​ ⌊ n / 2 ⌋ + 5 T_{max}=6\lfloor n/2\rfloor+5 . 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We maximize the cost function  T = 3 ​ D k + S k − d k + 2 ​ k T=3D_{k}+S_{k}-d_{k}+2k .
The maximum degree reduction required is  D k , m ​ a ​ x = ⌊ n / 2 ⌋ + 1 D_{k,max}=\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1 . 
 
 
 To maximize  T T , we seek the slowest convergence scenario. This occurs when  d i = 1 d_{i}=1  and  s i = 1 s_{i}=1  for all  i i . In this scenario: 
 
 •  
 
 D k = D k , m ​ a ​ x D_{k}=D_{k,max} . 
 
 •  
 
 k = D k , m ​ a ​ x k=D_{k,max}  (since  d i = 1 d_{i}=1 ). 
 
 •  
 
 S k = D k , m ​ a ​ x S_{k}=D_{k,max}  (since  s i = 1 s_{i}=1 ). 
 
 •  
 
 d k = 1 d_{k}=1 . 
 
 
 Substituting these into the cost function: 
 

 
 
 T m ​ a ​ x \displaystyle T_{max} 
 = 3 ​ D k , m ​ a ​ x + D k , m ​ a ​ x − 1 + 2 ​ D k , m ​ a ​ x \displaystyle=3D_{k,max}+D_{k,max}-1+2D_{k,max} 
 
 
 
 
 = 6 ​ D k , m ​ a ​ x − 1 \displaystyle=6D_{k,max}-1 
 
 
 
 
 = 6 ​ ( ⌊ n / 2 ⌋ + 1 ) − 1 \displaystyle=6(\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1)-1 
 
 
 
 
 = 6 ​ ⌊ n / 2 ⌋ + 5 . \displaystyle=6\lfloor n/2\rfloor+5. 
 
 
 ∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Appendix E  Deferred proofs of upper bounds on affine intersections of Maiorana-McFarland target sets 

 
 Here, we state and prove a few lemmas that are omitted from  Section   III.2.4 . Together the proofs here and in  Section   III.2.4  establish the upper bounds on  | A ∩ S k | |A\cap S_{k}|  given in ( 24 ). 
 
 
 Lemma E.1   (Upper bound on  | A ∩ R k | |A\cap R_{k}|  and  | A ∩ S k | |A\cap S_{k}|  when  dim A < k \dim A<k ) . 
 
 
 Let  k k  be a positive integer,  d d  a non-negative integer and  A A  a  d d -dimensional affine subspace of  𝔽 2 2 ​ k \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k} . Then 
 

 
 
 | A ∩ R k | ≤ 2 d , | A ∩ S k | ≤ 2 d . \displaystyle|A\cap R_{k}|\leq 2^{d},\quad|A\cap S_{k}|\leq 2^{d}. 
 
 (129) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 | A ∩ R k | ≤ | A | = 2 d |A\cap R_{k}|\leq|A|=2^{d}  and  | A ∩ S k | ≤ | A | = 2 d |A\cap S_{k}|\leq|A|=2^{d} .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma E.2   (Size of  R k R_{k}  and  S k S_{k} ) . 
 
 

 
 
 | R k | = 2 2 ​ k − 1 + 2 k − 1 , \displaystyle|R_{k}|=2^{2k-1}+2^{k-1}, 
 
 (130) 
 
 
 | S k | = 2 2 ​ k − 1 − 2 k − 1 . \displaystyle|S_{k}|=2^{2k-1}-2^{k-1}. 
 
 (131) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We can write 
 

 
 
 R k = ⨆ x ∈ 𝔽 2 k { x } × { x } ⟂ \displaystyle R_{k}=\bigsqcup_{x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}}\{x\}\times\{x\}^{\perp} 
 
 (132) 
 
 where  { x } ⟂ ⊂ 𝔽 2 k \{x\}^{\perp}\subset\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}  is the set of  k k -bit strings orthogonal to  x ∈ 𝔽 2 k x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}  under the standard dot product  ⟨ . , . ⟩ \langle.,.\rangle . But 
 

 
 
 | { x } ⟂ | = { 2 k if x = 0 ∈ 𝔽 2 k 2 k − 1 otherwise \displaystyle|\{x\}^{\perp}|=\begin{cases}2^{k}&\text{if}\quad x=0\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\\
2^{k-1}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases} 
 
 (133) 
 
 so  | R k | = 2 2 ​ k − 1 + 2 k − 1 |R_{k}|=2^{2k-1}+2^{k-1}  and  | S k | = 2 2 ​ k − 1 − 2 k − 1 |S_{k}|=2^{2k-1}-2^{k-1} .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Corollary E.1   (Upper bound on  | A ∩ R k | |A\cap R_{k}|  and  | A ∩ S k | |A\cap S_{k}|  when  dim A = 2 ​ k \dim A=2k ) . 
 
 
 Let  k k  be a positive integer and  A = 𝔽 2 2 ​ k A=\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k}  the  2 ​ k 2k -dimensional affine subspace of  𝔽 2 2 ​ k \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k} . Then 
 

 
 
 | A ∩ R k | = 2 2 ​ k − 1 + 2 k − 1 , \displaystyle|A\cap R_{k}|=2^{2k-1}+2^{k-1}, 
 
 (134) 
 
 
 | A ∩ S k | = 2 2 ​ k − 1 − 2 k − 1 . \displaystyle|A\cap S_{k}|=2^{2k-1}-2^{k-1}. 
 
 (135) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Immediate consequence of Lemma  E.2 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma E.3   (Upper bound on  | A ∩ R k | |A\cap R_{k}|  and  | A ∩ S k | |A\cap S_{k}|  when  dim A = 2 ​ k − 1 \dim A=2k-1 ) . 
 
 
 Let  k k  be a positive integer and let  A ⊂ 𝔽 2 2 ​ k A\subset\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k}  be a  ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) (2k-1) -dimensional affine subspace of  𝔽 2 2 ​ k \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k} . Then 
 

 
 
 | A ∩ R k | \displaystyle|A\cap R_{k}| 
 ≤ 2 2 ​ k − 2 + 2 k − 1 \displaystyle\leq 2^{2k-2}+2^{k-1} 
 
 (136) 
 
 
 | A ∩ S k | \displaystyle|A\cap S_{k}| 
 ≤ 2 2 ​ k − 2 . \displaystyle\leq 2^{2k-2}. 
 
 (137) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 If  k = 1 k=1 , then  | A | = 2 |A|=2  and  | S 1 | = 1 |S_{1}|=1 , so  | A ∩ R 1 | ≤ 2 |A\cap R_{1}|\leq 2  and  | A ∩ S 1 | ≤ 1 |A\cap S_{1}|\leq 1 . Assume  k > 1 k>1 . The affine space  A A  contains  2 2 ​ k − 1 2^{2k-1}  bit strings, but there are only  2 2 ​ k − 2 2^{2k-2}  bit strings of each possible suffix. Therefore,  F F  has at least two elements. Thus, by Remark  III.1  we have three cases to consider 
 

 
 
 dim F = 1 \displaystyle\dim F=1 
 ∧ 11 ∉ F \displaystyle\quad\land\quad 11\notin F 
 
 (138) 
 
 
 dim F = 1 \displaystyle\dim F=1 
 ∧ 11 ∈ F \displaystyle\quad\land\quad 11\in F 
 
 (139) 
 
 
 dim F = 2 \displaystyle\dim F=2 
 . 
 
 (140) 
 
 We prove the first two cases directly and the third one by induction. 
 
 
 If  dim F = 1 \dim F=1 , then Lemma  III.1  implies that  dim W ′ = 2 ​ k − 2 \dim W^{\prime}=2k-2  and therefore 
 

 
 
 A = 𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 ⊗ F . \displaystyle A=\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2}\otimes F. 
 
 (141) 
 
 If  11 ∉ F 11\notin F , then 
 

 
 
 A ∩ R k \displaystyle A\cap R_{k} 
 = R k − 1 ⊗ F \displaystyle=R_{k-1}\otimes F 
 
 (142) 
 
 
 A ∩ S k \displaystyle A\cap S_{k} 
 = S k − 1 ⊗ F \displaystyle=S_{k-1}\otimes F 
 
 (143) 
 
 and using Lemma  E.2 
 

 
 
 | A ∩ R k | \displaystyle|A\cap R_{k}| 
 = | F | ⋅ | R k − 1 | = 2 ⋅ ( 2 2 ​ k − 3 + 2 k − 2 ) = 2 2 ​ k − 2 + 2 k − 1 \displaystyle=|F|\cdot|R_{k-1}|=2\cdot(2^{2k-3}+2^{k-2})=2^{2k-2}+2^{k-1} 
 
 (144) 
 
 
 | A ∩ S k | \displaystyle|A\cap S_{k}| 
 = | F | ⋅ | S k − 1 | = 2 ⋅ ( 2 2 ​ k − 3 − 2 k − 2 ) < 2 2 ​ k − 2 . \displaystyle=|F|\cdot|S_{k-1}|=2\cdot(2^{2k-3}-2^{k-2})<2^{2k-2}. 
 
 (145) 
 
 If  F = { σ , 11 } F=\{\sigma,11\}  with  σ ≠ 11 \sigma\neq 11 , then 
 

 
 
 A ∩ R k \displaystyle A\cap R_{k} 
 = ( R k − 1 ⊗ σ ) ⊔ ( S k − 1 ⊗ 11 ) \displaystyle=(R_{k-1}\otimes\sigma)\sqcup(S_{k-1}\otimes 11) 
 
 (146) 
 
 
 A ∩ S k \displaystyle A\cap S_{k} 
 = ( S k − 1 ⊗ σ ) ⊔ ( R k − 1 ⊗ 11 ) \displaystyle=(S_{k-1}\otimes\sigma)\sqcup(R_{k-1}\otimes 11) 
 
 (147) 
 
 and using  R k − 1 ⊔ S k − 1 = 𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 R_{k-1}\sqcup S_{k-1}=\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2} , we find 
 

 
 
 | A ∩ R k | \displaystyle|A\cap R_{k}| 
 = | 𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 | < 2 2 ​ k − 2 + 2 k − 1 \displaystyle=|\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2}|<2^{2k-2}+2^{k-1} 
 
 (148) 
 
 
 | A ∩ S k | \displaystyle|A\cap S_{k}| 
 = | 𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 | = 2 2 ​ k − 2 . \displaystyle=|\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2}|=2^{2k-2}. 
 
 (149) 
 
 We now prove the case  dim F = 2 \dim F=2  by induction. We established the base case at the opening of the proof. Assume now that 
 

 
 
 | A ∩ R k − 1 | \displaystyle|A\cap R_{k-1}| 
 ≤ 2 2 ​ k − 4 + 2 k − 2 \displaystyle\leq 2^{2k-4}+2^{k-2} 
 
 (150) 
 
 
 | A ∩ S k − 1 | \displaystyle|A\cap S_{k-1}| 
 ≤ 2 2 ​ k − 4 . \displaystyle\leq 2^{2k-4}. 
 
 (151) 
 
 By Lemma  III.1 , the four affine subspaces  A σ ′ A_{\sigma}^{\prime}  of  𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2}  with  σ ∈ F \sigma\in F  in ( 30 ) and ( 31 ) arise as translations of the same linear subspace  W ′ W^{\prime}  of dimension  2 ​ k − 3 2k-3 , so at most two of them are distinct 
 

 
 
 A 1 ′ := A 11 ′ = A ρ ′ , A 2 ′ := A σ ′ = A τ ′ . \displaystyle A_{1}^{\prime}:=A_{11}^{\prime}=A_{\rho}^{\prime},\quad A_{2}^{\prime}:=A_{\sigma}^{\prime}=A_{\tau}^{\prime}. 
 
 (152) 
 
 The equation  R k − 1 ⊔ S k − 1 = 𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 R_{k-1}\sqcup S_{k-1}=\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2}  implies then that 
 

 
 
 | A ∩ R k | \displaystyle|A\cap R_{k}| 
 = | A 1 ′ | + 2 ⋅ | A 2 ′ ∩ R k − 1 | \displaystyle=|A_{1}^{\prime}|+2\cdot|A_{2}^{\prime}\cap R_{k-1}| 
 
 (153) 
 
 
 | A ∩ S k | \displaystyle|A\cap S_{k}| 
 = | A 1 ′ | + 2 ⋅ | A 2 ′ ∩ S k − 1 | \displaystyle=|A_{1}^{\prime}|+2\cdot|A_{2}^{\prime}\cap S_{k-1}| 
 
 (154) 
 
 and using our inductive hypothesis, we obtain 
 

 
 
 | A ∩ R k | \displaystyle|A\cap R_{k}| 
 ≤ 2 2 ​ k − 3 + 2 ⋅ ( 2 2 ​ k − 4 + 2 k − 2 ) = 2 2 ​ k − 2 + 2 k − 1 \displaystyle\leq 2^{2k-3}+2\cdot(2^{2k-4}+2^{k-2})=2^{2k-2}+2^{k-1} 
 
 (155) 
 
 
 | A ∩ S k | \displaystyle|A\cap S_{k}| 
 ≤ 2 2 ​ k − 3 + 2 ⋅ 2 2 ​ k − 4 = 2 2 ​ k − 2 \displaystyle\leq 2^{2k-3}+2\cdot 2^{2k-4}=2^{2k-2} 
 
 (156) 
 
 completing the proof of the Lemma.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma E.4   (Proxy expression for mixed recursive structure formulas with four terms) . 
 
 
 Let  k k  be a positive integer and  a , b , c ∈ 𝔽 2 2 ​ k a,b,c\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k} . For any four disjoint affine subspaces of the form 
 

 
 
 A 00 \displaystyle A_{00} 
 = a + W \displaystyle=a+W 
 
 (157) 
 
 
 A 01 \displaystyle A_{01} 
 = a + b + W \displaystyle=a+b+W 
 
 (158) 
 
 
 A 10 \displaystyle A_{10} 
 = a + c + W \displaystyle=a+c+W 
 
 (159) 
 
 
 A 11 \displaystyle A_{11} 
 = a + b + c + W \displaystyle=a+b+c+W 
 
 (160) 
 
 arising as translations of the same linear subspace  W ⊂ 𝔽 2 2 ​ k W\subset\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k} , there exist two disjoint sets  B 1 B_{1}  and  B 2 B_{2}  each of which is either empty or affine and such that 
 

 
 
 | A 00 ∩ S k | + | A 01 ∩ S k | + | A 10 ∩ S k | + | A 11 ∩ R k | = | B 1 | + 2 ⋅ | B 2 ∩ S k | \displaystyle|A_{00}\cap S_{k}|+|A_{01}\cap S_{k}|+|A_{10}\cap S_{k}|+|A_{11}\cap R_{k}|=|B_{1}|+2\cdot|B_{2}\cap S_{k}| 
 
 (161) 
 
 and  | B 1 | + | B 2 | = 2 ⋅ | W | |B_{1}|+|B_{2}|=2\cdot|W| . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 First note that for any  x ∈ W x\in W 
 

 
 
 𝟙 S k ​ ( a + x ) \displaystyle\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+x) 
 = 𝟙 S k ​ ( a ) + ω ⁡ ( a , x ) + 𝟙 S k ​ ( x ) \displaystyle=\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a)+\omega(a,x)+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(x) 
 
 (162) 
 
 
 𝟙 S k ​ ( a + b + x ) \displaystyle\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+b+x) 
 = 𝟙 S k ​ ( a ) + 𝟙 S k ​ ( b ) + ω ⁡ ( a , b ) + ω ⁡ ( a , x ) + ω ⁡ ( b , x ) + 𝟙 S k ​ ( x ) \displaystyle=\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a)+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(b)+\omega(a,b)+\omega(a,x)+\omega(b,x)+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(x) 
 
 (163) 
 
 
 𝟙 S k ​ ( a + c + x ) \displaystyle\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+c+x) 
 = 𝟙 S k ​ ( a ) + 𝟙 S k ​ ( c ) + ω ⁡ ( a , c ) + ω ⁡ ( a , x ) + ω ⁡ ( c , x ) + 𝟙 S k ​ ( x ) \displaystyle=\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a)+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(c)+\omega(a,c)+\omega(a,x)+\omega(c,x)+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(x) 
 
 (164) 
 
 
 𝟙 S k ​ ( a + b + c + x ) \displaystyle\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+b+c+x) 
 = 𝟙 S k ​ ( a ) + 𝟙 S k ​ ( b ) + 𝟙 S k ​ ( c ) + ω ⁡ ( a , b ) + ω ⁡ ( b , c ) + ω ⁡ ( c , a ) \displaystyle=\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a)+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(b)+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(c)+\omega(a,b)+\omega(b,c)+\omega(c,a) 
 
 
 
 
 + ω ⁡ ( a , x ) + ω ⁡ ( b , x ) + ω ⁡ ( c , x ) + 𝟙 S k ​ ( x ) \displaystyle+\omega(a,x)+\omega(b,x)+\omega(c,x)+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(x) 
 
 (165) 
 
 which means that 
 

 
 
 𝟙 S k ​ ( a + x ) + 𝟙 S k ​ ( a + b + x ) + 𝟙 S k ​ ( a + c + x ) + 𝟙 S k ​ ( a + b + c + x ) = ω ⁡ ( b , c ) . \displaystyle\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+x)+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+b+x)+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+c+x)+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+b+c+x)=\omega(b,c). 
 
 (166) 
 
 Thus, for any fixed  a , b , c ∈ 𝔽 2 2 ​ k a,b,c\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k} , we can infer  𝟙 S k ​ ( a + b + c + x ) \mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+b+c+x)  from  𝟙 S k ​ ( a + x ) \mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+x) ,  𝟙 S k ​ ( a + b + x ) \mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+b+x) , and  𝟙 S k ​ ( a + c + x ) \mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+c+x) . Indeed, if  ω ⁡ ( b , c ) = 0 \omega(b,c)=0 , then for every  x ∈ W x\in W  we have the following eight equivalences 
 

 
 
 
 ( a + x ∈ R k ) ∧ ( a + b + x ∈ R k ) ∧ ( a + c + x ∈ R k ) ⇔ a + b + c + x ∈ R k \displaystyle(a+x\in R_{k})\,\land\,(a+b+x\in R_{k})\,\land\,(a+c+x\in R_{k})\iff a+b+c+x\in R_{k} 
 
 (167) 
 
 
 
 ( a + x ∈ R k ) ∧ ( a + b + x ∈ R k ) ∧ ( a + c + x ∈ S k ) ⇔ a + b + c + x ∈ S k \displaystyle(a+x\in R_{k})\,\land\,(a+b+x\in R_{k})\,\land\,(a+c+x\in S_{k})\iff a+b+c+x\in S_{k} 
 
 (168) 
 
 
 
 ( a + x ∈ R k ) ∧ ( a + b + x ∈ S k ) ∧ ( a + c + x ∈ R k ) ⇔ a + b + c + x ∈ S k \displaystyle(a+x\in R_{k})\,\land\,(a+b+x\in S_{k})\,\land\,(a+c+x\in R_{k})\iff a+b+c+x\in S_{k} 
 
 (169) 
 
 
 
 ( a + x ∈ R k ) ∧ ( a + b + x ∈ S k ) ∧ ( a + c + x ∈ S k ) ⇔ a + b + c + x ∈ R k \displaystyle(a+x\in R_{k})\,\land\,(a+b+x\in S_{k})\,\land\,(a+c+x\in S_{k})\iff a+b+c+x\in R_{k} 
 
 (170) 
 
 
 
 … \displaystyle\quad\quad\ldots 
 
 
 
 
 ( a + x ∈ S k ) ∧ ( a + b + x ∈ S k ) ∧ ( a + c + x ∈ S k ) ⇔ a + b + c + x ∈ S k . \displaystyle(a+x\in S_{k})\,\land\,(a+b+x\in S_{k})\,\land\,(a+c+x\in S_{k})\iff a+b+c+x\in S_{k}. 
 
 (171) 
 
 If on the other hand  ω ⁡ ( b , c ) = 1 \omega(b,c)=1 , then for every  x ∈ W x\in W  we have the eight equivalences 
 

 
 
 
 ( a + x ∈ R k ) ∧ ( a + b + x ∈ R k ) ∧ ( a + c + x ∈ R k ) ⇔ a + b + c + x ∈ S k \displaystyle(a+x\in R_{k})\,\land\,(a+b+x\in R_{k})\,\land\,(a+c+x\in R_{k})\iff a+b+c+x\in S_{k} 
 
 (172) 
 
 
 
 ( a + x ∈ R k ) ∧ ( a + b + x ∈ R k ) ∧ ( a + c + x ∈ S k ) ⇔ a + b + c + x ∈ R k \displaystyle(a+x\in R_{k})\,\land\,(a+b+x\in R_{k})\,\land\,(a+c+x\in S_{k})\iff a+b+c+x\in R_{k} 
 
 (173) 
 
 
 
 ( a + x ∈ R k ) ∧ ( a + b + x ∈ S k ) ∧ ( a + c + x ∈ R k ) ⇔ a + b + c + x ∈ R k \displaystyle(a+x\in R_{k})\,\land\,(a+b+x\in S_{k})\,\land\,(a+c+x\in R_{k})\iff a+b+c+x\in R_{k} 
 
 (174) 
 
 
 
 ( a + x ∈ R k ) ∧ ( a + b + x ∈ S k ) ∧ ( a + c + x ∈ S k ) ⇔ a + b + c + x ∈ S k \displaystyle(a+x\in R_{k})\,\land\,(a+b+x\in S_{k})\,\land\,(a+c+x\in S_{k})\iff a+b+c+x\in S_{k} 
 
 (175) 
 
 
 
 … \displaystyle\quad\quad\ldots 
 
 
 
 
 ( a + x ∈ S k ) ∧ ( a + b + x ∈ S k ) ∧ ( a + c + x ∈ S k ) ⇔ a + b + c + x ∈ R k . \displaystyle(a+x\in S_{k})\,\land\,(a+b+x\in S_{k})\,\land\,(a+c+x\in S_{k})\iff a+b+c+x\in R_{k}. 
 
 (176) 
 
 In either case,  W W  can be partitioned into eight disjoint sets 
 

 
 
 W r ​ r ​ r \displaystyle W_{rrr} 
 : = { w ∈ W | ( a + w ∈ R k ) ∧ ( a + b + w ∈ R k ) ∧ ( a + c + w ∈ R k ) } \displaystyle:=\{w\in W\,|\,(a+w\in R_{k})\,\land\,(a+b+w\in R_{k})\,\land\,(a+c+w\in R_{k})\} 
 
 (177) 
 
 
 W r ​ r ​ s \displaystyle W_{rrs} 
 : = { w ∈ W | ( a + w ∈ R k ) ∧ ( a + b + w ∈ R k ) ∧ ( a + c + w ∈ S k ) } \displaystyle:=\{w\in W\,|\,(a+w\in R_{k})\,\land\,(a+b+w\in R_{k})\,\land\,(a+c+w\in S_{k})\} 
 
 (178) 
 
 
 W r ​ s ​ r \displaystyle W_{rsr} 
 : = { w ∈ W | ( a + w ∈ R k ) ∧ ( a + b + w ∈ S k ) ∧ ( a + c + w ∈ R k ) } \displaystyle:=\{w\in W\,|\,(a+w\in R_{k})\,\land\,(a+b+w\in S_{k})\,\land\,(a+c+w\in R_{k})\} 
 
 (179) 
 
 
 W r ​ s ​ s \displaystyle W_{rss} 
 : = { w ∈ W | ( a + w ∈ R k ) ∧ ( a + b + w ∈ S k ) ∧ ( a + c + w ∈ S k ) } \displaystyle:=\{w\in W\,|\,(a+w\in R_{k})\,\land\,(a+b+w\in S_{k})\,\land\,(a+c+w\in S_{k})\} 
 
 (180) 
 
 
 
 … \displaystyle\ldots 
 
 
 
 W s ​ s ​ s \displaystyle W_{sss} 
 : = { w ∈ W | ( a + w ∈ S k ) ∧ ( a + b + w ∈ S k ) ∧ ( a + c + w ∈ S k ) } \displaystyle:=\{w\in W\,|\,(a+w\in S_{k})\,\land\,(a+b+w\in S_{k})\,\land\,(a+c+w\in S_{k})\} 
 
 (181) 
 
 so that  W = W r ​ r ​ r ⊔ W r ​ r ​ s ⊔ … ⊔ W s ​ s ​ s W=W_{rrr}\sqcup W_{rrs}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup W_{sss} . Consider the following disjoint unions of these sets 
 

 
 
 W x ​ x ​ y \displaystyle W_{xxy} 
 : = W r ​ r ​ r ⊔ W r ​ r ​ s ⊔ W s ​ s ​ r ⊔ W s ​ s ​ s \displaystyle:=W_{rrr}\sqcup W_{rrs}\sqcup W_{ssr}\sqcup W_{sss} 
 
 (182) 
 
 
 W x ​ x ¯ ​ y \displaystyle W_{x\overline{x}y} 
 : = W r ​ s ​ r ⊔ W r ​ s ​ s ⊔ W s ​ r ​ r ⊔ W s ​ r ​ s \displaystyle:=W_{rsr}\sqcup W_{rss}\sqcup W_{srr}\sqcup W_{srs} 
 
 (183) 
 
 
 W x ​ y ​ y \displaystyle W_{xyy} 
 : = W r ​ r ​ r ⊔ W r ​ s ​ s ⊔ W s ​ r ​ r ⊔ W s ​ s ​ s \displaystyle:=W_{rrr}\sqcup W_{rss}\sqcup W_{srr}\sqcup W_{sss} 
 
 (184) 
 
 
 W x ​ y ​ y ¯ \displaystyle W_{xy\overline{y}} 
 : = W r ​ r ​ s ⊔ W r ​ s ​ r ⊔ W s ​ r ​ s ⊔ W s ​ s ​ r . \displaystyle:=W_{rrs}\sqcup W_{rsr}\sqcup W_{srs}\sqcup W_{ssr}. 
 
 (185) 
 
 The indicator function of  W x ​ x ¯ ​ y W_{x\overline{x}y}  restricted to  W W  is 
 

 
 
 𝟙 W x ​ x ¯ ​ y ​ ( x ) \displaystyle\mathbb{1}_{W_{x\overline{x}y}}(x) 
 = 𝟙 R k ​ ( a + x ) ⋅ 𝟙 S k ​ ( a + b + x ) ⋅ 𝟙 R k ​ ( a + c + x ) + 𝟙 R k ​ ( a + x ) ⋅ 𝟙 S k ​ ( a + b + x ) ⋅ 𝟙 S k ​ ( a + c + x ) \displaystyle=\mathbb{1}_{R_{k}}(a+x)\cdot\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+b+x)\cdot\mathbb{1}_{R_{k}}(a+c+x)+\mathbb{1}_{R_{k}}(a+x)\cdot\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+b+x)\cdot\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+c+x) 
 
 (186) 
 
 
 
 + 𝟙 S k ( a + x ) ⋅ 𝟙 R k ( a + b + x ) ⋅ 𝟙 R k ( a + c + x ) + 𝟙 S k ( a + x ) ⋅ 𝟙 R k ( a + b + x ) ⋅ 𝟙 S k ( a + c + x ) \displaystyle+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+x)\cdot\mathbb{1}_{R_{k}}(a+b+x)\cdot\mathbb{1}_{R_{k}}(a+c+x)+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+x)\cdot\mathbb{1}_{R_{k}}(a+b+x)\cdot\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+c+x) 
 
 (187) 
 
 
 
 = 𝟙 R k ​ ( a + x ) ⋅ 𝟙 S k ​ ( a + b + x ) + 𝟙 S k ​ ( a + x ) ⋅ 𝟙 R k ​ ( a + b + x ) \displaystyle=\mathbb{1}_{R_{k}}(a+x)\cdot\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+b+x)+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+x)\cdot\mathbb{1}_{R_{k}}(a+b+x) 
 
 (188) 
 
 
 
 = ( 1 + 𝟙 S k ​ ( a + x ) ) ⋅ 𝟙 S k ​ ( a + b + x ) + 𝟙 S k ​ ( a + x ) ⋅ ( 1 + 𝟙 S k ​ ( a + b + x ) ) \displaystyle=\left(1+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+x)\right)\cdot\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+b+x)+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+x)\cdot\left(1+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+b+x)\right) 
 
 (189) 
 
 
 
 = 𝟙 S k ​ ( a + x ) + 𝟙 S k ​ ( a + b + x ) \displaystyle=\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+x)+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(a+b+x) 
 
 (190) 
 
 
 
 = 𝟙 S k ​ ( b ) + ω ⁡ ( a , b ) + ω ⁡ ( b , x ) \displaystyle=\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(b)+\omega(a,b)+\omega(b,x) 
 
 (191) 
 
 which is an affine functional on  W W . Therefore,  W x ​ x ¯ ​ y W_{x\overline{x}y}  is empty or an affine subspace of  W W . So is  W x ​ x ​ y W_{xxy}  whose indicator function restricted to  W W  is  𝟙 W x ​ x ​ y ​ ( x ) = 𝟙 W x ​ x ¯ ​ y ​ ( x ) + 1 \mathbb{1}_{W_{xxy}}(x)=\mathbb{1}_{W_{x\overline{x}y}}(x)+1 . Moreover, ( 182 ) and ( 183 ) imply that  W x ​ x ​ y ⊔ W x ​ x ¯ ​ y = W W_{xxy}\sqcup W_{x\overline{x}y}=W . 
 
 
 Similarly, the indicator function of  W x ​ y ​ y ¯ W_{xy\overline{y}}  restricted to  W W  is  𝟙 W x ​ y ​ y ¯ ​ ( x ) = 𝟙 S k ​ ( b ) + 𝟙 S k ​ ( c ) + ω ⁡ ( a , b + c ) + ω ⁡ ( b + c , x ) \mathbb{1}_{W_{xy\overline{y}}}(x)=\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(b)+\mathbb{1}_{S_{k}}(c)+\omega(a,b+c)+\omega(b+c,x)  which is also an affine functional on  W W . Therefore,  W x ​ y ​ y ¯ W_{xy\overline{y}}  is empty or an affine subspace of  W W . So is  W x ​ y ​ y W_{xyy}  whose indicator function restricted to  W W  is  𝟙 W x ​ y ​ y ​ ( x ) = 𝟙 W x ​ y ​ y ¯ ​ ( x ) + 1 \mathbb{1}_{W_{xyy}}(x)=\mathbb{1}_{W_{xy\overline{y}}}(x)+1 . Moreover, ( 184 ) and ( 185 ) imply that  W x ​ y ​ y ⊔ W x ​ y ​ y ¯ = W W_{xyy}\sqcup W_{xy\overline{y}}=W . 
 
 
 We can write 
 

 
 
 | A 00 ∩ S k | = | W s ​ r ​ r | + | W s ​ r ​ s | + | W s ​ s ​ r | + | W s ​ s ​ s | \displaystyle|A_{00}\cap S_{k}|=|W_{srr}|+|W_{srs}|+|W_{ssr}|+|W_{sss}| 
 
 (192) 
 
 
 | A 01 ∩ S k | = | W r ​ s ​ r | + | W r ​ s ​ s | + | W s ​ s ​ r | + | W s ​ s ​ s | \displaystyle|A_{01}\cap S_{k}|=|W_{rsr}|+|W_{rss}|+|W_{ssr}|+|W_{sss}| 
 
 (193) 
 
 
 | A 10 ∩ S k | = | W r ​ r ​ s | + | W r ​ s ​ s | + | W s ​ r ​ s | + | W s ​ s ​ s | \displaystyle|A_{10}\cap S_{k}|=|W_{rrs}|+|W_{rss}|+|W_{srs}|+|W_{sss}| 
 
 (194) 
 
 irrespective of  ω ⁡ ( b , c ) \omega(b,c) , so that 
 

 
 
 
 | A 00 ∩ S k | + | A 01 ∩ S k | + | A 10 ∩ S k | \displaystyle|A_{00}\cap S_{k}|+|A_{01}\cap S_{k}|+|A_{10}\cap S_{k}| 
 
 (195) 
 
 
 
 = | W r ​ r ​ s | + | W r ​ s ​ r | + 2 ⋅ | W r ​ s ​ s | + | W s ​ r ​ r | + 2 ⋅ | W s ​ r ​ s | + 2 ⋅ | W s ​ s ​ r | + 3 ⋅ | W s ​ s ​ s | \displaystyle=|W_{rrs}|+|W_{rsr}|+2\cdot|W_{rss}|+|W_{srr}|+2\cdot|W_{srs}|+2\cdot|W_{ssr}|+3\cdot|W_{sss}| 
 
 
 also irrespective of  ω ⁡ ( b , c ) \omega(b,c) . The analogous expression for  | A 11 ∩ R k | |A_{11}\cap R_{k}|  depends on the value of  ω ⁡ ( b , c ) \omega(b,c) . We consider both cases constructing a pair of suitable affine spaces  B 1 B_{1}  and  B 2 B_{2}  for each one in turn. 
 
 
 First, if  ω ⁡ ( b , c ) = 0 \omega(b,c)=0 , then 
 

 
 
 | A 11 ∩ R k | = | W r ​ r ​ r | + | W r ​ s ​ s | + | W s ​ r ​ s | + | W s ​ s ​ r | \displaystyle|A_{11}\cap R_{k}|=|W_{rrr}|+|W_{rss}|+|W_{srs}|+|W_{ssr}| 
 
 (196) 
 
 and adding ( 195 ) yields 
 

 
 
 
 | A 00 ∩ S k | + | A 01 ∩ S k | + | A 10 ∩ S k | + | A 11 ∩ R k | \displaystyle|A_{00}\cap S_{k}|+|A_{01}\cap S_{k}|+|A_{10}\cap S_{k}|+|A_{11}\cap R_{k}| 
 
 (197) 
 
 
 
 = | W r ​ r ​ r | + | W r ​ r ​ s | + | W r ​ s ​ r | + 3 ⋅ | W r ​ s ​ s | + | W s ​ r ​ r | + 3 ⋅ | W s ​ r ​ s | + 3 ⋅ | W s ​ s ​ r | + 3 ⋅ | W s ​ s ​ s | \displaystyle=|W_{rrr}|+|W_{rrs}|+|W_{rsr}|+3\cdot|W_{rss}|+|W_{srr}|+3\cdot|W_{srs}|+3\cdot|W_{ssr}|+3\cdot|W_{sss}| 
 
 (198) 
 
 
 
 = | W | + 2 ⋅ ( | W r ​ s ​ s | + | W s ​ r ​ s | + | W s ​ s ​ r | + | W s ​ s ​ s | ) . \displaystyle=|W|+2\cdot\left(|W_{rss}|+|W_{srs}|+|W_{ssr}|+|W_{sss}|\right). 
 
 (199) 
 
 Define 
 

 
 
 B 1 \displaystyle B_{1} 
 : = ( a + b + W x ​ x ¯ ​ y ) ⊔ ( a + c + W x ​ x ​ y ) \displaystyle:=(a+b+W_{x\overline{x}y})\sqcup(a+c+W_{xxy}) 
 
 (200) 
 
 
 B 2 \displaystyle B_{2} 
 : = ( a + b + W x ​ x ​ y ) ⊔ ( a + c + W x ​ x ¯ ​ y ) \displaystyle:=(a+b+W_{xxy})\sqcup(a+c+W_{x\overline{x}y}) 
 
 (201) 
 
 so that  B 1 ⊔ B 2 B_{1}\sqcup B_{2}  is an affine space of dimension  dim W + 1 \dim W+1  and 
 

 
 
 | B 1 | \displaystyle|B_{1}| 
 = | W | \displaystyle=|W| 
 
 (202) 
 
 
 | B 2 ∩ S k | \displaystyle|B_{2}\cap S_{k}| 
 = | W r ​ s ​ s | + | W s ​ r ​ s | + | W s ​ s ​ r | + | W s ​ s ​ s | \displaystyle=|W_{rss}|+|W_{srs}|+|W_{ssr}|+|W_{sss}| 
 
 (203) 
 
 and hence 
 

 
 
 | A 00 ∩ S k | + | A 01 ∩ S k | + | A 10 ∩ S k | + | A 11 ∩ R k | = | B 1 | + 2 ⋅ | B 2 ∩ S k | . \displaystyle|A_{00}\cap S_{k}|+|A_{01}\cap S_{k}|+|A_{10}\cap S_{k}|+|A_{11}\cap R_{k}|=|B_{1}|+2\cdot|B_{2}\cap S_{k}|. 
 
 (204) 
 
 
 
 Now, if  ω ⁡ ( b , c ) = 1 \omega(b,c)=1 , then 
 

 
 
 | A 11 ∩ R k | = | W r ​ r ​ s | + | W r ​ s ​ r | + | W s ​ r ​ r | + | W s ​ s ​ s | \displaystyle|A_{11}\cap R_{k}|=|W_{rrs}|+|W_{rsr}|+|W_{srr}|+|W_{sss}| 
 
 (205) 
 
 and adding ( 195 ) yields 
 

 
 
 
 | A 00 ∩ S k | + | A 01 ∩ S k | + | A 10 ∩ S k | + | A 11 ∩ R k | \displaystyle|A_{00}\cap S_{k}|+|A_{01}\cap S_{k}|+|A_{10}\cap S_{k}|+|A_{11}\cap R_{k}| 
 
 (206) 
 
 
 
 = 2 ⋅ | W r ​ r ​ s | + 2 ⋅ | W r ​ s ​ r | + 2 ⋅ | W r ​ s ​ s | + 2 ⋅ | W s ​ r ​ r | + 2 ⋅ | W s ​ r ​ s | + 2 ⋅ | W s ​ s ​ r | + 4 ⋅ | W s ​ s ​ s | . \displaystyle=2\cdot|W_{rrs}|+2\cdot|W_{rsr}|+2\cdot|W_{rss}|+2\cdot|W_{srr}|+2\cdot|W_{srs}|+2\cdot|W_{ssr}|+4\cdot|W_{sss}|. 
 
 
 Redefine 
 

 
 
 B 1 \displaystyle B_{1} 
 : = ( a + W x ​ y ​ y ¯ ) ⊔ ( a + b + W x ​ y ​ y ¯ ) \displaystyle:=(a+W_{xy\overline{y}})\sqcup(a+b+W_{xy\overline{y}}) 
 
 (207) 
 
 
 B 2 \displaystyle B_{2} 
 : = ( a + W x ​ y ​ y ) ⊔ ( a + b + W x ​ y ​ y ) \displaystyle:=(a+W_{xyy})\sqcup(a+b+W_{xyy}) 
 
 (208) 
 
 so that  B 1 ⊔ B 2 B_{1}\sqcup B_{2}  is again an affine space of dimension  dim W + 1 \dim W+1  and 
 

 
 
 | B 1 | \displaystyle|B_{1}| 
 = 2 ⋅ | W r ​ r ​ s | + 2 ⋅ | W r ​ s ​ r | + 2 ⋅ | W s ​ r ​ s | + 2 ⋅ | W s ​ s ​ r | \displaystyle=2\cdot|W_{rrs}|+2\cdot|W_{rsr}|+2\cdot|W_{srs}|+2\cdot|W_{ssr}| 
 
 (209) 
 
 
 | B 2 ∩ S k | \displaystyle|B_{2}\cap S_{k}| 
 = | W r ​ s ​ s | + | W s ​ r ​ r | + 2 ⋅ | W s ​ s ​ s | \displaystyle=|W_{rss}|+|W_{srr}|+2\cdot|W_{sss}| 
 
 (210) 
 
 and hence 
 

 
 
 | A 00 ∩ S k | + | A 01 ∩ S k | + | A 10 ∩ S k | + | A 11 ∩ R k | = | B 1 | + 2 ⋅ | B 2 ∩ S k | \displaystyle|A_{00}\cap S_{k}|+|A_{01}\cap S_{k}|+|A_{10}\cap S_{k}|+|A_{11}\cap R_{k}|=|B_{1}|+2\cdot|B_{2}\cap S_{k}| 
 
 (211) 
 
 completing the proof of the Lemma.
∎ 
 
 
 
 We note the following conditional upper bound which is implied by Lemma  E.4  and which will facilitate its use in an inductive proof. 
 
 
 Corollary E.2   (Bound for mixed recursive structure formulas with four terms) . 
 
 
 Let  e e  and  k > 1 k>1  be positive integers. If  | B ∩ S k − 1 | ≤ 2 dim B − 1 + 2 k − 3 |B\cap S_{k-1}|\leq 2^{\dim B-1}+2^{k-3}  for every affine subspace  B B  of  𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2}  with  dim B ∈ { e , e + 1 } \dim B\in\{e,e+1\} , then 
 

 
 
 | A 00 ∩ S k − 1 | + | A 01 ∩ S k − 1 | + | A 10 ∩ S k − 1 | + | A 11 ∩ R k − 1 | ≤ 2 e + 1 + 2 k − 2 \displaystyle|A_{00}\cap S_{k-1}|+|A_{01}\cap S_{k-1}|+|A_{10}\cap S_{k-1}|+|A_{11}\cap R_{k-1}|\leq 2^{e+1}+2^{k-2} 
 
 (212) 
 
 for any four disjoint affine subspaces of the form 
 

 
 
 A 00 \displaystyle A_{00} 
 = a + W \displaystyle=a+W 
 
 (213) 
 
 
 A 01 \displaystyle A_{01} 
 = a + b + W \displaystyle=a+b+W 
 
 (214) 
 
 
 A 10 \displaystyle A_{10} 
 = a + c + W \displaystyle=a+c+W 
 
 (215) 
 
 
 A 11 \displaystyle A_{11} 
 = a + b + c + W \displaystyle=a+b+c+W 
 
 (216) 
 
 arising as translations of the same  e e -dimensional linear subspace  W ⊂ 𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 W\subset\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2}  with  a , b , c ∈ 𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 a,b,c\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 By Lemma  E.4 , there exist two sets  B 1 B_{1}  and  B 2 B_{2}  each of which is either empty or affine and such that 
 

 
 
 | A 00 ∩ S k − 1 | + | A 01 ∩ S k − 1 | + | A 10 ∩ S k − 1 | + | A 11 ∩ R k − 1 | = | B 1 | + 2 ⋅ | B 2 ∩ S k − 1 | \displaystyle|A_{00}\cap S_{k-1}|+|A_{01}\cap S_{k-1}|+|A_{10}\cap S_{k-1}|+|A_{11}\cap R_{k-1}|=|B_{1}|+2\cdot|B_{2}\cap S_{k-1}| 
 
 (217) 
 
 and such that  | B 1 | + | B 2 | = 2 ⋅ | W | |B_{1}|+|B_{2}|=2\cdot|W| . Thus, if  B 1 = ∅ B_{1}=\emptyset , then  B 2 B_{2}  is an affine subspace of  𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2}  of dimension  e + 1 e+1  and using the assumption in the Corollary, we obtain 
 

 
 
 | A 00 ∩ S k − 1 | + | A 01 ∩ S k − 1 | + | A 10 ∩ S k − 1 | + | A 11 ∩ R k − 1 | ≤ 0 + 2 ⋅ ( 2 e + 2 k − 3 ) = 2 e + 1 + 2 k − 2 . \displaystyle|A_{00}\cap S_{k-1}|+|A_{01}\cap S_{k-1}|+|A_{10}\cap S_{k-1}|+|A_{11}\cap R_{k-1}|\leq 0+2\cdot(2^{e}+2^{k-3})=2^{e+1}+2^{k-2}. 
 
 (218) 
 
 If  B 2 = ∅ B_{2}=\emptyset , then  B 1 B_{1}  is an affine subspace of  𝔽 2 2 ​ k − 2 \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k-2}  of dimension  e + 1 e+1 . Therefore, 
 

 
 
 | A 00 ∩ S k − 1 | + | A 01 ∩ S k − 1 | + | A 10 ∩ S k − 1 | + | A 11 ∩ R k − 1 | ≤ 2 e + 1 + 2 ⋅ 0 < 2 e + 1 + 2 k − 2 . \displaystyle|A_{00}\cap S_{k-1}|+|A_{01}\cap S_{k-1}|+|A_{10}\cap S_{k-1}|+|A_{11}\cap R_{k-1}|\leq 2^{e+1}+2\cdot 0<2^{e+1}+2^{k-2}. 
 
 (219) 
 
 Finally, if neither  B 1 B_{1}  nor  B 2 B_{2}  is empty, then both are affine and  dim B 1 = dim B 2 = e \dim B_{1}=\dim B_{2}=e . Therefore, 
 

 
 
 | A 00 ∩ S k − 1 | + | A 01 ∩ S k − 1 | + | A 10 ∩ S k − 1 | + | A 11 ∩ R k − 1 | ≤ 2 e + 2 ⋅ ( 2 e − 1 + 2 k − 3 ) = 2 e + 1 + 2 k − 2 \displaystyle|A_{00}\cap S_{k-1}|+|A_{01}\cap S_{k-1}|+|A_{10}\cap S_{k-1}|+|A_{11}\cap R_{k-1}|\leq 2^{e}+2\cdot(2^{e-1}+2^{k-3})=2^{e+1}+2^{k-2} 
 
 (220) 
 
 completing the proof of the Corollary.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 Appendix F  Reference Python Implementation 

 
 F.1  Reed Solomon Decoding using Synchronized EEA for Explicit Bézout coefficients 

 
 Fig.   9  gives our optimized implementation of Zalka’s synchronized reversible EEA  [ 12 ] .
 Fig.   10  uses this as a subroutine and shows how to implement the Chien Search  [ 23 ]  and Forney’s algorithm  [ 24 ]  steps for syndrome decoding of Reed Solomon codes, given the shared in-place register representation of the error locator polynomial  σ ⁡ ( z ) \sigma(z)  and error evaluator polynomial  Ω ⁡ ( z ) \Omega(z) . 
 
 
 Figure 9:  Our optimized implementation of Zalka’s EEA for explicit access to Bézout coefficients. 
 ⬇ 
 import   numpy   as   np 
 
 from   galois   import   Poly 
 
 
 
 
 
 def   poly_eea_zalka_gcd_impl ( A :   Poly ,   B :   Poly ,   stop ,   n_steps :   float   =   5.0)   ->   tuple [ np . ndarray ,   int ,   int ]: 
 
      gf ,   m   =   A . field ,   B . degree   +   3 
 
      a_array ,   b_array   =   gf . Zeros ( m ),   gf . Zeros ( m ) 
 
      a_array [0],   a_array [ m   -   A . degree   -   1   :],   b_array [ m   -   B . degree   -   1   :]   =   1,   A . coeffs [::-1],   B . coeffs [::-1] 
 
      n_A ,   n_B ,   n_a ,   n_b ,   n_q   =   A . degree   +   1,   B . degree   +   1,   1,   0,   0 
 
      flag ,   counter ,   halting_counter   =   1,   0,   0 
 
 
 
      def   swap_a_and_b (): 
 
          nonlocal   n_A ,   n_B ,   flag ,   n_a ,   n_b ,   a_array ,   b_array ,   halting_counter ,   counter 
 
          if   counter   ==   3   and   flag   and   halting_counter   ==   0: 
 
              n_A ,   n_B   =   n_B ,   n_A 
 
              n_a ,   n_b   =   n_b ,   n_a 
 
              a_array ,   b_array   =   b_array ,   a_array 
 
 
 
      def   update_flag (): 
 
          nonlocal   flag 
 
          if   counter   ==   0   and   halting_counter   ==   0: 
 
              flag   ^=   ( n_q   ==   0)   and   ( n_A   <   n_B ) 
 
              flag   ^=   n_A   ==   n_B 
 
          elif   counter   ==   1   and   halting_counter   ==   0: 
 
              flag   ^=   n_A   ==   n_B 
 
              flag   ^=   n_B   ==   1   or   b_array [-2]   !=   0 
 
          elif   counter   ==   2   and   halting_counter   ==   0: 
 
              flag   ^=   b_array [0]   !=   0   or   n_b   ==   0 
 
              flag   ^=   n_a   ==   n_b   +   1 
 
          elif   counter   ==   3   and   halting_counter   ==   0: 
 
              flag   ^=   n_a   ==   n_b 
 
              flag   ^=   n_q   ==   1 
 
          else : 
 
              assert   False 
 
 
 
      def   multiply_and_add_or_sub (): 
 
          nonlocal   b_array 
 
          q_i   =   0 
 
          if   counter   ==   0   and   halting_counter   ==   0: 
 
              q_i   ^=   ( a_array [-1]   **   -1)   *   b_array [-1] 
 
 
 
          for   i   in   range ( m   -   1,   -1,   -1): 
 
              swap_if   =   i   ==   n_a   +   n_q   and   counter   ==   0   and   halting_counter   ==   0 
 
              swap_if   |=   ( i   +   1)   ==   n_a   +   n_q   and   counter   ==   3   and   halting_counter   ==   0 
 
              if   swap_if : 
 
                  q_i ,   a_array [ i ]   =   a_array [ i ],   q_i 
 
 
 
              q_i_times_a_i   =   q_i   *   a_array [ i ] 
 
              add_if   =   ( counter   ==   3)   and   i   <   n_a 
 
              sub_if   =   ( counter   ==   0)   and   i   >=   m   -   n_A 
 
              if   add_if : 
 
                  b_array [ i ]   +=   q_i_times_a_i 
 
              if   sub_if : 
 
                  b_array [ i ]   -=   q_i_times_a_i 
 
 
 
          if   counter   ==   3   and   halting_counter   ==   0: 
 
              q_i   ^=   b_array [0]   *   ( a_array [0]   **   -1) 
 
          assert   q_i   ==   0 
 
 
 
      def   shift_b_array_right (): 
 
          nonlocal   b_array 
 
          for   i   in   range ( m   -   1,   -1,   -1): 
 
              do_shift   =   False 
 
              if   counter   ==   0: 
 
                  do_shift   =   ( i   >=   m   -   n_B   +   1)   and   not   flag 
 
              if   counter   ==   1: 
 
                  do_shift   =   i   >=   m   -   n_B   +   1 
 
              if   counter   ==   2: 
 
                  do_shift   =   0   <   i   <=   n_b 
 
              if   counter   ==   3: 
 
                  do_shift   =   (0   <   i   <   m   -   n_B )   and   not   flag 
 
              if   do_shift   and   halting_counter   ==   0: 
 
                  b_array [ i   -   1],   b_array [ i ]   =   b_array [ i ],   b_array [ i   -   1] 
 
 
 
      def   update_metadata (): 
 
          nonlocal   n_A ,   n_B ,   n_q ,   flag ,   n_a ,   n_b 
 
          if   counter   ==   0   and   halting_counter   ==   0: 
 
              n_q   +=   1 
 
              if   not   flag : 
 
                  n_B   -=   1 
 
          if   counter   ==   1   and   halting_counter   ==   0: 
 
              n_B   -=   1 
 
          if   counter   ==   2   and   halting_counter   ==   0: 
 
              n_b   +=   1 
 
          if   counter   ==   3   and   halting_counter   ==   0: 
 
              if   not   flag : 
 
                  n_b   +=   1 
 
              n_q   -=   1 
 
 
 
      def   check_stop (): 
 
          nonlocal   halting_counter 
 
          if   counter   ==   3   and   flag   and   stop ( n_A ,   n_B ,   n_a ,   n_b ): 
 
              halting_counter   +=   1 
 
 
 
      for   it   in   range ( int ( np . ceil ( n_steps   *   m ))): 
 
          update_flag () 
 
          multiply_and_add_or_sub () 
 
          shift_b_array_right () 
 
          update_metadata () 
 
          swap_a_and_b () 
 
          check_stop () 
 
          #   Advance   counter. 
 
          counter   =   ( counter   +   flag )   %   4 
 
      return   b_array ,   n_b ,   n_B 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 10:  RS Decoding using our optimized implementation of Zalka’s EEA  [ 12 ,  16 ]  presented in  Fig.   9 
 ⬇ 
 import   numpy   as   np 
 
 from   galois   import   Poly 
 
 from   poly_eea_zalka_gcd   import   poly_eea_zalka_gcd_impl 
 
 
 
 
 
 def   rs_syndrome_decoder_eea_zalka ( s :   np . ndarray )   ->   np . ndarray : 
 
      gf ,   n   =   type ( s [0]),   len ( s ) 
 
 
 
      def   _stop_rs ( n_A :   int ,   n_B :   int ,   _ :   int ,   __ :   int ): 
 
          return   n_A   ==   0   or   n_B   <   n   //   2 
 
 
 
      A   =   Poly ( s [::-1],   field = gf ) 
 
      B   =   Poly . Degrees ( coeffs =[1],   degrees =[ n ],   field = gf ) 
 
      b_array ,   n_b ,   n_B   =   poly_eea_zalka_gcd_impl ( A ,   B ,   stop = _stop_rs ,   n_steps =3.75) 
 
      assert   n_B   <=   n   //   2   and   n_b   <=   n   //   2 
 
 
 
      def   poly_eval ( c ): 
 
          omega_x_val ,   sigma_x_val ,   sigma_x_prime_val   =   gf (0),   gf (0),   gf (0) 
 
          m   =   len ( b_array ) 
 
          for   i   in   range ( m   -   1,   m   //   2,   -1): 
 
              c_i   =   c   **   ( i ) 
 
              omega_x_val   +=   b_array [ i ]   *   c_i 
 
          for   i   in   range ( m   //   2,   -1,   -1): 
 
              c_i   =   c   **   (- i ) 
 
              sigma_x_val   +=   b_array [ i ]   *   c_i 
 
              if   i   &   1: 
 
                  sigma_x_prime_val   +=   b_array [ i ]   *   c_i 
 
          return   omega_x_val ,   sigma_x_val ,   sigma_x_prime_val 
 
 
 
      block_length   =   gf . order   -   1 
 
      gammas   =   gf ([ gf . primitive_element ** i   for   i   in   range ( block_length )]) 
 
      errors   =   gf . Zeros ( block_length ) 
 
      for   i   in   range ( block_length ): 
 
          omega_x_eval ,   sigma_x_eval ,   sigma_x_prime_eval   =   poly_eval ( gammas [ i ]) 
 
          if   sigma_x_eval   ==   0: 
 
              j   =   block_length   -   i   if   i   else   i 
 
              c   =   gammas [ i ] 
 
              const_to_mul   =   c   **   -( len ( b_array )   -   1   +   n_b   -   n_B   -   1) 
 
              errors [ j ]   =   const_to_mul   *   omega_x_eval   /   sigma_x_prime_eval 
 
      return   errors 
 
 
 
 
 
 F.2  Reed Solomon Decoding using Dialog based EEA for Implicit Bézout Coefficients 

 
 Fig.   11  gives our optimized implementation of constructing a Dialog based on the Bernstein-Yang GCD algorithm  [ 17 ]  using a shared register representation to save space. We also show how once can consume the Dialog to perform modular multiplication or modular division.
 Fig.   12  uses uses the Dialog based EEA for syndrome decoding of Reed Solomon codes, and shows how one can evaluate the error locator polynomial  σ ⁡ ( z ) \sigma(z)  and it’s derivative  σ ′ ​ ( z ) \sigma^{\prime}(z) , given access to the Dialog of  σ ⁡ ( z ) \sigma(z) . 
 
 
 Figure 11:  In Place Dialog construction using register sharing and it’s use to perform modular division and modular multiplication. 
 ⬇ 
 from   galois   import   Poly ,   GF ,   gcd 
 
 
 
 
 
 def   _get_delta ( delta :   int ,   text :   list [ GF ]): 
 
      for   c   in   text : 
 
          is_swap   =   delta   >   0   and   c   !=   0 
 
          if   is_swap : 
 
              delta   =   - delta 
 
          delta   =   1   +   delta 
 
      return   delta 
 
 
 
 
 
 def   multiply_poly_with_divstep_dialog ( 
 
      a :   Poly ,   dialog :   list [ GF ],   delta_init :   int ,   mod :   Poly   =   None 
 
 )   ->   tuple [ Poly ,   Poly ]: 
 
      f ,   g ,   x   =   a ,   Poly . Zero ( a . field ),   Poly . Identity ( a . field ) 
 
      for   i ,   c   in   [* enumerate ( dialog )][::-1]: 
 
          is_swap   =   _get_delta ( delta_init ,   dialog [: i ])   >   0   and   c   !=   0 
 
          g   =   ( g   *   x )   %   mod   if   mod   else   g   *   x 
 
          if   c   !=   0: 
 
              g   =   ( g   +   c   *   f )   %   mod   if   mod   else   g   +   c   *   f 
 
          if   is_swap : 
 
              f ,   g   =   g ,   f 
 
      return   f ,   g 
 
 
 
 
 
 def   mod_divide_poly_with_divstep_dialog ( 
 
      a :   Poly ,   dialog :   list [ GF ],   delta_init :   int ,   mod :   Poly 
 
 )   ->   tuple [ Poly ,   Poly ]: 
 
      f ,   g ,   x   =   Poly . Zero ( a . field ),   a ,   Poly . Identity ( a . field ) 
 
      gf   =   GF ( a . field . characteristic ,   len ( dialog )   //   2   -   1,   irreducible_poly = mod ) 
 
      x_inv   =   ( x   **   ( gf . order   -   2))   %   mod 
 
      assert   ( x   *   x_inv )   %   mod   ==   Poly . One ( a . field ) 
 
      for   i ,   c   in   enumerate ( dialog ): 
 
          is_swap   =   _get_delta ( delta_init ,   dialog [: i ])   >   0   and   c   !=   0 
 
          if   is_swap : 
 
              f ,   g   =   g ,   f 
 
          if   c   !=   0: 
 
              g   =   ( g   -   c   *   f )   %   mod 
 
          g   =   ( g   *   x_inv )   %   mod 
 
      return   f ,   g 
 
 
 
 
 
 def   construct_dialog_poly_divstep_inplace ( f :   Poly ,   g :   Poly )   ->   tuple [ Poly ,   list [ GF ]]: 
 
      gf ,   n ,   x   =   f . field ,   1   +   max ( f . degree ,   g . degree ),   Poly . Identity ( f . field ) 
 
      #   In   general,   we   need   upto   3n   space   to   store   upto   n   coefficients   of   the   gcd. 
 
      #   If   we   know   that   gcd(f,   g)   =   1,   the   length   of   poly   will   reduce   to   2n   +   3. 
 
      _gcd_degree   =   gcd ( f ,   g ). degree 
 
      poly   =   [ gf (0)   for   _   in   range (2   *   n   +   3   +   _gcd_degree )] 
 
      poly [:   f . degree   +   1]   =   f . coefficients ( order = "asc" ) 
 
      poly [- g . degree   -   1   :]   =   g . coefficients ( order = "desc" ) 
 
      delta   =   f . degree   -   g . degree 
 
      dialog   =   [] 
 
      assert   poly [0]   !=   0 
 
      for   _   in   range (2   *   n ): 
 
          is_swap   =   delta   >   0   and   poly [-1]   !=   0 
 
          if   is_swap : 
 
              delta ,   poly   =   - delta ,   poly [::-1] 
 
          coeff   =   poly [-1]   //   poly [0] 
 
          dialog . append ( coeff ) 
 
          delta   =   1   +   delta 
 
          #   Perform   g   =   (g   -   f   *   coeff)   //   x 
 
          for   j   in   range ( len ( poly )   //   2   -   1,   -1,   -1): 
 
              if   j   <   len ( poly )   //   2   +   delta   //   2: 
 
                  poly [- j   -   1]   -=   poly [ j ]   *   coeff 
 
          assert   poly . pop ()   ==   0 
 
      return   Poly ( poly [:   1   +   ( delta   -   1)   //   2][::-1],   field = f . field ),   dialog 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 12:  Optimized implementation of EEA based syndrome decoder for RS codes using register sharing to efficiently compute the Dialog representation and use it to implicitly evaluate Bézout coefficient corresponding to polynomial  σ ⁡ ( z ) \sigma(z)  and  σ ′ ​ ( z ) \sigma^{\prime}(z) .

 import   numpy   as   np 
 
 
 
 
 
 def   construct_dialog ( s :   np . ndarray ,   n_iters :   int )   ->   tuple [ np . ndarray ,   list ,   int ]: 
 
      gf ,   n   =   type ( s [0]),   len ( s ) 
 
      poly   =   [ gf (0)   for   _   in   range (2   *   n   +   3)] 
 
      poly [0],   poly [- n :]   =   gf (1),   s 
 
      delta   =   0 
 
      dialog   =   [] 
 
      for   _   in   range ( n_iters ): 
 
          #   1.   Compute   the   dialog 
 
          is_swap   =   delta   >=   0   and   poly [-1]   !=   0 
 
          if   is_swap : 
 
              delta ,   poly   =   ~ delta ,   poly [::-1] 
 
          delta   =   1   +   delta 
 
          coeff   =   poly [-1]   //   poly [0] 
 
          dialog . append (( coeff ,   is_swap )) 
 
          #   2.   Perform   omega_x   -=   coeff   *   u_x   in   shared   register   representation. 
 
          for   j   in   range ( len ( poly )   //   2   -   1,   -1,   -1): 
 
              if   j   <   len ( poly )   //   2   +   delta   //   2: 
 
                  poly [- j   -   1]   -=   poly [ j ]   *   coeff 
 
          assert   poly . pop ()   ==   0 
 
 
 
      assert   len ( poly )   ==   2   *   n   +   3   -   n_iters 
 
      return   poly ,   dialog ,   delta 
 
 
 
 
 
 def   rs_syndrome_decoder_eea_dialog_inplace ( s :   np . ndarray )   ->   np . ndarray : 
 
      gf ,   n   =   type ( s [0]),   len ( s ) 
 
      #   Compute   the   dialog   representation   using   Bernstein-Yang   style   GCD   iterations. 
 
      #   Shared   register   stores   (u_x,   omega_x)   such   that: 
 
      #   u_x_{0},   u_x_{1},   ...,   u_x_{n},   0,   0,   omega_{n   -   1},   omega_{n   -   2},   ...,   omega_0 
 
      poly ,   dialog ,   delta   =   construct_dialog ( s ,   n   -   1) 
 
 
 
      def   omega_eval ( c ): 
 
          #   Evaluate   omega   using   shared   register   representation.   n_omega   =   n//2   -   delta   //   2 
 
          res   =   gf (0) 
 
          cpow   =   1 
 
          for   j   in   range ( n   //   2): 
 
              if   j   <   n   //   2   -   delta   //   2: 
 
                  res   +=   poly [- j   -   1]   *   cpow 
 
              cpow   =   cpow   *   c 
 
          return   res 
 
 
 
      def   sigma_and_sigma_prime_eval ( c ): 
 
          #   Use   the   dialog   representation   to   evaluate   sigma   and   sigma_prime   directly. 
 
          eval_w_x ,   eval_sigma_x   =   gf (0),   gf (1) 
 
          eval_w_x_prime ,   eval_sigma_x_prime   =   gf (0),   gf (0) 
 
          for   coeff ,   is_swap   in   dialog : 
 
              if   is_swap : 
 
                  eval_w_x ,   eval_sigma_x   =   eval_sigma_x ,   eval_w_x 
 
                  eval_w_x_prime ,   eval_sigma_x_prime   =   eval_sigma_x_prime ,   eval_w_x_prime 
 
              eval_w_x_prime_times_coeff   =   eval_w_x_prime   *   coeff 
 
              eval_sigma_x_prime   -=   eval_w_x_prime_times_coeff 
 
              eval_sigma_x_prime   =   eval_sigma_x_prime   *   c 
 
              eval_sigma_x_prime   +=   eval_sigma_x 
 
              eval_w_x_times_coeff   =   eval_w_x   *   coeff 
 
              eval_sigma_x_prime   -=   eval_w_x_times_coeff 
 
              eval_sigma_x   -=   eval_w_x_times_coeff 
 
              eval_sigma_x   =   eval_sigma_x   *   c 
 
          ct   =   n   //   2   +   delta   //   2 
 
          eval_sigma_x   =   eval_sigma_x   //   c 
 
          eval_sigma_x_prime   -=   ct   *   eval_sigma_x 
 
          return   eval_sigma_x ,   eval_sigma_x_prime 
 
 
 
      block_length   =   gf . order   -   1 
 
      gammas   =   gf ([ gf . primitive_element ** i   for   i   in   range ( block_length )]) 
 
      errors   =   gf . Zeros ( block_length ) 
 
      for   i   in   range ( block_length ): 
 
          sigma_x_eval ,   sigma_x_prime_eval   =   sigma_and_sigma_prime_eval ( gammas [ i ]) 
 
          if   sigma_x_eval   ==   0: 
 
              j   =   block_length   -   i   if   i   else   i 
 
              const_to_mul   =   gammas [ j ]   *   ( gammas [ i ]   **   n ) 
 
              errors [ j ]   =   const_to_mul   *   omega_eval ( gammas [ j ])   *   sigma_x_prime_eval **-1 
 
      return   errors
